# ai-fleet: one place to see and steer every agent session

Status: design only, nothing built yet. Written 2026-11-04 from a planning conversation.
Section 0 lists every requirement I stated. Sections 1–7 and 5a are the design we discussed. Section 8 lists additions that came up while writing. Section 14 records the decisions and why.

---

## 0. Requirements

What I asked for, in my own terms. Each one is tagged so the design and decisions can refer to it.

| ID | Requirement | Where it's handled |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | One central place to see and steer up to 40 sessions of Copilot, Claude Code, agy and vibe | §3, §4 |
| R2 | A central LLM session that can reach into the other sessions, spawn new ones and run agent workflows | §6 |
| R3 | A TUI, not a web UI (an optional browser view beside it since ADR-66) | §5, §5f |
| R4 | Visual: a grid of tiles coloured green, yellow and red, with animation | §5 |
| R5 | Keyboard driven, with arrow keys | §5 |
| R6 | Mouse: click into session windows | §5a |
| R7 | Tiling inside the TUI, with splits, to use a large monitor | §5a |
| R8 | Live session windows in the grid, not only summaries | §5a |
| R9 | Fast and efficient | §5a, §7, ADR-3, ADR-5 |
| R10 | Solve the RAM problem of running many agents | §7 |
| R11 | The central session must not get overloaded by every agent's context, and must be easy to clear or manage | §6 |
| R12 | Very short dev feedback loop: keep it open and add or remove features live while we talk | §10 |
| R13 | As simple as possible to build | §9, ADR-1 |
| R14 | Better than agent-deck, which only helps me switch between sessions | §1 |
| R15 | Low maintenance: not yet another tool that keeps breaking and needs constant fixing | §16, ADR-10 |
| R16 | Every component has a test, but tests never block progress | §15 |
| R17 | Go fast: no gates that slow me down; logs capture issues instead | §9, §15 |
| R18 | Modular: features can be added or removed without touching the rest | §10, §15 |
| R19 | Ready to change direction at short notice | §9 |
| R20 | Works with every tool variant I use: copilot, claude-copilot, claude, claude-personal, claude-work, agy, vibe. Profiles live in a data file, not hard-coded | §4 |
| R21 | Copilot is the main admiral session for now | §6 |

---

## 1. The problem

I often run up to 40 agent sessions at once (GitHub Copilot CLI, Claude Code, agy, vibe), each in its own terminal. The terminals themselves are not the hard part. My attention is.

1. **I can't see the fleet's state.** I can't tell which agents are working, stuck, waiting on me or done without visiting every terminal.
2. **I'm the only router.** Every answer, approval and follow-up has to come from me, typed into the right window.
3. **Agents don't share context.** I carry the overall plan in my head and pass it between agents by hand.
4. **Starting work takes manual setup.** Splitting a task across agents means opening terminals, picking directories and pasting prompts myself.
5. **RAM runs out.** Measured on 2026-11-04 (30 GB machine): a Copilot session uses 400–540 MB and a Claude or agy session 250–280 MB. 19 sessions used about 4.6 GB, so 40 would use about 15–20 GB.
6. **A central agent would get flooded.** If one admiral session reads raw output from 40 agents, its context fills with unrelated material and its judgement gets worse.

What I want is one place to talk to, which can see every agent and act on them for me: report state, answer or nudge agents, start new ones and coordinate multi-step work. That is a **control plane with an LLM in it**, not just a session switcher.

### Is this a known pattern?

Yes. It is usually called the supervisor (or api-service) and worker pattern. Existing tools such as agent-deck, claude-squad, Vibe Kanban and Crystal are mostly session managers: they make switching between agents faster, but no LLM supervises the others. agent-deck felt wrong for exactly that reason. It improves navigation but leaves all the supervising to me.

---

## 2. Design principles

1. **tmux does the work underneath.** Every agent runs in a named tmux window. The tmux server survives if the TUI or the admiral crashes. No custom terminal handling.
2. **One CLI, two front ends.** The `fleet` CLI is the only thing that touches tmux and state. The TUI uses it, and so does the admiral LLM. Anything I can do, the admiral can do, and the reverse.
3. **State lives on disk, not in any chat.** If something isn't written to the board, it doesn't exist.
4. **The admiral is stateless and can be thrown away.** Resetting it costs seconds and loses nothing.
5. **Raw output never reaches the admiral.** Agent output is summarised by a cheap model first.
6. **Parked is the normal state, not live.** Idle agents give up their process and resume on demand.
7. **Visual first, keyboard and mouse driven.** Colour and motion tell me where to look. Arrow keys or a click get me there.
8. **Let native code do the heavy lifting.** tmux (written in C) and the terminal emulator draw the live sessions. The fleet binary only draws the overview, and only redraws when something changes.

---

## 3. Architecture

```
┌───────────────────────────── fleet TUI (Go) ──────────────────────────────────┐
│  grid of tiles  ·  attention queue  ·  admiral pane  ·  status bar          │
└───────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────┬────────────────┘
                │ calls                                         │ calls
                ▼                                               ▼
        ┌──────────────┐        reads/writes          ┌──────────────────┐
        │  fleet CLI   │ ───────────────────────────▶ │  ~/.fleet/ state │
        └──────┬───────┘                              └──────────────────┘
               │ tmux new-window / capture-pane / send-keys / kill
               ▼
   tmux session "fleet": window per agent ── copilot | claude | agy | vibe
                                                   ▲
   admiral = one more agent (copilot), uses fleet CLI via bash
```

### State layout (`~/.fleet/`)

| Path | What it holds |
|---|---|
| `board.json` | One entry per agent: name, tool, repo/dir, goal, status, blocker, session ID, live/parked, last activity, RAM |
| `agents/<name>.md` | Agent card: goal, what's done, current step, next step, open questions. 10–30 lines, rewritten (not appended). |
| `decisions.log` | Append-only, one line each: timestamp, who decided (me or the admiral), what, for which agent |
| `tmux.json` | The watcher's last `list-panes -a` with pane and wall window options, for the overview's poll; deleted when the watcher stops (ADR-98) |
| `teams.json` | Teams: colour and members (§5c); separate from `board.json` so older binaries can't drop it |
| `admiral/` | The admiral's `session.json`, `handoff.md`, `handoffs/` and `reset.lock` (§6.5). `supervisor/` is a symlink to it for binaries from before ADR-26 |
| `events.jsonl` | Status transitions (for the TUI animation, notifications and later analysis) |
| `peek-cache/<name>.txt` | Last summary of each agent, with the hash it was made from, when, and by which model (§6.2) |

### Resume support (checked against `--help` on 2026-11-04)

| Tool | Set the ID at launch | Resume by ID |
|---|---|---|
| Copilot CLI | `copilot --session-id <id>` | `copilot --resume <id>` |
| Claude Code | `claude --session-id <uuid>` | `claude --resume <id>` |
| agy | not seen | `agy --conversation <id>` |
| vibe | not seen | `vibe --resume <id>` |

Where the ID can't be set at launch, `fleet` has to find it after the start. It looks for the newest session folder for that tool and directory (for Copilot, `~/.copilot/session-state/`). For agy and vibe, the location of session storage is still to be confirmed.

---

## 4. The `fleet` CLI

| Command | What it does |
|---|---|
| `fleet spawn [--worktree\|--no-worktree] [--driver tmux\|acp] <name> <tool> <dir> ["prompt"]` | New tmux window, starts the tool, records the session ID, creates the card; in a git repo the agent gets its own worktree (see below). every profile that runs copilot or agy (claude-copilot included) runs over ACP under `fleet acp-host` by default, the rest in their TUI; `--driver` picks (§7d, ADR-74). It fails before any worktree, board entry or pane when the dir does not exist or the command the window would run (the profile's, or its `acp_command`) is not on PATH (fleet#38, #23); `fleet up` checks the admiral's command the same way |
| `fleet ls [--json]` | All agents with status, live/parked and RAM; a `DRIVER` column (`acp` or `tmux`, ADR-77) |
| `fleet info <name>` | One agent's facts, the same lines as `fleet_info` (ADR-77) |
| `fleet spend [--json]` | ✅ built (2026-11-05) What each agent has cost so far and the fleet total, read from its own session file: AI credits for Copilot, tokens for Claude (§7b, ADR-46) |
| `fleet peek <name> [--raw] [-n 200]` | The pane text. `--summary`: about 5 lines from a cheap model instead (§6.2, ADR-40). |
| `fleet send <name> "text"` | Paste into the agent and press Enter |
| `fleet park <name>` | Ask the agent to update its card, wait, stop the process, mark it parked |
| `fleet wake <name>` | Restart with the tool's resume flag in the same tmux window |
| `fleet takeover <name> [--acp]` | Move an ACP agent's session into the tool's own screen on tmux, or (`--acp`) hand it back to an ACP host (ADR-82) |
| `fleet close <name>` | Stop it and archive the card (`kill` is a hidden alias) |
| `fleet note <name> "text"` | Add an entry to `decisions.log` |
| `fleet wait [--agents a,b] [--timeout 300s] [--as NAME] [--admiral NAME]` | ✅ built (2026-11-09) Block until a new report or message lands in the caller's mailbox (an agent's own, else the admiral's), at most 10m; prints the headlines and marks nothing read. Exit 0 on news, 2 on timeout or Ctrl-C (§6.7, ADR-72) |
| `fleet team <action> <team> ...` | ✅ built (2026-11-05) Create/delete teams, add/remove agents, set the colour, and send/show/hide/close a whole team (see §5c) |
| `fleet branches [--repo DIR] [--json] [--prune]` | The repo's `fleet/` branches on origin: merged into main (by ancestry or equal patch) and the live or parked agent on each. `--prune` deletes the merged ones no agent is on, each with a lease on the sha it checked (ADR-87) |
| `fleet worktrees [--repo DIR] [--json] [--prune]` | The repo's leftover agent worktrees under `<repo>.wt/`: size, merged, dirty (`result.md` when the agent's untracked handoff is the only change), and the live or parked agent holding each. `--prune` removes the merged, clean ones no agent holds, with `git worktree remove` (never `--force`, so git refuses uncommitted work) and `git branch -d` (ADR-79) |
| `fleet adopt [--name N] [--force] [--driver tmux\|acp] <profile> <session-id> [dir]` | Bring an existing session (started outside fleet) under management by resuming it in a fleet window, over the profile's driver (see below) |

### A worktree per agent (ADR-39, built)

Several agents on one checkout collide: they stage each other's files, and `git` has one index per work tree. So when the dir handed to `fleet spawn` is inside a git repo, the agent does not work there. Fleet runs `git worktree add` first and starts the tool in the result.

- **Where:** `<repo>.wt/<name>` beside the repo's *main* worktree, on branch `fleet/<name>` — the same place and shape the worker playbook already told agents to make by hand (§4 playbook). Spawning from inside a linked worktree still hangs the new one off the main checkout, so they never nest.
- **What the board records:** `worktree: {path, branch, repo}` on the agent entry, and `dir` is the worktree. `fleet info` shows `agent worktree: <path> on <branch>`. Closing an agent never removes the worktree or the branch (§6.7), so `fleet close` and the idle sweep name them instead: "Note: a1's branch is not merged into main (worktree …, branch fleet/a1)" and "worktree left behind (not merged into main): …". `fleet worktrees` lists what is left with its size and `--prune` removes the merged, clean ones no agent holds (ADR-79).
- **Pushed branches are deleted once merged (ADR-87).** An agent pushes `fleet/<name>` to origin for CI (ADR-69) and, after `fleet merge`, deletes it (pro playbook, checklist 8). As a safety net, closing an agent (`fleet close`, auto-close, the idle sweep) fetches its branch from origin and deletes it there when every commit is in main, by ancestry or by equal patch (`git cherry`, since `fleet merge` rebases first); the delete carries a lease on the sha it checked, and the local branch and worktree stay. `fleet branches [--prune]` lists origin's `fleet/` branches and deletes the merged ones no live or parked agent is on; the publisher runs it at release time. The GitHub repo also has "Automatically delete head branches" on, for branches merged through a PR.
- **Claude's file tools are fenced to `<repo>.wt/` (ADR-79).** A Claude agent (a profile whose `--settings {hooks}` carries fleet's hooks) spawned or woken in a fleet worktree gets `permissions.blockWritesOutsideWorkingDirectories` plus `additionalDirectories: [<repo>.wt]` in that settings JSON, so Write, Edit and NotebookEdit work in its own worktree and a sibling `../<name>.spike`, and refuse the main checkout, home and other repos. Shell, MCP and hooks are not covered, so `fleet report`, the result file and a `cp` to `~/Downloads` still work. `fence_writes = false` under `[defaults]` or `[profile.<name>]` turns it off.
- **Reuse:** a live worktree at that path is adopted as is, so re-spawning a name picks its work up rather than failing. An existing `fleet/<name>` branch is checked out again, and a registration left behind by a worktree dir someone deleted by hand is pruned and made again rather than handing the agent a path that is not there.
- **Opt out:** `worktree = false` in `config.toml` (`[defaults]` or `[profile.<name>]`), `fleet spawn --no-worktree` for one spawn (`--worktree` forces it back on), and `worktree: false` on the `fleet_spawn` MCP tool. A queued agent keeps the choice its spawn made, whatever the config says by the time a slot frees.
- **When git refuses** (the path is taken by something else, the branch is checked out elsewhere), or the checkout takes longer than 30s, the spawn falls back to the dir itself with a warning in `fleet.log` rather than failing: a usable agent in a shared checkout beats no agent. The bound matters because `spawn` blocks, including the `fleet_spawn` MCP call. A dir outside git simply has no worktree.
- **The baseline and the playbook come from the worktree**, not the spawn dir: a fresh worktree is clean, so auto-close (§6.7) sees exactly the agent's own work, and `.fleet/worker.md` is found through the worktree's `.git` file.
- `fleet adopt` never creates one — the session already has a dir — but when that dir *is* a linked worktree it is recorded the same way, so close and sweep report its leftovers too.

### The agent's dir is trusted before the tool starts, and is never $HOME (ADR-47, built)

Every tool now asks "do you trust this folder?" the first time it runs somewhere new, and a spawn that stops on that menu is a spawn nobody is watching. So the last thing fleet does before starting the tool — after the worktree exists, so it is the dir the tool will actually read — is write that dir into the tool's *own* trust store, the documented way:

- **Claude Code:** `projects["<dir>"].hasTrustDialogAccepted = true` in `$CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR/.claude.json`, else `~/.claude.json`, together with `hasClaudeMdExternalIncludesApproved`/`…WarningShown` — the modal that comes up right behind it when your own CLAUDE.md imports other files. The profile's env decides the file, so `claude-personal` and `claude-work` get their own.
- **Copilot CLI:** the dir is appended to `trustedFolders` in `~/.copilot/config.json`, keeping the `//` header that file opens with. (This is why copilot agents don't prompt today: the user's own home is usually on that list already.)
- **vibe:** added to `trusted` and removed from `untrusted` in `$VIBE_HOME/trusted_folders.toml`, default `~/.vibe` — removed too, because vibe decides by closest decided ancestor.
- **agy** has no trust store on disk that fleet can find, so it falls through to the on-screen fallback below.

Each file is read and rewritten under a lock of its own in `~/.fleet/locks` — nothing is left behind in a directory the tool itself reads — and the read happens inside that lock, into a temp file in the same dir, then renamed: a tool writing its own config at the same moment can lose nothing but the race. Every other key is preserved. A missing config dir means the tool has never run, so there is nothing to pre-answer and fleet logs it instead of creating one. `auto_trust = false` in `config.toml` (per profile or in `[defaults]`) turns it off.

**Fallback:** when fleet could not pre-mark the dir *and* the tool's own `waiting` patterns mention the trust question (today: agy), it watches the new pane for up to 10s — ending the moment the input box is up — and picks the dialog's "Yes, I trust …" option. It runs *before* the first prompt is pasted, because the dialog reads as `waiting` and would otherwise swallow it. A spawn fleet did pre-mark never waits for this.

**Never $HOME.** A spawn or adopt dir that resolves to the home directory or `/` is moved to `~/.fleet/scratch/<name>`, which is then trusted like any other dir. The CLI and `fleet_spawn` say so in their reply ("fleet does not start an agent in the home directory, so this is a scratch dir of its own"), and so does `fleet.log`. `fleet up`'s own fallback for a missing admiral dir uses the same scratch root.

### `fleet adopt`

Moves a session that was started in a plain terminal into fleet, one at a time (§8 item 6).

- Starts the profile's `resume` args (`{id}` = the session ID) in a new hidden agent window, exactly like `spawn` but with no launch ID and no prompt. The board entry records the session ID, so `wake` and `park` work on it later. Under the ACP driver (the default for profiles that run copilot or agy, `--driver` picks) the window runs `fleet acp-host --session <id>` instead, as a wake does (§7d).
- **dir**: given, or for Copilot the `cwd:` from `~/.copilot/session-state/<id>/workspace.yaml`, or the current directory.
- **name**: `--name`, or the base name of dir (made safe for names), with `-2`, `-3`… if taken.
- **Session must exist**: profiles may set `session_path`, a glob with `{id}` and `~` (Copilot: `~/.copilot/session-state/{id}`, Claude: `<config dir>/projects/*/{id}.jsonl`). If it is set and nothing matches, adopt fails. Profiles without it (agy, vibe, fake) skip the check.
- **Session must not be running**: two processes on one session corrupt it. Adopt refuses when a live process holds it: a Copilot `inuse.<pid>.lock` in the session folder with a live pid, or any process whose command line contains the session ID. The error names the pid and says to close that terminal first. `--force` skips both checks.
- MCP: `fleet_adopt` (profile, session_id, optional dir, name and driver), same checks.
- Limit: a Claude session started fresh (no `--resume <id>` on its command line) has no lock file, so the in-use check can't see it. Close it before adopting.
- Out of scope: discovering sessions to adopt (a picker), closing the outside terminal for you.

### Spawn startup: autopilot and the first prompt (ADR-13)

Measured on 2026-11-04 against Copilot CLI, the two reasons a freshly spawned agent needed hand-holding:

1. **The first prompt was swallowed.** `spawn` slept a fixed 1500 ms and then pasted. The Copilot TUI is not in raw mode yet at that point, so both the pasted text *and* the Enter went nowhere — the input box stayed empty. Reproduced on `tmux -L fleet-test-autostart`: paste at 1.5 s → empty box; at 15 s → submitted.
2. **The session started in Interactive / Manual Approval.** Nothing asked for autopilot, so every tool call stopped for approval.

The fix is in two layers, both data-driven (profiles are data, ADR-5):

- **Launch-time delivery, preferred.** A profile may declare `prompt_args` (args carrying the initial prompt, `{prompt}` substituted) and `mode_args` per mode. The tool then starts already in the right mode with the prompt submitted by the tool itself — no keystrokes, no race. Copilot: `prompt_args = ["-i", "{prompt}"]`, `mode_args = { autopilot = "--allow-all --autopilot" }`. `--autopilot` on its own opens a blocking "Enable autopilot mode" permission dialog; `--allow-all` is what makes it unattended, so it is part of the mode args. Claude Code's equivalent is `--permission-mode`.

  **`auto` is the one mode name every profile knows** (ADR-56): it means "run every tool call without asking", and each built-in profile maps it to its own tool's flag: `--allow-all --autopilot` (Copilot), `--permission-mode bypassPermissions` (Claude Code), `--dangerously-skip-permissions` (agy), `--auto-approve` (vibe), nothing at all (the fake agent, which never asks). One `mode = "auto"` under `[defaults]` therefore makes the whole fleet unattended, whatever tool each agent runs. The tool-specific names stay as aliases, so `mode = "autopilot"` still works for Copilot.

  `mode_args` and `mode_keys` are **inline tables of space-separated strings**, not of arrays: the `internal/conf` TOML subset types an inline table as `map[string]string`, and fleet splits each value on whitespace. `context_args` has the same shape, keyed by effort level instead of mode (ADR-60). A dotted key such as `mode_args.autopilot = [...]` is not part of the subset and is silently dropped. Limitation: an argument that itself contains a space cannot be expressed this way — a tool needing one has to get it from `args` or local `extra_args`.
- **Paste fallback, for tools without those flags.** Wait until the pane is actually ready — poll `capture-pane` until the profile's status patterns classify it as **idle or waiting** (those two describe a live input box), or an explicit `ready` regex matches — then paste, then press Enter. `working` and `error` deliberately do not count: a bare spinner glyph matches the `working` patterns and can render during startup before the TUI is in raw mode, which is the very condition that lost the prompt. Tools whose banner arrives before any input box (the `fake` agent) set an explicit `ready` regex instead. A profile may also declare `mode_keys` (keys to send once ready) for tools that only offer a keystroke. Readiness replaces the fixed sleep in both paths.

### Local spawn preferences (`$XDG_CONFIG_HOME/fleet/config.toml`)

Repo defaults stay neutral: fleet out of the box spawns in whatever mode the tool defaults to. Autopilot is a **local, per-user** choice and lives in a file that is never committed — `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME/fleet/config.toml`, default `~/.config/fleet/config.toml` (`FLEET_CONFIG` overrides the path, for tests). It is the same TOML subset as `tools.toml`.

```toml
[defaults]                      # applies to every profile
mode = "auto"                   # no permission prompts, whatever the tool; "" = leave the tool's default
auto_submit = true              # press Enter after a pasted prompt
ready_timeout = "15s"           # how long to wait for the TUI before giving up
worktree = true                 # a git worktree per agent (false: spawn straight into dir)
auto_trust = true               # pre-answer the tool's "do you trust this folder?" (ADR-47)

[profile.copilot]               # per-profile override of the above
mode = "autopilot"
extra_args = ["--reasoning-effort", "high"]

[profile.claude]
mode = "accept-edits"

[profile.my-copilot]            # defines a new profile, or overrides a built-in
command = "copilot"
args = ["--model", "claude-opus-5.5"]
patterns = "copilot"
```

- `[profile.<name>]` is a third override layer for tool profiles, after the built-ins and `~/.fleet/tools.toml`; it accepts every `tools.toml` key, so a local file can define or redefine a profile without touching the repo.
- `mode` names a key in the profile's `mode_args` / `mode_keys`; `auto` is the one name every built-in profile defines (ADR-56). An unknown mode for a profile is logged and ignored rather than failing the spawn. A profile from your own `tools.toml` that defines no `auto` therefore keeps prompting, so give it one.
- `extra_args` are appended to the launch argv.
- `profile` in `[defaults]` is the tool profile `fleet_spawn` and `fleet_squad_spawn` use when the call names none; re-read on every spawn. With neither, the call fails and says to pass a profile or set this. Every spawn reply, the CLI's too, names the profile and its model: the last `--model` in the profile's `args` or in `extra_args`, or "tool default model" when neither sets one (fleet#31).
- `auto_trust` writes the agent's dir into the tool's own trust store before launch, and lets fleet answer a trust dialog that still shows (ADR-47). `false` leaves both to you.
- `worktree` turns the per-agent worktree on or off for a profile (ADR-39); `fleet spawn --worktree` / `--no-worktree` and the `fleet_spawn` `worktree` argument override it for one spawn.
- Precedence: `[profile.<name>]` > `[defaults]` > built-in (no mode, `auto_submit = true`, `ready_timeout = 15s`). `spawn` blocks for up to `ready_timeout`, including the `fleet_spawn` MCP call, so the default stays well under a typical MCP client tool timeout.
- `auto_submit = false` also disables launch-time prompt delivery: `copilot -i <prompt>` submits the prompt itself, so honouring "paste but don't press Enter" means falling back to the paste path.
- A `[profile.<name>]` section that neither names an existing profile nor sets `command` is ignored with a warning, so a typo like `[profile.coplot]` cannot create a command-less profile.
- **Spawn args are the same across agents of one profile** (PF7): `Argv` and `ACPArgv` share one order (peer MCP, plugin, user MCP switches, then hooks and the rest), the MCP switches are sorted and the excluded-tools lists are constants, so only the agent's name, session id, plugin dir and prompt differ and agents started close together share the tool's cached prompt prefix. The built-in `claude` passes `--exclude-dynamic-system-prompt-sections` (cwd and git status move to the first user message). Per-agent values stay in argv: the MCP config is not part of the model's prompt, and moving `--agent <name>` to the env would depend on the tool passing its env to MCP children.
- **`native_binary = true`** (PF6, default off) makes a Copilot profile start the native binary the Node launcher would run (`node_modules/@github/copilot-<platform>-<arch>/copilot`, found from the launcher's real path the way Node resolves it), under tmux and ACP, saving the launcher's ~65 MB per agent. It skips the launcher's version pick and auto-update; when the binary isn't found fleet logs why and starts the launcher.
- Out of scope: changing the mode of an already running agent, and a CLI flag per spawn.

### Worker playbook: the process every spawned agent gets (ADR-22, built)

On 2026-11-05 the admiral repeated the same process rules in every prompt, and agents still broke them: one pushed during a push hold, some waited on choices the user had already delegated, a test fixture nearly leaked private data, and parallel agents edited the same files. `fleet spawn` now prepends a short **worker playbook** to the first prompt, so every new agent gets the process with no one having to remember it.

- **What an agent receives:** the playbook, then `## Your task`, then the prompt. The default text is `internal/playbook/worker.md` (about 40 lines): time matters, so avoidable time is not spent and an earlier correct result is better; read-only tasks edit nothing; one worktree per agent — stay in the one spawn made (`fleet/<name>`), make `../<repo>.wt/<name>` only when there is none; plan the files first and check `fleet touching` for overlap, with merge order read from branch state (fewer touched files first) rather than waited on from a reply; own your area and name your dependencies; decide rather than wait when a choice was delegated; escalate `blocked` early; keep scope (extra work becomes a follow-up in the report); take the ADR number from the task prompt rather than reading DESIGN.md §14; test first when cheap, with real but scrubbed fixtures; one line for a leader (split the task, one agent per piece, one summary upward, escalate anything you may not answer — the rest is in the leader's own MCP instructions, §6.11); an eight-step pre-merge checklist (build and the changed packages' tests locally, subagent review, docs/ADR, clean diff, conventional commit with `Assisted-by:`, squash fixups before the first push, push the `fleet/<name>` branch and merge only once CI is green on its head (ADR-69), merge into local main and leave the worktree and branch for fleet and the user to clean up, ADR-29); never push main, force-push or rewrite pushed history (the agent's own `fleet/` branch is the only push, ADR-69); stop on merge conflicts unless told to use best judgment, except the CHANGELOG.md and DESIGN.md top-of-section conflicts every batch produces, where both sides' lines are kept; finish with `fleet_report` (or `fleet report` from a shell; no marker lines, ADR-20) with optional `next:`, `risks:`, `decisions:` and `retro:` detail lines. Why each rule is there: `docs/process.md` (ADR-35).
- **Effort levels (ADR-57).** The full text is professional-code-work advice and over-steers a small job, so a spawn picks how much of it to pay for: `light` (`internal/playbook/light.md`, about 12 lines: time matters, work in the dir you were started in, decide rather than wait, report with `fleet_report`, plus the safety core every level carries — ADR-59) for an errand or a quick read-only question; `standard` (`standard.md`, about 22 lines: light plus work on your own branch — the worktree's, or a `fleet/` branch made in a shared checkout — a two-or-three-line plan, escalate early, keep scope, build and test what you changed, conventional commit with `Assisted-by:`, push only its own `fleet/` branch for CI (ADR-69), merge only if the task says to) for a contained change; `pro` (`worker.md`) for the full process above. The default is `pro` when the spawn asked for a worktree *and* the dir is a git repo — including when git refused the worktree and the agent fell back to the shared checkout, which is where the process matters most — and `light` otherwise. So an ordinary code spawn behaves exactly as it did before, while `--no-worktree` / `worktree: false` in a repo now also drops to `light`: that opt-out is for errands and read-only work, and work that must land in the user's checkout asks for `--effort pro` with it. `fleet spawn --effort LEVEL`, `effort` on `fleet_spawn` and `fleet_squad_spawn`, and `fleet playbook --effort LEVEL` to read one. Effort never changes the model: every fleet agent runs Opus at every level (ADR-100). A queued agent carries its level to the drain, like its worktree choice and its card. The user's and the repo's layers apply at every level, and `playbook = false` still turns all of it off.
- **Layers**, applied in order, each appended after a blank line: built-in → `~/.fleet/worker.md` (the user's own rules) → `.fleet/worker.md` in the agent's repo, found by walking up from the spawn dir to the first directory with a `.git` entry (so a worktree finds its committed copy). A file whose first line is `<!-- fleet:replace -->` replaces everything before it; a replace file with nothing else switches the playbook off for that repo.
  This repo's own `.fleet/worker.md` adds the real done-check (`make test` always exits 0) and the test-socket rules.
- **Off switch:** `playbook = false` in `[defaults]` or `[profile.<name>]` of the local `config.toml`.
- **Inspect:** `fleet playbook [--effort light|standard|pro] [dir]` prints the effective text for a directory, with its sources on stderr (default `pro`).
- **Conflict check:** `fleet touching [-C dir] [--json] [file...]` lists the files every other worktree of the repo changes (committed on its branch since the merge base with `main`, plus uncommitted). The uncommitted task-card result file of any agent on the board is left out (each agent writes its own; a committed one still counts, since it would conflict on merge), here and in `fleet merge`'s order (#11). With file arguments it prints only the overlap and exits 1 when there is one (0 none, 2 error, so a failed check never reads as all-clear); it works as a pre-edit check. Committed renames report both paths.
- **Remote builds:** `fleet exec [--host H] -- <command...>` runs one build or test for the current worktree on `[exec] host` (an ssh destination): rsync copies the worktree to `<[exec] root, default fleet-exec>/<dir>-<hash>` on that host, leaving out `.git` and git-ignored files, then ssh runs the command in the same subdirectory and passes its output and exit code back. One argument is a shell line, several are quoted words. With no host set it runs locally, so a playbook can always say `fleet exec` (ADR-107).
- **Merging back:** `fleet merge [-C dir] [--dry-run [--json]] [--check CMD] [--retries N]` runs the pre-merge step the playbook describes: rebase this worktree's branch onto `main`, run the checks, merge into the `main` checkout (fast-forward when possible, else `--no-ff`) and run them again there. Conflicts in `CHANGELOG.md` and `DESIGN.md` keep both sides in that file's own order — the replayed line goes above in the CHANGELOG (newest first) and below in DESIGN.md (ADRs ascend) — and any other conflict aborts the rebase and exits 1, naming the files and the other worktree. If `main` moves while the checks run it starts over, up to `--retries` times (default 3). `--dry-run` prints the order it would take, from the same branch state `fleet touching` reads. It never pushes and never removes the worktree (ADR-29, ADR-48).
- The board's goal stays the user's prompt, not the playbook, and the logged argv redacts the whole composed prompt. tmux refuses a launch command over about 16 KB, so when the composed prompt would push it past 12 KB, spawn sends it by the paste path instead of as an argument.
- Not covered: a spawn without a prompt (nothing to prepend to, so the admiral always spawns with the task as the prompt), and adopted sessions (they keep their own context).
- The admiral relies on the playbook: its prompts give the task, the owner boundaries and any exceptions, not the process rules (`internal/mcp/instructions.md`).
- Two of the day's failures are spawn mechanics, not process, and a prompt can't fix them: agents in Manual Approval mode blocking on path-access prompts for their own worktree, and pasted prompts left unsubmitted. They belong with spawn options (ADR-13, fleet-mcp).

What the playbook took from established practice (checked 2026-11-05), and what it left out:

| Practice | In the playbook as | Left out |
|---|---|---|
| Definition of done | The seven-step pre-merge checklist, with checks the agent runs itself | Coverage targets, sign-off roles |
| Small, PR-sized changes | "one small, reviewable change"; one worktree and branch per agent | Stacked PRs (local main only, nothing to stack on) |
| TDD | "failing test first when cheap"; real captured fixtures for parsers | Mandatory TDD for every change |
| Conventional commits | `feat:`/`fix:`/`docs:` subject plus `Assisted-by:` | Scopes, breaking-change footers, changelog tooling |
| ADRs | "a decision others will ask why about gets a short ADR" (repo's own ADR list) | A separate ADR directory and template |
| Review checklist | A subagent review of the diff before merging, plus the clean-diff item | A long checklist (agents skim long docs) |
| Agent practice: plan first, scoped context, verification loop | Plan the files and the check before editing; quote the command that passed | Plan-mode approval gates (they stalled agents today) |
| Agent practice: ask vs. assume | Decide when delegated; ask only for scope, public or irreversible steps | — |
| Agent practice: handoffs | `fleet_report` with done/blocked/question and a summary: headline line, optional detail lines (ADR-20) | Free-form status chatter |

### Finding sessions to adopt: `fleet sessions`

Nobody remembers session IDs. Fleet finds them from the tools' own session stores, so adopt works with words.

- `fleet sessions [--all] [--json] [-n 20] [words...]` lists sessions newest first: age, suggested profile, dir, title, short ID, and `in use` / `on board` markers. Title = the tool's own name for the session (Copilot `workspace.yaml` `name`/`summary`, Claude `custom-title`/`summary`), else the first user prompt, cut to one line.
- Sources are the profiles' `session_path` globs (no new config). Several profiles sharing one store (copilot, claude-copilot) give one source; the suggested profile is the one whose `--model` matches the session's model family (claude vs gpt), else the one with no args.
- Hidden unless `--all`: sessions with no user prompt (hook and probe runs) and sessions already on the board.
- `words`: case-insensitive, every word must appear in title, dir, branch or ID.
- `fleet adopt [--name N] [--dir D] [--force] [profile] <id | words...>`: the profile is optional (suggested one is used). An exact ID or a unique ID prefix of at least 8 characters adopts that session; otherwise the words search, leaving out sessions in use or on the board. One match is adopted, none or several is an error that lists up to 5 candidates. A positional dir after an exact ID still works.
- MCP: `fleet_sessions` (optional `query`, `limit`) returns one line per session; `fleet_adopt` takes `session_id` or `query`, `profile` optional. Admiral rule: on "adopt my X session" call `fleet_sessions` with the user's words, ask which one if more than one, then `fleet_adopt` with the ID.
- Reading is bounded: only the newest sessions are opened, and at most the first 256 KB of a transcript is read.
- In use = a live Copilot `inuse.<pid>.lock`, or a process with the ID as a whole argument (tmux and screen servers are ignored: they keep the command they were started with).
- Built 2026-11-04.
- Out of scope: a TUI picker, agy and vibe stores.

### Status detection

Status is read from the bottom of the pane each poll (every 1–2 seconds):

| Status | Signal |
|---|---|
| working | pane text changed since the last poll, or the tool's spinner or "thinking" marker is visible |
| waiting | approval prompt, y/n prompt or question form (one pattern set per tool) |
| error | error banner, crashed process or non-zero exit |
| stuck | grinding: the same error banner twice in a row, or working with an unchanged pane hash for `stuck_after` (default 10m) — see "The stuck rule" below |
| idle | turn finished: empty input box and the tool's idle footer, nothing asked |
| parked | no process; resumes on demand |

How the patterns are applied (`internal/status`, per-tool sets in `patterns.toml` with keys `waiting`, `error`, `working`, `idle`, `transcript`, `pending`, `input`, `placeholder`, and the scalar `scan`):

- Only the last 14 non-empty lines are read: enough for a question form or approval box plus the footer under it.
- **waiting wins anywhere in that window.** A Copilot approval box ("Do you want to run this command?", "↑/↓ to navigate · enter to select · esc to cancel") or question form ("Copilot needs information.", "↑/↓ select · enter accept …") can sit above a footer that still says "Working". The line matched by the first `waiting` pattern in list order becomes `waiting_for` (with the question under "needs information." appended).
- `transcript` marks lines of the scrolling conversation (Copilot: lines ending in the right-hand scrollbar `┃`). A waiting pattern there only counts on the transcript's last content line, so prompt text an agent quoted earlier in its work doesn't make it look blocked.
- Otherwise the **bottom-most** line matching `error`, `working` or `idle` decides — normally the tool's footer. Copilot: busy is "● Working · … esc esc interrupt"; idle is the mode footer ("Interactive · Allow All · / commands · space hold to record") without "Working". Picker hints like "esc to cancel" mean waiting, never working.
- `scan = 'window'` makes `working`, and then `error`, win anywhere in the window instead, like `waiting` already does; it is checked after `waiting` and `pending` and before the bottom-most scan. Claude, agy and vibe need it: they draw the spinner and the error banner *above* the prompt box and keep a mode footer below it that reads the same busy or not ("⏵⏵ auto mode on", "ctx ░░░ 0%", "0/202k tokens"), so the bottom-most rule always said idle. Working is tried before error so a stale error line above a live spinner still reads as working. Copilot and fake have no `scan` key and keep the bottom-most rule.
- `input` matches a line of the prompt box; its first capture group is the text typed there. Copilot: `'^┃(?: (.*))?$'` (the box is `┃` lines between the `▄▄▄` and `▀▀▀` rules) and `'^❯(?: (.*))?$'` (the other layout: `❯` between `───` rules); Claude, agy and vibe: `'^\s*[>❯][\s\x{a0}](.*)$'` and the bare box; fake: `'^>\s?(.*)$'`. The bottom-most run of matching lines, plus the indented rows a wrapped entry continues on below it up to a rule, is the box. Report wakes use it (§6.7, ADR-36). Claude's empty box is `❯` followed by a **non-breaking space** (U+00A0), which Go's `\s` does not match, so every one of these patterns spells out `[\s\x{a0}]`.
- `placeholder` matches a box that is empty but shows a hint rather than nothing — Claude's `Try "refactor <filepath>"`, agy's and vibe's "Ask anything…". `InputBox` reports those as empty, so the wake guard types into them instead of treating the hint as the user's unsent text. The `error` and `working` patterns of a `scan = 'window'` set are kept narrow for the same reason the window is wide: a tool result the agent is still reading (`⎿ Error: ls: /nope`) or a bullet in its own prose must not read as a crash or a spinner, so they match the banner forms only ("● API Error", "⚠ failed to", "Error: Failed to", "Error ID:") and Claude's five spinner glyphs.
- `pending` marks text pasted into the input box but not submitted (Copilot: `'^┃ \[Paste #\d+'`, the `[Paste #1 - 13 lines]` chip on the input-box row). Checked after `waiting`, on non-transcript lines of the same window: a match is `waiting` with `waiting_for` "unsubmitted input: [Paste #1 - 13 lines]". Claude has no `pending` pattern yet (no captured fixture of its paste chip).
- tmux's "Pane is dead (status …)" banner, or a dead pane, is always `error`.
- **A tool's own hook beats the screen when it is newer.** `~/.fleet/status/<name>.json` holds what that agent's hooks last said (`status`, `waiting_for`, `event`, `at`; written by `fleet hook <event>`, §7c, ADR-49). It decides when the screen matched no pattern, and overrides a match that is older than the hook. Scraping stays the fallback for every tool without hooks. `working` is the one hook state with an expiry (two minutes): a tool killed mid-tool-call leaves its last `PreToolUse` behind, while `idle` and `waiting` are states an agent legitimately rests in.
- A **menu** on screen is `waiting` whatever the other patterns made of the pane, numbered (`❯ 1. Yes`) or not (Claude's folder-trust check: `❯ No, exit` / `  Yes, I trust this folder`). Its own question, not the hint under it, is `waiting_for`. An unnumbered ❯ list needs at least two option lines, exactly one cursor, no blank line between them and labels with a word in them, so a prompt box with typed text is never a menu. `❯` and `›` are cursors on their own; a plain `>` (agy's cursor) also starts a quoted shell command in an agent's own prose, so a `>` block counts only when the screen also shows how to work the picker ("↑/↓ Navigate · enter Confirm", "Esc to cancel"). Options are numbered in the parse either way, so `fleet answer` / `fleet_answer` picks one by index or label (Up/Down, Enter) and reports that the numbers were not on screen.
- `fleet refresh` tags the pane (`@fleet_status`, shown in the pane title) on every transition, and re-syncs the tag whenever it differs from the board.

**The stuck rule (ADR-45).** An agent is *stuck* when it is grinding rather than progressing. Two triggers, in `internal/status`:

- **The same error banner twice in a row.** A screen classified `error` records its banner. It counts again only after a poll that read *no* error, so a banner that merely stays on screen is one failure however much else moves — which matters for the window-scan tools (ADR-42), where `Match` reports a banner for as long as it is anywhere on screen. A `working` screen between two sightings is the retry, not a recovery, and keeps the memory; the second sighting is stuck.
- **No progress.** `working` with a pane hash unchanged for `stuck_after` (`[defaults]`/`[profile.<name>]` in `config.toml`, default `10m`, `"off"` or `"0"` turns this trigger off). A build that prints output changes the hash, so a long but progressing build is never stuck; a long *silent* one is, which is the known cost of reading screens.

It clears when the screen changes: literally for the no-progress trigger, and for the error trigger when the screen stops showing that failure — the agent reached its prompt (`idle`), is asking me (`waiting`), hit a different error, or has been working without that banner for two minutes. The memory is also dropped when the pane dies or the agent goes `gone`, so a resumed agent starts a fresh episode.

Stuck is a label and a message, not an intervention. The tile goes red through the existing error/failing theme role, and the admiral is told **once per episode** through the normal report/wake path (ADR-36): a `blocked` report in its mailbox and one wake line. The episode is claimed on the board under the board lock, and only the process that drains the spawn queue (the overview TUI) claims it, so a one-shot `fleet ls` can neither wake the admiral nor use up the episode's one notification. Fleet never pauses, parks or kills a stuck agent by default; `stuck_action = "interrupt"` opts into sending one `Escape` to the pane as well, under the same guards as a report wake (ADR-36): the pane must still be alive and still be that agent's, and the user must not be typing.

**Ghost text is not on the screen.** Every capture drops a faint run that ends its line, such as Claude Code's grey next-prompt suggestion, so an input box showing only a suggestion reads as empty and idle (ADR-65).

**Fixtures are captured, never written by hand.** Every pattern set is backed by real pane captures in `internal/status/testdata/<tool>/<state>.txt`, and `TestCaptures` globs them and asserts the status named by the filename (the part before the first `-`, one of working, waiting, idle, error, stuck; `waiting-approval.txt` and `idle-typed.txt` are variants). `fleet snap [-C dir] [--set NAME] [--socket S] <agent | pane-id> <state>` writes one: it captures the agent's pane, normalises it the way the existing fixtures are kept (trailing whitespace off each line, no trailing blank lines, one final newline, non-breaking spaces left alone), writes it into the testdata dir found by walking up from the working dir, and warns when the capture does not currently classify as the state asked for. A raw tmux pane id instead of an agent name needs `--set` to say which pattern set it belongs to, and `--socket` to read a scratch tmux server; the socket must be a `fleet-test-*` one, so snap can never read the user's own tmux. The state must be lower-case words joined by `-` and the set must already exist, so a capture can only ever land on a fixture the table test reads. That is how the claude, agy and vibe fixtures were taken without paying for an agent turn: a local stub at `ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL` drives Claude Code into working, waiting and error, an unreachable `HTTPS_PROXY` gives a real error screen, and a TUI that has not been given a prompt yet is free.

Hooks are better where they exist. Claude Code and Copilot can run hooks on events such as "turn finished" and "needs permission". Those hooks can write status straight to the board, which is more reliable than scraping. Use scraping as the fallback that works with every tool.

### Subagents (built)

An agent that launches subagents (Copilot's `task` tool: builder, reviewer, explore …) shows one dot per subagent on the bottom row of its overview tile.

Source: the tool's own session event log, not the pane. The pane only holds the visible screen, so a subagent's "○ Read (id) Waiting for result…" line scrolls away while the subagent still runs, and a background subagent shows nothing at all once its `task` call returns. Copilot writes every subagent to `~/.copilot/session-state/<id>/events.jsonl` (checked on 737 local sessions, 2026-11-05):

| Event | Meaning |
|---|---|
| `subagent.started` (`data.toolCallId`, `agentId`, `data.agentName`) | running |
| `subagent.completed` | done; `data.cancelled: true` means stopped → failed |
| `tool.execution_complete` of that `toolCallId` with `success: false` | failed |
| `tool.execution_start` of `write_agent` (`agent_id` / `agent_ids`) | that finished subagent runs again (restored from the remembered `agentId` if already pruned; an unknown id does nothing) |
| `assistant.message` with that subagent's `agentId` and no `data.toolRequests`, then its `assistant.turn_end` | its final answer → done at the turn end. A later message with tool requests in the same turn cancels it (about 1 in 5 tool-less messages are mid-turn). After a re-wake this is often the only end signal (13 of 55 local re-wakes logged no second `subagent.completed`) |
| `assistant.turn_start` with the `agentId` of a finished (or pruned but known) subagent | running again. Catches a message queued while the subagent was busy, which restarts it right after its final turn with no `write_agent` in the log |
| `session.start` / `session.resume` / `session.shutdown` | anything still running died with the process → failed |

- The log path is profile data: `subagent_log = "~/.copilot/session-state/{id}/events.jsonl"` (Copilot profiles only for now). Agents without a session id, or tools without the key, show no dots.
- Without a log, the tool's own hooks (ADR-99): the fleet plugin runs `fleet hook subagentstart`/`subagentstop`, which keep the agent's list in `~/.fleet/status/subs/<name>.json` (flock, atomic rename). A start adds a running subagent (Claude's `agent_id`/`agent_type`, Copilot's `agentName`); a stop ends the running one with that id, else the oldest of that type, else the oldest; a session end fails what still runs. This drives Claude's dots on both drivers once a subagent has been reported. Copilot keeps the log, which also sees a finished subagent woken again by `write_agent` or a queued message; its hooks fire start once per subagent and stop once per subagent turn.
- `fleet refresh` reads only the bytes added since the last poll (offset kept on the board; a shorter file restarts from 0) and only decodes lines that mention a subagent, `write_agent`, a running `toolCallId`, or an `assistant.message`/`assistant.turn_end` carrying a running subagent's `"agentId"`, or an `assistant.turn_start` carrying a finished or known one's. A partial last line waits for the next poll. If tmux can't list panes, the poll leaves subagents alone.
- Kept on the board per agent: running subagents, plus finished ones for 5 minutes after they end (at most 12 in total; oldest finished go first), and the last 32 `agentId` → launch mappings (`sub_known`) so a late `write_agent` can bring a pruned one back. An agent whose pane is gone or dead has no running subagents.
- Tile: line 4, `•` per subagent, in start order. Running: green, with a slow wave across the running dots (dim step: dark green `#628067`, never grey, so it can't be mistaken for a done dot) (one step per 300 ms animation tick; colour only, the row never changes width, so no flicker). Done: grey. Failed or cancelled: red. More dots than fit: the newest that fit, then `+N`. No row when there are none.
- `fleet_status` gets a `subagents` column: running count (blank when 0). `fleet ls --json` carries the board's `subagents` list.
- Limit: an adopted session whose Copilot process had crashed shows its old subagents green until the resumed process logs `session.resume`, then red for 5 minutes.
- Limit: a second process resuming the same session marks running subagents failed; one that keeps working turns green again at its next turn (seen once in 667 local logs, about 11 seconds red).
- Out of scope: Claude Code's `Task` tool (its transcript has the same information; needs its own parser), dots on chips and wall borders, nested subagents beyond what the parent log records.

---

## 5. The TUI

Built in **Go with Bubble Tea and Lip Gloss** (see ADR-10). It's one binary, the same one as the CLI. Go 1.26 is already installed.

### Layout

```
┌ fleet ─ 31 agents · 9 live · 22 parked · RAM 4.1/30 GB · ⚠ 3 waiting ───────────────┐
│ ┌─ api-auth ──────┐ ┌─ web-nav ──┐ ┌─ infra-dns ─────┐ ┌─ db-migrate ────┐ │
│ │ 🟢 ⠹ copilot      │ │ 🟡 ● claude      │ │ 🔴 ✖ agy          │ │ 💤 vibe          │ │
│ │ api-service     │ │ web-ui        │ │ infra-repo  │ │ db-tools...... │ │
│ │ running go test  │ │ approve: npm test│ │ kustomize failed │ │ parked 2h        │ │
│ │ 512 MB · 3m      │ │ 280 MB · wait 4m │ │ 260 MB · 12m     │ │ —                │ │
│ └──────────────────┘ └──────────────────┘ └──────────────────┘ └──────────────────┘ │
│  … more rows, grouped by repo …                                                      │
├ admiral ─ ctx 34% 🟢 ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ > 3 agents are waiting. web-nav wants to run `npm test` — approve?            │
└ ←↑↓→ select · ⏎ replace ▶ · a add · Space preview · Tab next waiting · x take off wall · ? all keys ─┘
```

### Tile states

| State | Colour | Motion |
|---|---|---|
| working | green | static ○, last line of output updates |
| waiting for me | yellow | static ●, steady colour |
| error / stuck | red | still border, ✖ icon; stuck also shows "no output 12m" |
| idle / done | grey | dimmed, no motion |
| parked | dark grey | 💤, no process, no RAM shown |
| queued | dark grey | ⏸ and queue position, no process; starts when fewer than `max_working` agents are busy (§7a) |
| subagents | per dot: green running, grey done, red failed | bottom row of the tile, steady colours (§4 Subagents) |
| admiral | own tile | context fill: green < 40%, yellow 40–69%, red ≥ 70% (built as the admiral pane border label, §6.5) |

Nothing animates or blinks (ADR-81): one static glyph per state, in the state's colour, like Copilot CLI's own `○ Working`. A frame changes only when the board does, so the overview writes nothing while nothing happens.

### Keyboard (yes, arrow driven)

Every key that exists is in the registry (`internal/keys`) and in the generated table
under "Hotkey hints and help" below; that table, not this section, is the list. In short:
`←↑↓→` / `hjkl` select a tile, `⏎` puts the selected agent into the `▶` pane, `a` adds it
as another pane, `Space` previews it in a popup, `Tab` / `Shift-Tab` walk the waiting
agents, `x` takes a pane off the wall, `p` pins it, `u` undoes the last replace,
`1/2/4/6/9/m` pick the wall layout, `R` and `P` reset and re-profile the admiral, `g`
shows what's new and `?` shows the whole list.

Planned, not built. Nothing below is bound yet, and each has to find a free key:

| Key | Action | Note |
|---|---|---|
| `z` / `w` | Park / wake the selected agent | planned, §7 |
| `a` / `x` | Approve / reject when the agent is at a y/n prompt | planned, not specified yet; both keys are taken (`a` adds a pane, `x` takes one off the wall), so approve/reject needs different keys or a mode |
| `s` | Send a prompt to the selected agent (one-line input) | planned, not specified yet |
| `n` | Spawn wizard: name, tool, directory, first prompt | planned, not specified yet |
| `/` | Filter tiles by name, repo or status | planned, not specified yet |
| `f` | Focus session on this agent | planned, §6.4 |

### Hotkey hints and help (built)

The bottom tmux status line and the overview header show a short hint in plain words, written with `Alt-` instead of tmux's `M-`. The full list is one keypress away:

- **`Alt-?`** anywhere in the fleet tmux server opens a popup (`tmux display-popup`) with every hotkey, grouped by area (panes, wall, review, admiral, overview), one line each. Any key closes it. `fleet keys` prints the same list in a terminal.
- **`?`** in the overview opens the same list as an overlay; `q`, `Esc` or `?` closes it.
- One registry (`internal/keys`) is the single source of truth: the status hint, the overview hint, the popup, the overlay, `fleet keys` and the generated table below come from it. Tests fail if a tmux `M-*` or `fleetwall` chord binding in `fleet up` is missing from the registry (or the reverse), or if the table below is out of date (`fleet keys --markdown` regenerates it).

<!-- keys:begin -->
| Group | Key | Description |
|---|---|---|
| Panes | `Alt-←↑↓→` | move to the pane in that direction |
| Panes | `Alt-n` | put the next waiting agent into the ▶ pane |
| Panes | `Alt-z` | zoom this pane; press again to restore |
| Panes | `Alt-x` | hide this pane (the agent keeps running) |
| Panes | `Alt-o` | jump to the marked pane (right-click → Mark) |
| Panes | `click a pane` | make it the ▶ pane (the next card goes there) |
| Wall | `Alt-w 1/2/4/6/9/m` | same as Alt-1…9/m: Alt-w, let go, then the key |
| Wall | `Alt-1/2/4/6/9` | wall: show up to N panes |
| Wall | `Alt-m` | wall: one main pane + stack |
| Wall | `Alt-X` | wall: hide all agent panes; again: put them back |
| Review | `Alt-g` | what's new in fleet: its latest features, unseen ones marked |
| Admiral | `Alt-s` | show/hide the admiral |
| Admiral | `Alt-S` | move the admiral left/right |
| Admiral | `Alt-R` | reset the admiral to a fresh chat (asks y/n) |
| Admiral | `Alt-P` | pick the admiral's profile (copilot, claude, …) |
| Overview | `Alt-c` | overview: tiles ↔ compact chips |
| Overview | `⏎ / click` | put the selected agent into the ▶ pane (or onto the wall if no pane is ▶) |
| Overview | `a / +` | add the selected agent as a new pane (more slots if full) |
| Overview | `Space` | preview the selected agent in a popup (⏎ there puts it in ▶) |
| Overview | `Tab / Shift-Tab` | put the next/previous waiting agent into the ▶ pane (or onto the wall) |
| Overview | `Esc` | clear the ▶ target |
| Overview | `u` | undo the last replace (again: redo) |
| Overview | `p` | pin/unpin: a pinned pane is never replaced or evicted |
| Overview | `right-click` | menu: put in ▶, add, preview, take off, pin |
| Overview | `x` | take the selected agent off the wall |
| Overview | `←↑↓→ / hjkl` | select a tile |
| Overview | `g` | same as Alt-g: what's new in fleet |
| Overview | `1/2/4/6/9/m` | same as Alt-1…9 / Alt-m |
| Overview | `R` | same as Alt-R: reset the admiral (asks y/n) |
| Overview | `O` | hand the selected agent to another admiral (several admirals only) |
| Overview | `P` | same as Alt-P: pick the admiral's profile |
| Overview | `q` | quit the overview |

`Alt-?` (tmux) · `?` (overview): show this list.
<!-- keys:end -->

Out of scope: rebinding keys from config.

## 5a. Screen layout: a tiled wall of live sessions (R6, R7, R8, R9)

I want live session windows on a grid that I can click into, tiled across a large monitor. Drawing 40 live terminals inside the app itself would be slow. So the design splits the work between two layers:

- **Overview (fleet binary):** small tiles for *all* agents, showing summaries only. Cheap to draw.
  - Every agent gets a card while the pane has room: cards wrap onto as many rows as the height allows, taking more rows when that gets them to a comfortable width (`TileComfortW`, 34 columns), then share the pane width (26 to 48 columns, `internal/layout` `Tiles`).
  - Text shortens, it doesn't just cut: the profile is cut before `status age`, and the last line loses brackets before it is truncated (`53% (105k/200k)` → `53% 105k/200k`).
  - Only when the cards can't all fit does the last row become an overflow line, `+N hidden:` followed by clickable chips and `+k` for chips that don't fit. The card window scrolls so the selected agent always has a card. A pane too short for one card shows chips only.
- **Live wall (tmux panes):** real, interactive terminals for the 1–9 agents I'm working with right now. tmux and the terminal emulator draw these, both written in native code.

Everything sits in one tmux window, so one terminal window fills the monitor:

```
┌ overview (fleet) ─────────┬ live wall: real tmux panes ────────────────────────────┐
│ 🟢 api-auth   🟡 web-nav  │ ┌ web-nav (claude) ─────┬ infra-dns (agy) ──────┐ │
│ 🔴 infra-dns  💤 db-mig │ │ Approve `npm test`? [y/n]   │ Error: terraform plan │ │
│ 🟢 ci-cache    ⚪ docs │ │ █                           │ failed in modules/dns │ │
│ … 34 more, by repo …      │ ├─────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┤ │
│                           │ │ api-auth (copilot)         │ ci-cache (claude)│ │
│                           │ │ ⠹ running go test ./...     │ ⠹ writing tests        │ │
├ admiral ───────────────┤ └─────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────┘ │
│ > 3 waiting. Approve…     │                                                         │
└───────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**How it works**

- Each agent's pane lives in a hidden tmux window when it isn't on the wall.
- Clicking a tile in the overview (or pressing `Enter`) moves that agent's pane onto the wall with `tmux join-pane`/`swap-pane`. If the wall is full, the slot used least recently goes back to its hidden window. The process keeps running throughout. Nothing restarts.
- **Mouse:** `set -g mouse on`. Click a live pane to type into it. Drag a border to resize. Scroll to read history. Click an overview tile to bring that agent onto the wall. Drag-select in any pane copies to the system clipboard, and so do double-click (word) and triple-click (line): fleet takes the left-button drag for tmux even in panes whose program asked for the mouse (ADR-38), and `fleet up` sets tmux `copy-command` to `wl-copy`, `xclip`, `xsel` or `pbcopy`, whichever the session has (ADR-28). Clicks, the wheel and the right-click menu still reach the program.
- **Tiling layouts**, chosen from the overview (`1 2 4 6 9 m`), globally with `Alt+w` then the digit, or `Alt+<digit>` in terminals that don't use Alt+digit for tabs (Ptyxis and GNOME Terminal do): `1` one large pane; `2` side by side; `4` a 2×2; `6` a 3×2; `9` a 3×3; `M` main + stack (one large pane plus a column of small ones). Built from tmux's own layouts (`main-vertical`, `tiled`) plus saved custom layout strings.
- **Pin** (`p`, §5e) keeps a pane on the wall so it is never swapped out.
- **Zoom** (`Alt-z`, or tmux `prefix + z`) makes one pane fill the whole wall for a moment.
- Wall panes show a coloured border in the same colour as their tile, so status is visible in both places.
- Named wall setups ("web-ui work", "gitops incident") save and restore which agents are on the wall and in which layout.
- **Two monitors** (ADR-108): `fleet up --monitors 2` moves the admiral into its own window `admiral` for the first screen and opens `fleet wall attach` in a second terminal, a session grouped with `fleet` that shows `main` (overview + wall) full screen. Each screen sizes its own window; closing either terminal detaches it and nothing else. `fleet up --monitors 1` undoes it.

**Why this is fast**

- Agents off the wall have no on-screen terminal. Their output costs nothing to display.
- Status changes arrive as events from **tmux control mode** (`tmux -C`, `%output` messages per pane) instead of reading 40 panes every second. Panes are only captured when their output actually changed, and only the last lines.
- The overview redraws only the tiles that changed. Animation runs on a 300 ms timer (about 3 fps), and only for tiles that are spinning or pulsing. Each animated frame rewrites whole tile rows through tmux, and tmux moves the cursor out of the active pane and back for each one. At 10 fps that flickered the admiral pane's bottom lines on VTE. `fleet up` also turns on tmux's `sync` terminal feature for `xterm*` so each repaint lands in one piece.
- Agents run on a separate tmux server (`tmux -L fleet`) so my own tmux sessions and the dev loop never get mixed up with the fleet.
- Terminal emulator: today it's Ptyxis (GNOME/VTE). If drawing feels slow on the big monitor with 9 busy panes, try a GPU-accelerated terminal (kitty, WezTerm, Ghostty or foot). Nothing in the design depends on which one.

---

## 5b. Smarter wall tiling (R4, R7, R9)

The first wall was dumb in three ways: it evicted by last use alone, so a pane waiting for my answer could be thrown off the wall by a pane I had just opened; the grid shape was a fixed preset, so five panes on a 300-column monitor still drew a 2×2 and hid one; and every pane got the same size whether it was asking me a question or quietly running tests. Three rules fix that. All three are pure geometry plus pane status, so they live in `internal/layout` (shape) and `internal/wall` (which pane, which status).

### 5b.1 Evict by status, not just last use

When the wall is full and another pane has to come on, the pane that leaves is chosen by **class first, last use second**:

| Class | Panes | Evicted |
|---|---|---|
| 0 | pinned (`@fleet_pin` = `1`) | never |
| 1 | `waiting`, `error`, `stuck` | never |
| 2 | `working` | only if no class-3 pane is left |
| 3 | `idle`, `gone`, `unknown`, untagged | first |

Within a class the least recently used pane (`@fleet_used`) goes. A pane that needs me (`waiting`) or has failed (`error`, `stuck`) is never swapped out automatically — those are exactly the panes I opened the wall to see. If every pane on the wall is protected, the new pane is **not** forced on: tmux shows `wall full: every pane is waiting or failing (M-x to free one)` and nothing moves. Pressing `M-x` (explicit hide) always works, whatever the status.

Status comes from the pane itself: `fleet refresh` already tags `@fleet_status` on every transition, and now also `@fleet_status_since` (unix nanoseconds). No board read from the wall code, so state stays in tmux (ADR-1, ADR-4).

Pinning is built: `p` in the overview, the card menu or `fleet wall pin NAME` toggles `@fleet_pin` (§5e).

**Replaced by the target slot (§5e, ADR-31).** The first version reused the pane I was last in (`#{pane_last}`), but only on a full wall, and nothing showed which pane that was. A card now replaces the visible `▶` target pane, full wall or not, and the class order below applies only when there is no target.

### 5b.2 A grid sized to the screen, not to the preset

Presets (`M-1/2/4/6/9`) stop being fixed shapes and become **caps on the number of slots**. The shape is computed from the space actually available — the wall rectangle, not the window. On the reference 300×80 terminal that rectangle is 209×64: 300×79 after the tmux status line, minus the 90-column admiral and its border, minus the 14-row overview and its border.

- A pane should be at least `MinPaneW`×`MinPaneH` = **64×17** cells. 16 usable rows plus the one row `pane-border-status top` takes for the title. 64 columns is what an agent TUI needs before it starts wrapping badly; demanding 80 would cap the reference wall at a 2×2 and make this rule a no-op exactly where it matters.
- Given the wall rectangle and `n` panes, every row count `r` from 1 to `n` is tried. `cols = ceil(n/r)`, and the shape scores `min(cellW/MinPaneW, cellH/MinPaneH)` — not raw area, which ignores that cells are about twice as tall as they are wide and would put five panes in one row of 41 columns. **The score is clamped at 1**: once a cell clears the minimum, extra slack is worth nothing, so a shape that fits is never beaten by a roomier one with worse proportions (without the clamp, 3 panes would tile 1+2 instead of a single row of three). Shapes that divide evenly (`n % r == 0`) get a 10% bonus *after* the clamp, so 6 panes stay 3+3 instead of going ragged. Highest score wins; ties go to fewer rows, because height is the scarce axis.
- A score below 1 means nothing fits the minimum; the best-scoring shape is used anyway. A cramped pane beats a hidden agent — down to the readability floor in §5b.4, which is where that stops being true.
- Panes spread over the rows as evenly as possible with the remainder on the **bottom** rows, and each row fills the full width. New panes join at the end, so the ragged row is where growth happens and the rows I am already reading keep their shape. 5 panes become **2 + 3**, 7 become 2 + 2 + 3, 3 sit in a single row.

`M-1` still means one pane — plus any pane that refused to leave, since a waiting or failing pane is never evicted (§5b.1) and the leftovers auto-grid. `M-m` still means main + stack. `M-9` no longer needs the `Wall9MinCols` special case: a narrow window simply cannot fit three 64-column panes and the grid falls back on its own.

### 5b.3 The pane that needs attention gets the space

Equal tiles are wrong when one agent is asking a question and the rest are grinding. The wall picks one **emphasis pane** and gives it `FocusPercent` = 60% of the wall; the others share the rest in an auto-grid (§5b.2):

- **Off by default** since ADR-31: a pane growing on its own moves every other pane under the pointer. `fleet wall emphasis` turns it on.
- Emphasis pane = the **longest-waiting** pane (`waiting`, then `error`/`stuck`, by `@fleet_status_since`); if none needs attention, no emphasis and the grid is even. Focus never picks it (ADR-31): choosing a slot must not resize it.
- Stacked or side by side is decided by scoring both remainders with the grid rule above, tie to stacked — on a 209×64 wall stacked gives the emphasis pane the full 209 columns and the others better shapes, while side by side leaves one cramped column.
- The emphasis pane takes the first slot. The other panes keep their relative order: reordering them to follow focus would mean `swap-pane`, and every agent TUI would repaint and lose its scroll position.
- Emphasis is skipped when it would push the remaining panes under the minimum pane size, or when there are fewer than two panes. With the default 14-row overview that means emphasis is off above about four wall panes — intended, not a bug.
- **Hysteresis**, because resizing a pane makes an agent TUI reflow: emphasis moves only when its pane leaves the wall or stops needing attention, or when a candidate has waited 10s *and* the current emphasis has held 30s. The choice lives in `@fleet_emphasis_pane` / `@fleet_emphasis_at`, and a re-layout that produces the same layout string as `@fleet_layout` is skipped entirely.
- `fleet wall emphasis` toggles it on and back off (window option `@fleet_emphasis`, on only when `1`). No key binding: the hint bar is already full, and the remaining key budget belongs to pinning.

A pane that is merely `working` therefore shrinks on its own as soon as another pane starts waiting, and grows back when I answer it.

### 5b.4 A readability floor that scales with the screen (ADR-41)

`MinPaneW`×`MinPaneH` (64×17) is the *comfort* minimum the grid scores against; a pane may fall under it, because a cramped pane beats a hidden agent (ADR-15). The **floor** is where that stops being true: below it a pane is wrapped garbage, and the wall shows **fewer panes** rather than tiling smaller.

The floor is not one number, because one number is wrong at both ends: 64 columns would mean a 95-column laptop wall could never hold two panes, and 40 columns is needlessly mean on a 249-column ultrawide. So it is derived from the wall rectangle:

```
floorW = clamp(wallW / FloorDiv, FloorMinW, MinPaneW)   # FloorDiv 4, FloorMinW 40
floorH = clamp(wallH / FloorDiv, FloorMinH, MinPaneH)   # FloorDiv 4, FloorMinH 10
```

A quarter of the wall is the pane size a 2×2 would get, so the floor says "no smaller than a quarter of what you have", never below the absolute bound where text stops being readable (40 columns, 10 rows = 9 usable plus the border-status row), and never above the comfort minimum. The bigger the screen, the higher the floor.

| Terminal | Wall rectangle | Floor | Panes |
|---|---|---|---|
| laptop 160×42 | 95×27 | 40×10 | 4 |
| 1080p 210×52 | 145×37 | 40×10 | 9 |
| 1440p 280×70 | 189×55 | 47×13 | 9 |
| ultrawide 340×80 | 249×65 | 62×16 | 9 |
| half screen 120×35 | 55×20 | 40×10 | 1 |

**The grid prefers shapes that clear it.** §5b.2's search now looks at floor-clearing shapes first and only falls back to the best-scoring shape when none clears, so the grid never renders a cramped shape while a readable one exists. On a 77×21 wall two panes stack rather than sit side by side in 38 columns, even though side by side scores a hair higher.

**Presets step down.** A preset is a cap on slots (§5b.2), and the floor lowers that cap: `M-9` on a laptop puts four panes up and says `preset 9: this window fits 4 readable panes`. The preset itself is never rewritten, so widening the terminal gets all nine back. `M-m` is measured against its own shape, not the grid: its stack column is only 40% of the wall, so a laptop that fits a 2×2 grid fits no main+stack at all and `M-m` means one pane there.

**Adds are refused, with a reason.** `Add` (the card menu, `fleet wall add`) steps the preset up on a full wall; once a bigger preset cannot help, the pane is **not** joined and tmux says `wall full: another pane would be too small to read here (M-x to free one, or grow the window)`. A card going into the `▶` target slot is unaffected: it replaces a pane in place and the pane count does not change, so an explicit choice still beats the floor (ADR-31). The `▶` pane is also never the one shed, so the floor cannot undo the choice on the next apply.

**It recomputes on resize.** Every apply enforces it, and `client-resized` runs one, so shrinking the terminal sheds panes instead of crushing them. Eviction follows the usual class + least-recently-used order (§5b.1), which means **a pinned, waiting or failing pane is never shed**: a wall of nothing but those goes cramped rather than hiding the pane that needs me. That is ADR-14 still winning over the floor.

### Out of scope here

Adaptive overview height, auto-slotting waiting agents onto the wall (§18 #13), keeping hand-dragged pane sizes across a re-layout, and saved named walls (§5a) are all still unbuilt and unchanged by this.

---

## 5c. Teams: group agents and act on a group

A team is a named group of agents with its own colour. It lets me see which agents belong together and act on all of them at once.

### Behaviour

- **One team per agent.** Adding an agent that is already in a team moves it (the reply says from where). One team keeps the colour unambiguous and gives the comms access rule (below) a single answer.
- **State on disk:** `~/.fleet/teams.json`, its own file with its own `teams.lock` (flock + atomic rename, as `board.json`). Shape: `{"teams": {"<name>": {"color": "#rrggbb", "members": ["a1","a2"], "created": "<rfc3339>"}}}`. It is a separate file, not a field in `board.json`, so an older `fleet` binary that is still running (the admiral's `fleet mcp`) cannot drop team data when it rewrites the board. It survives restarts because it is only on disk; pane tags are re-derived from it on every refresh.
- **Names** follow agent-name rules (`[A-Za-z0-9][A-Za-z0-9_.-]{0,31}`). A team may contain names that are not on the board yet (add before spawn); members that are not running are skipped by team actions and reported.
- **Colour:** a new team gets the first colour of a fixed palette that no other team uses (cycling when all are taken): blue, pink, teal, mauve, sky, sapphire (Catppuccin Mocha; hue changes at each step). The palette leaves out the status colours (green, yellow, peach, red, maroon, grey), the selection lavender, and rosewater/flamingo (they read as the waiting pulse on a dark background), so a team mark is never read as a status. `fleet team color <team> <colour>` takes a palette name or `#rrggbb`.
- **Closing an agent** (`fleet close`, `fleet_close`) also removes it from its team. Deleting a team never touches its agents.

### How a team shows

A diamond `◆` in the team colour after the agent name (`●` is already the waiting glyph, so a dot would put two dots on a waiting agent). Status keeps owning tile borders, text colour and the pane border colour, so nothing else changes colour. Agents without a team look exactly as before.

- Overview tile: `○ name ◆ ▣` on the first line (the name is truncated, never the marks); the team name, cut to 10 cells, starts the second line (`alpha · copilot · working 3m`). Chips: `○ name ◆`.
- Wall pane border: `○ name ◆ working`, no team name (narrow panes cut the border from the right, and the status matters more). The format reads pane options `@fleet_team` and `@fleet_team_color`, which refresh keeps in sync with `teams.json` (set only when they differ, unset when the agent leaves).
- `fleet ls`: a `TEAM` column (`◆ alpha`, dot coloured on a terminal) appears only when at least one agent has a team. `fleet ls --json` adds `team` and `team_color` to each agent that has one.
- `fleet_status`: a `team` column after `name` (empty for agents without a team).

### Surface

| CLI | MCP (`fleet_team`, `action` =) | What it does |
|---|---|---|
| `fleet team ls [--json]` | `list` | Teams with colour and members (running members marked) |
| `fleet team create <team> [colour]` | `create` (`team`, opt. `color`) | New empty team; auto colour unless given |
| `fleet team delete <team>` | `delete` | Remove the team and its memberships; agents keep running |
| `fleet team add <team> <agent>...` | `add` (`team`, `agents`) | Add agents (moves them from another team). Creates the team if it does not exist |
| `fleet team rm <team> <agent>...` | `remove` | Take agents out of the team |
| `fleet team color <team> <colour>` | `color` | Change the colour |
| `fleet team send <team> "text"` | `send` (`text`) | Paste into every running member; reports per member |
| `fleet team show <team>` / `hide <team>` | `show` / `hide` | Put running members on the wall / take them off. Show stops at the wall's limit and says which members did not fit |
| `fleet team close <team> [--yes]` | `close` (`confirmed`) | Close every member. CLI asks `y/N` unless `--yes`; MCP needs `confirmed=true` after the user said yes. The team itself stays (empty) |

No new key bindings (the hint bar is full).

### Teams and agent comms

The inter-agent protocol (§6.6, ADR-19) gives agents access to each other through pairwise grants. Teams plug into it as an **implicit two-way grant** (built, ADR-34): under the default `agent_mail = "team"`, two agents in the same team may message each other without a request, and agents in different teams must ask. The team package exposes `team.Store.SameTeam(a, b string) bool` and `Teammates(name) []string` for that access check; teams store no grants and comms stores no teams. Team membership is changed only by the user (CLI) and the admiral (`fleet_team`), never by the agent-scoped MCP server, so an agent cannot join a team to gain access.

### Out of scope

Multiple teams per agent, nested teams, team-level wall layouts, keys for teams, spawning straight into a team, and the comms protocol itself.

---

## 5d. What's new in fleet (built)

One list of fleet's own user-facing features, newest first, so I can see what the fleet can do now.
