It is the same wherever it is opened: it never reads the repo of the pane, the cwd or the agents' dirs. (The first version showed the git history of the pane's repo; that was not what I wanted, ADR-30.)

### Behaviour

- **Source:** `CHANGELOG.md` at the repo root, one line per user-facing change under a `## YYYY-MM-DD` heading, newest day first and newest line first within a day. A line may end with refs in brackets: short commit hashes and `ADR-n`, e.g. `(66a2ece, ADR-27)`. The file is built into the binary with `go:embed`, so an installed `fleet` always knows its own features.
- **Fallback:** when fleet can find its own source repo (the binary's resolved path is `<repo>/bin/fleet` and `<repo>/go.mod` names the fleet module), it adds commits on that repo's local `main` whose subject starts with `feat` (`feat:`, `feat(x):`, `feat!:`) and that the changelog does not cover: not cited by hash, not touching `CHANGELOG.md`, and not older than the newest changelog day. They show dimmed, with the hash, after the curated lines of the same day. Read-only git with `GIT_OPTIONAL_LOCKS=0`; no repo, no git or an error means no fallback, silently (logged).
- **Version:** the first line is `fleet <commit> · built <YYYY-MM-DD HH:MM> · N new` in Overlay1, from the Go build info (`vcs.revision`, `vcs.time`; `+dirty` when `vcs.modified`), or `fleet (unknown build)`. `· N new` is left out when nothing is new.
- **New:** an entry is new when its id is not in `~/.fleet/whatsnew-seen` (one id per line). The id is the first cited hash, else a hash of date and text, so editing a line's text marks it new again. Opening the popup shows the new marks, then records every listed id; the next opening shows none until an entry is added. On first use every entry is new. The CLI prints the marks but never records them.
- **Rows:** date (`YYYY-MM-DD`, Overlay1, on the first row of each day only; one blank line between days), a `✦` in Text bold for new (not Peach `●`, which means waiting on the wall), then the text wrapped at most 100 cells wide, continuation lines indented under it. Markdown backticks are stripped and code spans drawn in bold Text. Refs follow in Overlay0 as one unbreakable `(…)` token, on their own continuation line when they don't fit. Fallback rows draw their subject in Overlay1. No selection: the view scrolls by line, and a dim `↓ more` on the bottom line says there is more.
- **States:** an empty changelog with no fallback shows `no entries`.

### Surface

- `Alt-g` anywhere in the fleet server, `g` in the overview, or a click on `Alt-g what's new` in the tmux status line opens the popup (`fleet changes --popup`, 90%×85%, titled ` what's new in fleet · q closes `). All three use one tmux command. In the popup: `↑↓`/`jk`, `PgUp`/`PgDn`, `Home`/`End` and the wheel scroll; `q`/`Esc`/`g`/`Alt-g` close it.
- `Alt-g what's new` is the last tmux hint before `Alt-? all keys`, so it is the first dropped on a narrow status line. It is a `#[range=user|changes]` range; a `MouseDown1Status` binding opens the popup on that range and runs tmux's default (`switch-client -t =`) anywhere else.
- `fleet changes [--json]` prints the same list in a terminal: the version line, then one line per entry (`date  ✦  text  (refs)`, backticks stripped).
- `fleet version` (or `--version`) prints the release tag the binary was linked with, then the commit and build time: `fleet v0.4.0 (1ede0f4 · built …)`. A `go install …@vX.Y.Z` binary uses its module version; a build with neither prints the commit line alone (ADR-73).
- `install.sh` installs or upgrades the release binary in one line, `curl -fsSL https://raw.githubusercontent.com/sturlabragason/fleet/main/install.sh | sh`, after checking it against `SHA256SUMS` (ADR-78) and, when `gh` is installed and logged in, against its build attestation (ADR-103). Anyone can check a release asset with `gh attestation verify <file> -R sturlabragason/fleet`.
- Keeping it current is part of done: a user-facing feature or fix adds its changelog line in the same commit (worker playbook, `.fleet/worker.md`, AGENTS.md).

### Out of scope

Reviewing the agents' commits and diffs (use git or the agent's pane), release tags and version numbers, an MCP tool, filtering and search.

---

## 5e. Clicking around: a target slot on the wall (ADR-31)

Status: the target slot, `a`, `Tab`/`Alt-n`, `Esc`, the right-click menu, `Space` preview, `u` undo, `p` pin and the overview tiers are built.

The problem and the options weighed are in `docs/ux-interaction.md`. In short: "click a pane, then click a card" replaced the pane only on a full wall, nothing showed which pane a card would replace, clicking a pane resized it, and cards moved under the mouse.

### Target slot

- The wall has one **target slot**, the window option `@fleet_target` (a pane id). Focusing a wall pane by any means (click, `Alt-←↑↓→`, a card bringing it on) makes it the target. The `pane-focus-in` hook sets it with an inline tmux command, so no process runs per click. Focusing the overview or the admiral does **not** clear it.
- **A card click or `Enter` puts that agent into the target slot**, full wall or not, with `swap-pane` so position and size are kept. The target stays on that slot (now the incoming pane), so clicking cards one after another cycles agents through one slot. A pinned target is never replaced.
- A card whose agent is already on the wall makes its pane the target and focuses it; nothing moves.
- **No target** (fresh start, `Esc`, the target pane left the wall): today's rules (§5b.1), append if there is room, else evict by class. The pane that would leave carries a dim `next out` in its title.
- The target pane's border title starts with `▶` in the select colour, whichever pane has focus. Its card shows `▶` after the name. That colour says "target" and nothing else (ADR-37): the overview cursor is an underline in the agent's status colour, the active pane is the bright neutral, unfocused panes dim one step, and `· next out` is the dimmest of all.
- **Undo:** `u` in the overview swaps the last replace back (one level, window option `@fleet_undo` = `<slot pane> <pane that left>`).
- **Add without replacing:** `a` or `+` in the overview appends the cursor agent as a new slot. On a full wall the preset steps up (1 → 2 → 4 → 6 → 9, `m` → 6) because you asked for one more pane; at 9 it refuses with a message.
- **Next waiting:** `Tab` / `Shift+Tab` in the overview, and `Alt-n` anywhere, put the next/previous waiting agent (longest wait first) into the target slot. Repeated presses work through approvals in one slot.
- **Emphasis is off by default and follows attention only** (amends ADR-16): focusing a pane no longer gives it 60%, so choosing a slot never resizes it. `Alt-z` is the way to give one pane the space.
- **Focus stays in the overview.** A card click, `Enter`, `a`, `Tab` and `Alt-n` never move keyboard focus; a swap into the target keeps focus where it was. So clicking cards or pressing `Tab` repeatedly keeps working.
- `fleet up` unbinds tmux's `M-MouseDrag1Pane`, `M-MouseDrag1Border` and `C-MouseDrag1Pane`, which move panes out of the wall order or create untracked panes.

### Right-click and preview

- **Right-click a card or chip:** a tmux menu at the pointer (opened on release, with mouse on) titled with the agent: `Put in ▶ pane` (or `Add to wall` with no ▶ pane or a pinned one), `Add as a new pane`, `Preview`, then `Pin`/`Unpin` and `Take off the wall` (only when on the wall). Items that don't apply are hidden. Menu items run `fleet wall --notify --keep-focus ...`, which shows the result with `display-message` and never moves focus.
- **`Space`** in the overview, or Preview in the menu, opens a read-only live view of the agent in a popup (`fleet peek --live NAME`, captured 4× a second). `Enter` in the popup puts the agent into the target slot and closes it; `q`/`Esc`/`Space` close it. The popup title says what `⏎` does.
- **Pin** finally gets its binding: `p` in the overview (and the menu) toggles `@fleet_pin` on the cursor agent's pane (§5b.1 class 0). A pinned agent shows `⚑` (one cell, muted) after its name on the pane border, the card and the chip. A pinned `▶` is never replaced: a card click places the agent by the old rules and says `<name> is pinned · p unpins`, with the cursor left on the pinned agent.
- **Undo:** `u` in the overview, or `fleet wall undo`, puts the replaced agent back into its slot (`@fleet_undo` holds `<in> <out> <out name>`); `u` again redoes. Replace messages end with `· u undo`.
- **Minimize all:** `Alt-X`, `fleet wall hide-all` or `fleet_wall` `hide_all` takes every agent pane off the wall at once, pinned ones too; the admiral and overview stay and the agents keep running. `Alt-X` on an empty wall, `fleet wall restore` or `fleet_wall` `restore` puts those panes back in their old order (ADR-67). Both move every pane in one tmux call, so each pane is resized once (ADR-88).

### Visible keys

Every key above is in the key registry (`internal/keys`), so it shows in `?`, `Alt-?` and `fleet keys`. The overview header names the target and the next action: `▶ orch-auth · click card replace · a add · Space preview · Tab next waiting · Esc clear · ? all keys`, or `no ▶ pane · click a wall pane to pick one · ⏎ add/evict · ? all keys` without one. Items drop from the right on a narrow pane; the target and `? all keys` stay. The `?` overlay scrolls (`↑↓`, wheel) when the pane is too short for every key.

### Overview past one screen of cards (ADR-32)

- The strip height stays fixed (§5a; wall geometry is worth more than overview rows).
- Agents that need you or are on the wall (waiting, error, stuck, on the wall) get **full cards**. Everyone else gets a **one-line row** (glyph, name, team mark, status age) in columns of at least 30 cells. A click on a row does the same as on a card.
- In tier mode there is at most one row of cards, in order waiting (longest first), error, stuck, on the wall; the rest of the strip is a row area of one-line rows (glyph, name, `⚑ ▶ ◆` marks, age) in columns of at least 30 cells. Urgent agents that don't fit as cards become the first rows. Arrow keys move one cell or one line; the selected row is drawn reversed.
- If the rows still overflow, idle and gone agents fold into one clickable `+N idle` chip that unfolds them in place (`− fold idle` folds again), and the wheel scrolls the row area, with `↑ N more` / `↓ N more` on the edge lines. Nothing is ever unreachable.
- **Stable order:** within a tier, agents keep their position. Re-sorting waits while the mouse was over the overview or a key was pressed in the last 3 seconds, so nothing moves under the pointer. Within a tier, team members sit together.
- With every agent fitting as a full card (the common case), the overview looks as before.

### Out of scope

Drag a card onto a pane (tmux sends the release to the pane the drag started in), Shift+click and Shift+Enter (the terminal keeps Shift+click for selection; Bubble Tea v1 cannot see Shift+Enter), an interactive preview, collapsing teams, a taller overview size.

## 5f. Web view: `fleet web` (ADR-66, alpha)

A browser front end beside the TUI and MCP, for steering from another device (a phone through a login gateway). Package `internal/web`, stdlib only, page embedded in the binary.

- **Run:** `fleet web [--listen ADDR] [--allow-host NAME,...]`, in the foreground. Default `127.0.0.1:7777` (`$FLEET_WEB_LISTEN`). Refused inside an agent pane: it acts as the user.
- **Tiles:** every board agent with status colour, profile, last line and latest report, pushed over SSE when `board.json` changes. It only reads the board; the watcher stays the one refresher (ADR-49), and a stale heartbeat shows a banner.
- **Peek drawer:** the agent's pane in colour (`Fleet.PeekColor`, the last 200 lines; the web package never calls tmux itself, so other backends can serve it later) turned into HTML, refreshed once a second while open. Never attaches a tmux client, so window sizes don't change.
- **Input:** a numbered prompt shows as buttons that call `Choose` with the question as `expect` (a changed prompt is refused); a send box (`Send`, paste plus Enter); buttons for allowlisted keys. Send and keys are refused while `SendBlocker` says the user is typing there; there is no force.
- **Auth:** a token from `$FLEET_WEB_TOKEN`, else `~/.fleet/web.json` (0600, made on first run, same across restarts; delete it to rotate). The printed URL `/?token=` trades it for an `HttpOnly`, `SameSite=Strict` cookie; a proxy can send `Authorization: Bearer <token>` instead. Every request's `Host` and every POST's `Origin` must be loopback, the listen host or a `--allow-host` name (`$FLEET_WEB_HOSTS`); POSTs must be JSON.
- **Behind a gateway:** listen on the LAN (`--listen 0.0.0.0:7777` or the laptop's address), let the proxy inject the bearer header, and allow the gateway's host name. The token check stays on behind the gateway, because the page can type into agents that run with every permission.

Out of scope for the alpha: spawn, close, park and wake, teams, the web wall, mail, starting from `fleet up`, TLS.

---

## 6. The admiral without context overload

The admiral is a normal Copilot session in the bottom pane. Its `AGENTS.md` tells it how to use the `fleet` CLI and the rules below.

### 6.1 State on disk

The admiral reads `board.json`, agent cards and `decisions.log`. Anything it learns that matters goes into a card or the log before it moves on. Its conversation is a scratchpad.

### 6.2 Summaries, not raw panes

`fleet peek --summary <name>` and MCP `fleet_peek(name, summary=true)` run the pane, the agent's last message and its board status through a cheap model (Haiku 5.5, ADR-40). They return about 5 lines: what the agent is doing, what it waits for, any error. The admiral is not allowed `--raw`.

Cheap by construction: a summary is made only when it is asked for, never on a poll loop; the answer is cached in `peek-cache/<name>.txt` under a hash of screen + last message + status, so an unchanged agent costs nothing and a state change makes a new one; one agent can trigger at most one model call per `min_interval` (default 30s), and inside it the last summary comes back marked stale; only the last 6000 characters of the pane are sent. Every failure is open — no model, no CLI, a timeout or an error gives no summary and an explanation, and nothing else in fleet waits for it. `[summary]` in `config.toml` sets the model, the CLI, the interval and the timeout, or turns the feature off.

### 6.3 One-key reset (`R`)

Kill the admiral process and start a fresh one with a boot prompt:
"Read `~/.fleet/board.json` and the last 50 lines of `decisions.log`. Report what needs me, then wait." This loses nothing because state lives on disk.

Automatic version: when the admiral's context goes red, it writes anything pending to the board and resets itself. Built as described in §6.5.

### 6.4 Focus sessions (`f`)

For a hard problem in one agent, open a short-lived admiral that loads only that agent's card and raw pane. It can go deep, then updates the card and exits. The main admiral only sees the updated card.

### 6.5 Admiral memory: notes, handoff, context meter (R11, §18 #14)

The admiral's chat fills up over a long day. Fleet does not compact it (ADR-4). The admiral saves what it must not forget to disk as it goes. When its context gets full it writes a handoff and fleet replaces it with a fresh session that starts from the handoff and the board. Agents aren't affected: they live on the board and in tmux, not in the admiral's chat.

**On disk** (`~/.fleet/`)

| Path | What |
|---|---|
| `decisions.log` | Append-only notes, one line each: `2026-11-04T21:40 [agent] text`. Decisions, user preferences, promises ("tell me when web-nav is done"). |
| `admiral/session.json` | Current admiral: `session_id`, `profile`, `pane_id`, `started`, `generation`, `previous` (last session ID). |
| `admiral/handoff.md` | Latest handoff written by the outgoing admiral (max 4 KB is shown to the next one). Earlier ones are moved to `admiral/handoffs/<timestamp>.md`; the last 20 are kept. |

**Context meter.** For a Copilot admiral, fill = `inputTokens / maxPromptTokens` of the last `session.usage_record` in `~/.copilot/session-state/<session_id>/events.jsonl`. Only the end of the file is read. The session ID comes from `session.json`, or from `--session-id <uuid>` in the admiral pane's start command if the file is missing (admirals started before this feature). No ID, a non-Copilot admiral or no usage yet means the fill is unknown, and nothing is shown or warned.

- Admiral pane border: `admiral · ctx 34%`, green < 40%, yellow 40–69%, red ≥ 70% (the handoff threshold; same bands as §5). The overview's refresh loop sets the `@fleet_ctx` pane option when the value changes.
- `fleet admiral` prints one line: short session ID, fill (`ctx 34% (68k/200k)`), generation, uptime. `--json` gives the same fields.

**MCP tools** (admiral)

- `fleet_note(text, agent?, pin?)` appends a line to `decisions.log`. `pin=true` (`fleet note --pin`) also appends it to `~/.fleet/pinned.log`, for standing user rules; delete the line there to unpin (ADR-62).
- `fleet_brief()` returns: the handoff (if any, with its time), every pinned note, the last 30 other lines of `decisions.log`, then the same table as `fleet_status`. A fresh admiral calls this first.
- `fleet_handoff(summary)` writes `admiral/handoff.md` (rotating the old one) and asks tmux to run `fleet admiral reset --handoff` in the background after about 2 seconds. It returns at once, because the reset kills the admiral and the MCP server with it. The summary covers things the board doesn't hold: open questions to the user, what it promised to watch, plans in flight. It doesn't restate agent statuses.
- When the fill is at or above the threshold (default 70, env `FLEET_ADMIRAL_HANDOFF_PCT`; the deprecated `FLEET_SUP_HANDOFF_PCT` is read when it is unset), or the context holds at least 80k tokens whatever the percent (env `FLEET_ADMIRAL_HANDOFF_TOKENS`, 0 turns it off; ADR-93), every fleet tool result ends with one line: `[admiral context 74%, 148k tokens: save anything pending with fleet_note, then call fleet_handoff]`. The pane border turns red at the same point.

**Reset** (`fleet admiral reset [--handoff]`, tmux `M-R`, overview `R`)

1. Find the `@role=sup` pane in `fleet:main`. If there is none, fail with "no admiral pane; run fleet up".
2. If no handoff is newer than the current session's start (a manual reset), write a stub handoff: "Manual reset at <time>; previous admiral wrote no handoff. Previous session: <id>."
3. Build the same command as `fleet up` with a new session ID, plus for Copilot `-i "<boot prompt>"`. Boot prompt: "You are taking over as fleet admiral. Call fleet_brief, tell the user in 1–3 lines what needs them, then wait."
4. `respawn-pane -k` the admiral pane with that command, then update `session.json` (generation + 1, `previous` = old ID). The old Copilot session isn't deleted; its ID is in the handoff, so `copilot --resume <id>` still opens it.

`M-R` and overview `R` go through tmux `confirm-before` ("reset admiral? (y/n)"), because a reset by mistake loses the chat. `--handoff` (called from `fleet_handoff`) doesn't ask.

**Out of scope:** Copilot `/compact` or any in-place summarising; context meters for non-Copilot admirals; deleting old admiral sessions; agent cards (`agents/<name>.md`); focus sessions (§6.4).


### 6.6 Agents talking to each other: free within a team, on request across (ADR-19, ADR-34)

**Built 2026-11-04.** Code in `internal/mail` (store), `internal/fleet/mail.go` (identity, nudge), `internal/mcp/agent.go` (agent mode), `cmd/fleet/mail.go` (CLI).

Agents can message each other without the admiral. Teammates, and agents in one repo that have no team, may do so freely; every other pair needs a grant first (ADR-34).

**Store.** `~/.fleet/mail/`, guarded by one `flock` (`mail.lock`), every rewrite atomic (temp + rename), like `board.json`. Survives restarts; needs no admiral, overview or MCP process running.

| Path | What |
|---|---|
| `mail/grants.json` | One entry per unordered pair: `a`, `b`, `state` (`pending`, `granted`, `denied`), `requested_by`, `reason`, `decided_by` (agent name or `user`), times |
| `mail/<agent>/inbox.jsonl` | Append-only messages to that agent: `id`, `from`, `to` (`*` for broadcast), `kind` (`msg`, `request`, `grant`, `deny`, `revoke`), `text`, `t` |
| `mail/<agent>/read` | ID of the last message the agent has read |

**Policy (ADR-34).** `[defaults] agent_mail` in `config.toml` (fleet-wide) decides which pairs have *implicit* access, needing no request:
- `"team"` (default): agents in the same team (§5c); and agents in the same repo, or in worktrees of it (same `git rev-parse --git-common-dir` of their board dirs), when neither has a team. A team member and a teamless agent, or members of two teams, ask, except that a squad leader (`leader` on the board) and any agent in the same repo reach each other freely (`free (lead)`): the lead takes a hand-off from outside its team without the user relaying it, and its members stay private (fleet#35). A refusal to a member of such a team names the lead when the sender may reach it: "no access to X: request it with fleet_access_request (to X), or message L (lead of X's team), who you may reach freely".
- `"open"`: every pair on the board.
- `"ask"`: none; every pair asks (the behaviour before ADR-34).

An explicit pair always wins: `granted` allows, `denied` blocks. Revoking a pair that has implicit access stores it as `denied` (decided by the revoker), so the revoke sticks; a new request and grant reopens it. `fleet_agents` / `fleet mail agents` show implicit access as `free (squad)`, `free (team)`, `free (repo)`, `free (lead)` or `free (open)`. A request for a pair that is already free returns `granted` without notifying anyone. An unknown value fails config loading like any other bad key (fleet then logs it and runs with the default). The setting is reread on every access check, so running agents see a change at once.

**Access.** A grant is two-way: once A and B are `granted`, either can message the other.
- *Request*: A asks for access to B with a reason. The pair becomes `pending`, and B receives a `request` message. If B already has a pending request towards A, the new request grants the pair.
- *Grant / deny*: only the target (B) or the user can decide. The user decides with `fleet access grant|deny A B`, or through the admiral (`fleet_access_decide`, which requires `confirmed=true` after an explicit yes). The requester can never grant itself. A denied pair can be asked again.
- *Revoke*: either side of the pair, or the user, at any time; for implicit access it leaves a `denied` pair (see Policy). `fleet close` drops every pair of the closed agent and keeps its mailbox.
- Both agents must be on the board when access is requested.

**Messages.** Text capped at 8 KB. `to="*"` broadcasts to every agent the sender may message (granted or implicit), not every agent. Sending without access fails with "no access to B: request it with fleet_access_request". Every message is logged (slog, component `mail`) and stays in the recipient's inbox; the admiral reads the traffic with `fleet_access limit=N` and the user with `fleet access log [-n N]` (last N messages across all inboxes, a broadcast once). The recipient's tools show messages as untrusted text from another agent: an agent reads them as information, never as orders from the user.

**Identity.** The caller is resolved from the tmux pane it runs in: `$TMUX_PANE` and that pane's `@fleet_agent` tag on the `fleet` socket. `fleet mcp --agent NAME` and `fleet mail --as NAME` override it only outside an agent pane, or when they name the pane's own agent. `fleet access grant|deny|revoke` refuses to run inside an agent pane. `user` is a reserved agent name. With neither, agent tools refuse ("not a fleet agent"). This controls cooperation, not security: any agent with a shell can edit `~/.fleet`.

**Surfaces.**
- MCP, agent mode: `fleet mcp --agent NAME` (or run inside an agent pane) exposes only `fleet_agents` (name, profile, status, dir, access state with me), `fleet_access_request`, `fleet_access_respond` (grant/deny a request to me), `fleet_access_revoke`, `fleet_msg_send` (`to` = name or `*`; `needs_action`, ADR-92) and `fleet_inbox` (unread by default and marks them read; `all=true` shows the last N without marking anything read) and `fleet_instructions` (ADR-102). Every agent-mode result ends with "(N unread)" when N > 0.
- MCP, admiral: `fleet_access` shows the policy, then pairs and pending requests (`limit=N` adds the last N messages); `fleet_access_decide` grants, denies or revokes any pair (`confirmed=true`).
- CLI, for agents without MCP (agy, vibe, already-running agents): `fleet mail agents | inbox [--all] [-n N] | send [--fyi|--act] <to|*> "text" | request <to> ["reason"] | grant <who> | deny <who> | revoke <who>` with `--as NAME`. For the user: `fleet access [ls] | log [-n N] | grant A B | deny A B | revoke A B`.

**Wiring.** Profiles get a data field `peer_args`, inserted straight after the command, with `{peer_mcp}` expanded to a one-server JSON config (`fleet-peer` → `{fleet} mcp --agent {name}`). Copilot profiles use `--additional-mcp-config {peer_mcp}`; Claude profiles use `--mcp-config {peer_mcp_claude}` (Claude's server schema has no `tools` key). Spawn and adopt both add it. A user profile whose `command` is a built-in tool (`claude`, `copilot`) and that sets no `peer_args` or `hook_args` takes the built-in's, so a `[claude-work]` with its own `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` still gets `fleet_report` and the status hooks; `peer_args = []` opts out (ADR-80). agy, vibe and fake have none and use the CLI. Agents that are already running keep their tools until they are respawned. They can use `fleet mail` in the meantime. A worker does not load the user's global MCP servers: a profile's `user_mcp_config` names the tool's file of them and `user_mcp_off` the args that switch them off (Copilot: `--disable-mcp-server <name>`; Claude: `--disallowedTools=mcp__<name>`, one per server), on spawn, wake and adopt and on both drivers. `keep_mcp = ["name"]` in config.toml keeps one (ADR-91). Copilot profiles with `plugin_mcp = true` carry fleet-peer in the fleet plugin's `.mcp.json` instead of `--additional-mcp-config`; `peer_args` is the fallback when the plugin can't be written (ADR-99).

**Nudge.** After a message or request is written, the sender's process types one line into the recipient's pane, but only when its board status is `idle`, never `waiting`, `working` or anything else: `[fleet] 1 new message from A. Read it with fleet_inbox (or: fleet mail inbox)`. A recipient is nudged once per unread batch. If it isn't idle, it sees the unread count on its next fleet tool call. Only mail that needs action nudges (ADR-92): a message to one agent by default, an access request, or anything sent with `needs_action=true` (`--act`). FYI mail (a `*` broadcast by default, or `needs_action=false`, `--fyi`) is stored with `fyi: true` and never nudges: idle, done and parked agents see it on their next turn through "(N unread)", and `fleet_inbox` tags it `msg fyi`. `fleet_info` has a `mail` line ("2 unread (2 FYI, queued for its next turn)") and `fleet_status` adds "(N FYI queued)" to an agent that isn't working. Admiral boxes are woken as before.

**Teams.** Same-team membership is an implicit two-way grant (see Policy). `mail.Store` has an `Implicit(a, b)` hook and knows nothing about teams; `internal/fleet/mail.go` fills it from the policy, `team.Store` and the board.

**Out of scope:** threads and replies-to, attachments, cross-machine mail, message expiry, read receipts, structured task handoff (that stays with task cards, §18 #5–7), approval through the TUI.


### 6.7 Agents report back to the admiral (ADR-20)

**Built 2026-11-04.** Code in `internal/mail/report.go`, `internal/fleet/report.go` (wake, auto-close), `internal/mcp/reports.go`, tile line in `internal/ui/view.go`.

An agent tells the admiral it is done, blocked, has a question, or has made progress. It does this with a tool call instead of printing a marker such as `FLEET-DONE:` and hoping someone reads the screen.

A Claude or Copilot agent that holds a task and tries to end its turn without a done, blocked or question report is sent back once with "Call fleet_report (done, blocked or question) before stopping." by its Stop/agentStop hook (ADR-105); the no-report flag of §7c catches the rest.

**Surface.**
- Agent MCP (agent mode, §6.6): `fleet_report(state, summary)`. `state` is `done`, `blocked`, `question` or `progress`. `summary` starts with a one-line headline and may carry detail on later lines. The full text is stored (control characters dropped, at most 8000 characters) and is what `fleet_reports` returns, also after the agent is closed or auto-closed. The board, the tile, the `report` column and the wake line use the headline: newlines folded, at most 200 characters (100 in the wake line).
- CLI for agents without MCP: `fleet report [--as NAME] <state> "summary"`. Identity rules are the same as `fleet mail`. It files nothing and prints its usage when the text is empty or whitespace or begins with `-` (a stray `--help` is not a question, #5), and `-h`/`--help` anywhere prints the usage; `fleet_report` refuses an empty or whitespace summary with an error. Both use `mail.CheckReport`.
- CLI for reading reports: `fleet reports [--all] [--limit N] [--as NAME]`, a thin wrapper over the same mailbox read as `fleet_reports`, so a worker or the user in a shell sees the history too. Reports only ever land in the admiral's box, so that is the one box it reads. Only the admiral marks them read — a shell in no agent pane and without `--as`; an agent pane, or `--as NAME`, peeks instead, so reading the history never empties the admiral's unread queue. `--all` shows the last N (default 20, `-n` is an alias) and marks nothing. Identity rules are otherwise the same as `fleet mail`.
- Admiral MCP: `fleet_reports` returns unread reports, oldest first, in full, and marks them read. `name=<agent>` returns only that agent's and marks only those read: ids read out of order sit in `read.ids` beside the read mark until the mark passes them, so the other agents' reports stay unread. `all=true` returns the last N without marking anything read, one headline each (time, agent, first line, at most 120 characters) and a hint to pass `name`; `all=true name=<agent>` returns that agent's last N in full. The result ends with the task cards of the agents whose reports it shows, none when it shows none (issue #13). `fleet_squad_reports` takes `name` too. `fleet_status` gains a `report` column with the latest state, its age and summary (`question (answered), 8h ago: …` once the agent works again after a question or blocked report, ADR-90), and `fleet_brief` lists unread reports. Every admiral tool result ends with "(N unread reports)" when N > 0.
- Waiting (ADR-72): `fleet_wait(agents?, timeout_s?)` on the admiral, leader and agent MCP blocks until a message the caller's mailbox did not hold when the call began arrives (from one of `agents`, if given), or the timeout passes (default 300 s, max 600 s; 170 s when the client sent no `progressToken`). While it waits it stats the inbox file every second and sends `notifications/progress` every 30 s, which keeps Copilot CLI's 180 s call timeout from firing. It returns one headline per new message ("- a done: …") and where to read the full text, or "timeout after Ns, nothing new". It marks nothing read. `fleet wait` is the same for a shell: exit 0 on news, 2 on timeout.

**Storage.** Reuses the mailbox (§6.6). The admiral is a well-known recipient called `admiral`, a reserved agent name like `user` (the old name `supervisor` is reserved too and maps to the same box). Each report is a message of kind `report` in `mail/admiral/inbox.jsonl` (`mail/supervisor` is a symlink to it), with the state at the start of its text. No grant is needed to report. The latest report is also stored on the board entry (`report: {state, summary, at}`), so it survives restarts and the card can show it.

**Card.** While an agent is not `working`, line 3 of its tile shows the latest report (`✓ done: …`, `✗ blocked: …`, `? question: …`, `… progress: …`) instead of the last screen line, coloured green, red, yellow or dim, with `(answered)` after the state once a turn started after a question or blocked report. An idle agent whose newest report is an open question is drawn as waiting (colour, glyph, tier, wall border) and `fleet_status` calls it `waiting (question)` until its next turn starts (ADR-90). While the agent is working, the tile shows the screen line as before. A new report replaces the old one.

**Wake the admiral.** Every report is on disk and, while unread, in two always-visible places: the tmux status line (the resident poll sets the global user option `@fleet_reports`, "✉ 3 reports: a, b", and `fleet up`'s `status-left` shows it; while a watcher is live only the watcher writes it, ADR-75) and the overview header, which carries the same count as a badge ("✉ 3 reports", one existing colour role, the waiting one). Both clear once the admiral has read its reports. For `done`, `blocked` and `question` (never `progress`), fleet also types one wake line into the admiral pane (`fleet:main`, `@role sup`): `[fleet] report from A: done: <headline>. Full text: call fleet_reports.`, or for several queued reports one coalesced line `[fleet] 3 new reports: a (done), b (blocked). Full text: call fleet_reports.` Each wake is appended to `mail/admiral/wake-pending` (mail id, agent, state, line); entries whose report has been read are dropped. How often an entry has been typed without being submitted is counted in `wake-pending-tries` beside it, not in the line, so a fleet binary from before ADR-51 still reads the queue correctly during a rollout. The reporting process and then the resident poll, which is the watcher (§7c) while one is live and the overview only when none is, type the line only when all of these hold (ADR-36): the reports not yet announced have settled, meaning the newest of them is at least 30 s old or the oldest at least 90 s old (by the reports' own mailbox times), so a burst of reports costs the admiral one turn, not one each (ADR-93); the admiral pane looks idle by its profile's status patterns; its input box is on screen (the `input` pattern, §4) and empty; no client of the fleet session pressed a key in the last 3 s (`#{client_activity}`); and no wake has yet announced these reports — the mark is the newest message id a wake covered, not the read mark, so a report that lands after a wake is announced even while the earlier ones sit unread (ADR-51). A profile without an `input` pattern is never typed into; the badge is its only signal. The line is pasted without Enter. Fleet then reads the box back for up to 1.5 s and presses Enter only when it holds exactly the wake line (whitespace ignored, so a wrapped line still matches), 150 ms after the paste at the earliest, because Copilot can drop an Enter that comes right after one. It then checks that the box let go of the line, and presses Enter once more if it did not. If Enter fails or the line still sits there, fleet deletes the line it typed, so the box reads empty again and the next poll can retry (ADR-80). Only a box read back without the line counts as submitted; a failed capture does not. While it types, the line is recorded in `wake-pending-typed` with the claim and queue entries it took. A box that later holds exactly that line (the typing process died before its Enter) is fleet's own text, so the next poll submits it instead of waiting for an empty box that never comes. When Enter will not take, or the box shows only the line's start, fleet deletes it and puts the entries back on the queue as for any undelivered wake. Otherwise it presses no Enter and takes back its own text with Backspace (the box was empty just before): when the user typed after the line, it deletes both and types the user's characters again; when the box ends with the line, or shows only its start, it deletes the line's length. Anything else is left alone and logged. Those reports are not typed again (the badge still shows them); anything that stopped the line from being submitted — a failed paste, a guard that found something else in the box, a claim that could not be taken — puts the entries back on the queue, winds the claim back and is logged, so the next poll tries again and the report is never silently dropped (ADR-51). Three typed attempts is the limit: after that fleet logs an error naming the report and stops typing it, because a box it can never read back would otherwise be typed into on every poll. Only one process types at a time, across processes: a `LOCK_NB` flock on `wake-pending.lock` is held from taking the queue to pressing Enter. The queue file is only ever moved aside under a unique name, never deleted in place, so a report appended meanwhile is kept. Entries are removed for good only when the admiral has read their reports. The admiral also sees the unread count on its next tool call.

**Auto-close (opt-in).** Local preference `auto_close_done` (`[defaults]` or `[profile.x]` in `config.toml`, default `false`). When it is true and an agent reports `done`, fleet closes the agent unless the agent itself left work behind (ADR-29):
- A dir that git says is not a work tree, or that no longer exists, closes (a read-only review in a plain folder has nothing to lose). A git error or timeout (3 s) blocks ("git state unreadable").
- At spawn (and queue drain, not adopt) fleet records a baseline on the board entry (`baseline: {head, dirty, worktrees}`): `HEAD`, every path in `git status --porcelain --untracked-files=all` with a fingerprint of its content (FNV-64; size and mtime for non-regular or >64 MB files), and `git worktree list`. Over 2000 dirty paths records no baseline.
- With a baseline, the close is refused only for the agent's own work: a path that is dirty now and was clean, or whose fingerprint changed, since spawn ("uncommitted changes by the agent: a, b (+N more)"); a linked worktree that did not exist at spawn, is named after the agent (the playbook's `../<repo>.wt/<name>`) and is dirty or unmerged ("worktree left behind (uncommitted changes): <path>"; other new worktrees belong to sibling agents and are ignored); or a `HEAD` that moved and is not an ancestor of the local `main`/`master` ("not merged into main"). Dirt that predates the agent and an unchanged `HEAD` (even on an unmerged branch) don't block. The agent's own task-card result file (`card.result`, untracked or modified) never counts as uncommitted work, here, in the strict rule without a baseline, in the close note and in `fleet_info`'s dirty count (#11).
- Without a baseline (adopted agents, agents spawned by an older fleet) the strict rule stands: a clean `git status --porcelain` and `HEAD` an ancestor of `main` (or `master`).

- An agent the user is talking to is never closed, before any git check (ADR-61): flagged `interactive`, its pane open in a tmux client, or a key press in it within 10 min ("not auto-closed: interactive").

A refusal keeps the report and appends the reason to the admiral line ("not auto-closed: uncommitted changes by the agent: g"). The close runs through `tmux run-shell -b` after 2 s, so the agent's own MCP call returns first. It never removes the worktree or the branch.

*Idle sweep.* `auto_close_idle` (a duration such as `"15m"`, default off, needs `auto_close_done`): the overview TUI's poll closes an agent whose latest report is `done`, that has been idle at least that long, and whose idle spell began within 5 minutes of the report (one that went back to work after reporting is left alone), once the same check passes. Each agent's git state is checked at most once a minute, in the background. This catches a branch the admiral merged after the report, and a close that failed at report time.

*Dead panes (ADR-33).* Agent panes keep `remain-on-exit on`, so an agent whose process ends (the user typed `/exit`, or it crashed) stays on the board as `error / process exited` until it is closed. The overview TUI's poll (the same one that drains the queue and runs the idle sweep) handles such panes. It reads the exit from tmux, not from the screen: `#{pane_dead_status}` (exit code) and `#{pane_dead_signal}` (signal number) in the pane listing. The tool runs as `bash -lc 'exec …'`, so the code is the tool's own.
- Clean exit (status 0, no signal): close the agent right away, as `fleet close` does (board, overview and wall). The admiral gets a `done` report "exited, closed" and the wake line `[fleet] <name> exited, closed.`, no question.
- Crash (non-zero status, a signal, or tmux too old to say): the agent stays `error`. The admiral gets a `blocked` report with the exit code or signal and the last few screen lines, and the wake line `[fleet] <name> crashed (exit 3): <last line>. Closing in 5m. Full text: call fleet_reports.` The agent is closed once it has been dead for `dead_pane_grace` (local preference in `[defaults]` or `[profile.x]`, a duration, default `"5m"`; `"0"` closes at once, `"off"` never closes a crashed pane). Clean exits always close.
- Work is never lost silently: before the report fleet runs the auto-close check (`AgentBlocker`, ADR-29). The dead pane is still closed, but a refusal reason is added to the report and the wake line ("left work behind: uncommitted changes by the agent: a; the session is kept, `fleet adopt` can resume it"). Closing never removes the dir, worktree, branch or the tool's session.
- The first sighting is recorded on the board entry (`exit: {code, signal, at}`) before anything is sent, so each dead pane is reported once, also across overview restarts and with several overviews. A close that failed is retried at most once a minute. Without a running overview nothing happens; the pane waits as before.

**Out of scope:** structured payloads (files, links), reports from the admiral to the user, desktop notifications (§18 #13), removing worktrees, panes without `remain-on-exit` (adopted panes that vanish become `gone`), restarting crashed agents.

### 6.8 Admiral tool ergonomics (ADR-24)

**Built 2026-11-05.** Code in `internal/status/{menu,screen}.go`, `internal/fleet/{answer,info}.go`, `Fleet.Send`, `internal/mcp/send.go`.

The admiral's tools answer real Copilot/Claude pickers, cost fewer tokens and need fewer peeks.

**Behaviour.**
- *Numbered prompts.* `status.Set.Menu` reads the bottom 14 lines for a run of options `1.`, `2.`, … with a `❯`/`>` cursor on one, skipping transcript lines (so a numbered list in the agent's reply is no prompt). It returns the options ("N. label", label at most 60 runes), the selected one and the question (closest line above ending in "?"). `fleet_status` appends them to a waiting agent's `waiting_for`: `Do you want to allow this? [1 Yes | 2 Yes, and add these directorie… | 3 No, …]` (at most 200 runes; only waiting agents' panes are captured).
- *Answering.* `fleet_answer` takes `answer` (y/n) or `option` (1–9), exactly one, and still needs `confirmed=true` (ADR-9). With a menu on screen fleet re-captures the pane, moves the cursor with Up/Down from the `❯` item to the target and presses Enter; `y` = option 1, `n` = the first option starting with "No". Menu gone at that point: "prompt no longer on screen". No menu: y/n is typed as before; `option` errors. Optional `expect` (the question the user confirmed): a case-insensitive substring of the question or of the target option's label; no match → "prompt changed: now '<question>'" and nothing is pressed. The reply names the option chosen and the question.
- *Keys.* `fleet_send` takes `keys` from an allowlist (Enter, Escape, Up, Down, Left, Right, Tab, BTab, Space, BSpace, 1–9, y, n). `text`+`keys` pastes without Enter, then presses the keys.
- *Confirmation gate.* Any `fleet_send` (text or keys, every target) and `fleet_team send` to an agent that is on a prompt (a waiting pattern or a numbered menu in a fresh capture) needs `confirmed=true`, since its Enter would answer the prompt; without it that agent is refused/skipped with "agent is waiting on a prompt …". An unsubmitted paste alone is not a prompt, so `keys=["Enter"]` submits it without confirmation.
- *Safer submit.* `Fleet.Send` pastes, waits 150 ms, presses Enter, then watches the pane for up to 2 s for the `pending` pattern (§4). Still there: one more Enter (only if no prompt has come up meanwhile), then an error "pasted but not submitted; try fleet_send keys=[Enter]". `fleet_team send` uses the same path. Tools without `pending` skip the watch.
- *Several targets.* `fleet_send` takes exactly one of `name`, `names` or `team`. Several targets are sent one by one; the reply has one line per agent (`a1: sent`, `a2: skipped (…)`); the confirmation gate applies per agent.
- *Closed agents.* A send to an agent closed in the last 24 h is refused with "x closed at 21:30; pass resume=true to resume its last session" (CLI: `fleet send --resume`). `resume=true` (one `name`, text, no keys) brings it back on its last session with its card, team, owner, squad lead, profile, driver and dir, then sends the text (ADR-95).
- *Peek.* Cleaned by default: lines of only box/block characters dropped, `│┃║` borders and the scrollbar stripped, trailing spaces trimmed, blank runs collapsed; `lines` counts cleaned lines. `raw=true` gives the old output. `last_message=true` returns the agent's last main-agent reply from its session log: fleet's transcript for an ACP agent (the newest agent entry); else profile `subagent_log` (Copilot `assistant.message` without `agentId`/`parentToolCallId`, non-empty); else, for `claude` patterns, the session `.jsonl` under `session_path` (the text blocks of the last non-sidechain assistant message). Logs are read from the last 1 MB, at most 4000 runes (the end). A tool with none (agy, vibe) gets the last 40 cleaned screen lines headed `(from the screen)` instead of an error; `summary=true` uses only the log.
- *`fleet_info(name)`.* `key: value` lines: profile, team, `driver` (`tmux`, or `acp via <ACP server>`: `copilot --acp`, `agy acp`, `claude-agent-acp@0.103.0`; ADR-77), `fleet version` (the build that last started the agent's window; omitted for agents started before v1.2.0), status and its age, last activity, dir, git worktree/branch/dirty files/merged into main (or master)/commits ahead (each git call times out after 3 s; `git: not a git work tree` otherwise), session id, context % (Copilot profiles), mode from the footer (`Autopilot · Allow All`, `Interactive · Manual Approval`).
- *Close note.* `fleet_close` names uncommitted or unmerged work in the agent's dir (the auto-close check, §6.7) in its refusal and in its result. It never blocks the close.

**Out of scope:** auto-answering prompts (ADR-9), report-back and wake-up (§6.7), spawning worktrees, forcing autopilot, hot reload of the MCP server (§6.9), RAM/CPU figures, CLI flags for any of this.

### 6.9 Hot reload of the MCP server (ADR-25)

**Built 2026-11-05.** Code in `internal/mcp/reload.go`, the read loop in `internal/mcp/server.go`, `--probe` in `cmd/fleet/mail.go`.

**Problem.** The admiral CLI starts `fleet mcp` (stdio) once. `make build` puts a new `bin/fleet` in place (new inode), but the running MCP process keeps the old code. On 2026-11-05 an admiral's MCP process ran a deleted binary long after a status fix; its old classifier and the TUI's new one both wrote the board, so agents flipped between waiting and working on every admiral call. Until now the only fix was a handoff, which loses the chat.

**Behaviour.** Every `fleet mcp`, admiral or `--agent`, re-execs itself in place:
- At start the server records the identity of the running binary (`/proc/self/exe`, falling back to the path) and watches the path (`Fleet.Exe`, symlinks resolved).
- Before handling each request line it stats the file once. The file has changed when it is a different file (inode) or its modtime or size differ.
- On a change it first runs `<exe> mcp --probe` with a 5 s timeout. The probe starts like a normal `fleet mcp`, checks the tool definitions are not empty, prints `ok` and exits 0. If the probe fails (half-written file, crash, an older binary without `--probe`), the server logs a warning and handles the request with the old code. It doesn't probe the same file identity again for 10 s.
- If the probe passes, the server writes the current request line and any bytes already read ahead from stdin to a 0600 file under `<home>/run/` (after an earlier reload, also the part of that reload's pending input not read yet). It stats the path again, and if the file changed after the probe it drops the pending file and tries on the next request. Then it `syscall.Exec`s the same path with the same argv and `FLEET_MCP_PENDING=<file>`, `FLEET_MCP_RELOADED=1`. The pid and the stdio pipes stay the same, so the client sees no disconnect. If the exec fails, the server deletes the file and handles the request with the old code (same 10 s back-off).
- The new process clears both variables so children don't inherit them, writes `notifications/tools/list_changed`, reads and deletes the pending file, then serves those bytes before stdin. It doesn't need a new `initialize`, because the server keeps no handshake state. The first tool result after a reload ends with `[fleet updated: tools reloaded]`.
- `initialize` advertises `capabilities.tools.listChanged = true`. Copilot CLI refetches the tool list on the notification (auto-refresh, 300 ms debounce), and Claude Code supports it too.

**What reloads:** tool code and the tool list. State is all on disk (board, mail, notes), and the in-memory `Server` holds only the agent name, a lock and closures that `New` rebuilds, so nothing is lost.
**What doesn't:** the MCP instructions text (clients read it only at `initialize`) and the admiral's own chat and model. Those need the CLI restarted with `--resume <session id>` (manual fallback) or a handoff (last resort).
**Changed instructions (ADR-102).** At `initialize` the server keeps a 12-hex SHA-256 prefix of the instructions it served (for an admiral with `--rules-in-prompt`, of the rules in its system prompt) and passes it across the exec in `FLEET_MCP_SERVED`. When the text the running binary would serve hashes differently, the next tool result ends with `[fleet] instructions changed since this session started; call fleet_instructions`. The hint, or a `fleet_instructions` call, makes the current hash the served one, so each change is reported once, also across later reloads. `fleet_instructions`, in both admiral and agent mode, returns the current text and its hash.

**Out of scope:** a `fleet_reload` tool (reload happens on the next call anyway), automatic `--resume` restarts, reloading the TUI.

### 6.10 Choosing and swapping the admiral's profile (ADR-27)

**Built 2026-11-05.** Code in `cmd/fleet/admiral_profile.go` (resolution, swap, restart, rollback), `cmd/fleet/admiral_picker.go` (Alt-P popup), `internal/supervisor/profile.go` (last choice), `internal/mcp/admiral.go` (`fleet_admiral_profile`).

The admiral (the supervisor before ADR-26; `supervisor` names keep working) runs under one tool profile from `fleet profiles`: `copilot`, `claude-copilot`, `claude-personal` and so on. This section lets the user pick that profile at start and swap it while fleet runs. It only changes the admiral; agents keep the profile they were spawned with (§4).

**Choosing at start.** `fleet up` picks the admiral profile in this order, first match wins:
1. `--admiral-profile NAME` on `fleet up`, or before any command (`fleet --admiral-profile NAME` alone means `fleet up`). `--supervisor-profile` is a deprecated alias (one line on stderr). An unknown name is an error, and nothing starts.
2. `FLEET_ADMIRAL_PROFILE`, then `FLEET_SUPERVISOR_PROFILE`.
3. The last choice, `<home>/admiral/profile` (one line).
4. `[admiral] profile = "NAME"` in the local `config.toml`.
5. `copilot`.
A name from 2–4 that is no longer a profile is skipped with a warning in the log and on the tmux status line, and the next source is tried. If the admiral is already running under another profile, `fleet up --admiral-profile NAME` swaps it (below) and then attaches.

**Swapping live** (`fleet admiral profile NAME`; `fleet supervisor profile` is the same handler)
- `fleet admiral profile` with no name prints the active profile and the list, the active one marked.
- tmux `Alt-P` and overview `P` open a picker popup (`display-popup`) listing the profiles, the active one marked; arrows or `j`/`k` and ⏎ pick, a digit picks directly, `Esc`/`q` cancels. The picker exits at once and the swap runs in the background (`tmux run-shell -b`); the result shows on the status line.
- MCP `fleet_admiral_profile(profile, summary?)` (admiral only). It checks the name, writes `summary` as the handoff when given, schedules `fleet admiral profile NAME` through `tmux run-shell -b` after about 2 seconds and returns at once ("switching to NAME in a few seconds. Stop here."), because the swap replaces the pane its own MCP server runs in.
- `fleet admiral restart` restarts the admiral under its current profile and resumes the same session. It is also what `fleet admiral profile <current>` does.

**Context carries over.** A different profile is a different CLI or account, so the chat cannot move.
- *Same profile* (restart): `respawn-pane -k` with the profile's `resume` args and the same session ID, no boot prompt. Same generation, same `session.json` except `pane_id`. The chat survives; the MCP instructions text and the model are reloaded (the manual fallback named in §6.9). A profile without `resume` args, or no known session ID, falls back to a cross-profile style swap with a handoff.
- *Other profile* (swap): fleet writes an automatic handoff first. If the outgoing admiral wrote a handoff since its session started (or passed `summary`), that text is kept; otherwise it is the last 40 lines of the admiral pane (trimmed, inside the 4 KB cap). Both get a first line: `Admiral profile swap at <time>: OLD → NEW. Previous session: <id> (resume: <old resume command>).` Then the reset path of §6.5 runs with the new profile: new session ID, boot prompt, generation + 1, `previous` = old ID. The boot prompt reaches the new CLI through the profile's `prompt_args` (copilot `-i`, claude positional); a profile without them gets it pasted once its TUI is ready.
- The admiral's lean flags follow the CLI: copilot-pattern profiles keep §16a's args; claude-pattern profiles get `--mcp-config` with the fleet MCP server (stdio, `--rules-in-prompt`), `--strict-mcp-config`, `--allowedTools mcp__fleet`, the rules as `--append-system-prompt-file <admiral dir>/rules.md` (written from the binary on every launch) and the env `ENABLE_TOOL_SEARCH=false`, `ENABLE_CLAUDEAI_MCP_SERVERS=false` (ADR-86) and `ENABLE_PROMPT_CACHING_1H=1` (ADR-93). Every admiral starts in its own dir under fleet home (`~/.fleet/admiral` for main, `~/.fleet/admirals/<name>` for the others), outside any git checkout; a copilot admiral gets its `.mcp.json` there. `FLEET_ADMIRAL_DIR` overrides it. `session.json` records the dir (`dir`), and a claude admiral resumes there, since Claude finds a session by its project dir; a session saved without one resumes in the old `supervisor/` dir (ADR-93).

**Shown.** The admiral pane border reads `admiral · claude-personal · ctx 34%` (pane option `@fleet_admiral_profile`, set by up, reset, restart and swap, and refreshed by the overview loop from `session.json`). `fleet admiral` prints the profile after the session ID (`--json` already has `profile`). `fleet_status` and `fleet_brief` start with one line: `admiral: claude-personal · session 3f2a91c · gen 3`.

**Persisted.** After a successful start (up with an explicit flag, swap, restart) the profile is written to `<home>/admiral/profile`. Reset (§6.5) keeps the running profile.

**Failure keeps the old admiral.** All under the reset lock (`reset.lock`).
- Unknown profile: error before anything changes (CLI exit 1, MCP tool error, picker status line).
- After the respawn fleet watches the pane for 5 s (`FLEET_ADMIRAL_START_WAIT` overrides, for tests). If it dies (command not found, auth failure on start), fleet restores the old admiral: resume of the old session when the old profile has `resume` args and an ID, else a fresh old-profile admiral with the boot prompt. `session.json` and the persisted choice stay as they were, the handoff stays on disk, and the error ("admiral profile NAME failed to start; kept OLD") goes to the status line, stderr and the log.

**Out of scope:** per-profile admiral settings beyond `[admiral] profile` (mode, extra args); context meters for non-Copilot admirals (still unknown, §6.5); moving chat history between CLIs; changing the profile of running agents.

### 6.11 Squads: an agent that leads its own agents (ADR-53)

**Built 2026-11-05.** Code in `internal/fleet/squad.go` (model, caps, scope, spawn, close), `internal/mcp/squad.go` (the leader's tools), `internal/fleet/wake.go` (one wake queue per mailbox), `internal/fleet/report.go` (routing).

One admiral cannot hold ten agents' detail at once. A **leader** is an ordinary agent that is also allowed to run its own small squad, so a big piece of work arrives at the admiral as one summary instead of six.

**The model.** A leader is a board agent with `leader = true`; each of its agents carries `lead_by = <leader>`. The board is the truth; the squad is mirrored into a team named after the leader, so the squad shares a colour on the wall and may message freely (§6.6). Closing a leader detaches its agents (they report to the admiral again), re-files the squad's unread reports to the admiral, and deletes the mirror team — only a team fleet created for that squad (`squad` in `teams.json`); a user's team of the same name is never joined or deleted (ADR-59). From inside a leader's pane the `fleet` CLI's `close`, `send`, `park` and `wake` act only on its own agents, and `send` refuses a member on a prompt.

**Spawning one.** `fleet spawn --leader <name> <profile> [dir]` and `fleet_spawn(..., leader: true)`. A leader gets the same profile, worktree, task card and playbook as any spawn, plus the leader section of the playbook.

**Orientation on turn one (ADR-58).** The squad tools are MCP tools, and whether a tool is listed at all depends on the board flag being right at the moment the leader's MCP server starts. So the leader is also told in plain text, in its *first prompt*: `internal/playbook/leader.md`, appended for a leader at every effort level and even with `playbook = false`. It names the eight squad tools and what each is for, says its agents report to it and peers in its team are reached with `fleet_agents`/`fleet_msg_send`, forbids spawning through the `fleet` CLI or a shell, and tells it to report `state=blocked` if it has `fleet_*` tools but no `fleet_squad_*` among them rather than improvise. A profile with no `peer_args` (§6.6: `agy`, `vibe`, `fake`) runs no MCP server at all, so the orientation says that too: with no `fleet_*` tools the CLI is that leader's supported path. A `fleet spawn` run from inside a leader's pane is routed to `SquadSpawn` anyway, so the CLI cannot silently make a sibling; `--leader` from a leader's pane is refused with a pointer to `fleet_squad_spawn`.

**The leader's tools** (agent mode, on top of §6.6's peer tools): `fleet_squad_spawn`, `fleet_squad_profiles`, `fleet_squad_status`, `fleet_squad_info`, `fleet_squad_peek`, `fleet_squad_send`, `fleet_squad_reports`, `fleet_squad_close`. Every one that names an agent refuses a name outside the squad ("not in your squad"); a non-leader calling any of them is told it does not lead a squad. There is no `fleet_answer` for a leader, and `fleet_squad_close` is the leader's own call: `confirmed=true` closes on the first call and the reply names any uncommitted or unmerged work left behind, while `confirmed=false` only lists that work. A leader has no user to ask, so the admiral's "ask the user, then confirmed=true" rule for `fleet_close` does not carry over (fleet#33).

**Routing.** A squad agent's `fleet_report` goes to its leader's own inbox and wakes the leader's pane through the §6.7 machinery, which is now one queue, try counter and lock per mailbox under `mail/<box>/` instead of per admiral. The queue line keeps its four fields. The admiral sees only the leader's reports; dead-pane and stuck reports follow the same route. The badge and the tmux status line stay admiral-only.

**Caps.** `[squad] max_members` (4) and `max_depth` (1, so a squad agent may not itself lead), read live, `0` meaning unlimited. Squad spawns go through the normal spawn path, so `max_working` queues them like any other agent and spend and memory count them like any other agent.

**Safety.** A leader escalates rather than acts: it may not answer a permission prompt (`fleet_squad_send` refuses an agent whose status is `waiting`); `force=true` gets past the user-typing and unsubmitted-input check, as on `fleet_send`, never past a prompt (fleet#20), and merging, pushing and applying infra are the user's, as they are for any agent. The chain is squad → leader → admiral → user.

**Shown.** `fleet_status` has a `squad` column (`leads m1,m2` / `→ lead`), the overview tile and row carry a `▲` for a leader, and `fleet info` prints a `squad:` line. `fleet close <leader>` warns about live squad agents; `--squad` (MCP `squad: true`) closes them too.

### 6.12 Several admirals on one fleet (ADR-64)

**Built 2026-11-07.** Code in `internal/supervisor/admirals.go` (registry), `internal/fleet/admiral.go` (give, close, scope), `internal/mail/move.go`, `internal/mcp/admirals.go`, `cmd/fleet/admiral_cmd.go`, `internal/layout` (`Sups`) and `internal/ui/owner.go`.

One admiral steering two unrelated streams of work (say Bubbla and the print app) mixes both into one chat and one report queue, and fills its context twice as fast. This lets two (or a few) admirals run at once on one fleet. Each owns its own agents and gets only their reports; both see the whole board.

**The model.** An admiral has a name. The admiral that exists today is `main` and keeps every path, mailbox and pane it has now, so a running admiral needs no restart. Each board agent carries `admiral = <name>`; empty means `main`, which is what every existing agent has. A squad agent (§6.11) belongs to its leader's admiral. The board, teams, decisions log, pinned notes, wall and overview stay one each, shared.

**Starting one.** `fleet admiral add <name> [--profile P]` (refused past `[admiral] max`, default 3; values above 3 are clamped to 3, the palette has three admiral colours) starts a new admiral pane under the existing one in `fleet:main`'s left column (the overview stays on top and the wall on the right; `internal/layout` splits the admiral area evenly). The heights stay as the user sets them: a border drag or `fleet admiral min <name>` (which toggles that admiral between 2 rows and an even share) is kept through every relayout (ADR-84). `fleet admiral ls` lists them: name, profile, pane, agents owned, unread reports. `fleet admiral close <name>` (or main's `fleet_admiral_close`, ADR-109) ends one: its agents go back to `main`, its unread reports are re-filed to `main`, as closing a leader does (§6.11). `main` cannot be closed. `fleet up` restarts every admiral listed on disk. Names follow agent-name rules; `user`, `admiral`, `supervisor` and agent names already on the board are refused.

**Per admiral on disk.** `main` keeps `admiral/` and `mail/admiral/`. Another admiral gets `admirals/<name>/` (`session.json`, `handoff.md`, `handoffs/`, `profile`) and the mailbox `mail/admiral.<name>/`. Reset, restart, profile swap and the context meter (§6.5, §6.10) work per admiral; `fleet admiral reset|restart|profile` take `--name N` (default: the admiral whose pane runs the command, else `main`), and `M-R` / `Alt-P` act on the admiral pane that has focus, else `main`.

**Identity.** Every admiral gets its own MCP config whose fleet server args carry `--admiral <name>` (claude: inline `--mcp-config`; copilot: a non-main admiral starts in its own dir, `admirals/<name>/`, whose workspace `.mcp.json` is its only `fleet` server, so it never competes with the shared `supervisor/.mcp.json` that `main` keeps), and the admiral pane is tagged `@role=sup` and `@fleet_admiral=<name>`. `fleet mcp` resolves `--admiral`, then `FLEET_ADMIRAL_NAME`, then its pane's tag; an untagged `@role=sup` pane is `main` (an admiral started before this feature, so it keeps working after the MCP hot reload, §6.9). A name that is not `main` or in the registry makes `fleet mcp` exit with `unknown admiral`. There is no fallback to `main`: with no pane, another tmux server or a pane that is not an admiral, the server runs with identity unknown, still answers reads (`fleet_status`, `fleet_peek`, `fleet_info`, `fleet_brief`, `fleet_profiles`, `fleet_sessions`, `fleet_access`, `fleet_touching`, `fleet_changes`, `fleet_instructions`) and refuses every change and every per-admiral tool (spawn, adopt, send, answer, close, park, takeover, team, wall, adr, note, reports, handoff, handover, worktrees, branches, admiral add, admiral mail, access decisions) until the admiral is restarted with `fleet admiral restart --name <name>`. Like §6.6, this governs cooperation, not security.

**What an admiral may do to agents.**
- Read anything: `fleet_status`, `fleet_peek`, `fleet_info` and the overview show every agent, with an `admiral` column/mark.
- Act only on its own: `fleet_send`, `fleet_answer`, `fleet_close`, `fleet_park` (park and wake), `fleet_team` send/close/edit and `fleet_access_decide` for a pair with one of its agents. Another admiral's agent is refused with "<agent> belongs to admiral print; ask for it with fleet_handover_request name=<agent>, or ask the user". `fleet_handover_request` mails the owner "admiral Y asks for <agent>; approve with fleet_handover <agent> to=Y" (no access grant needed); only the owner's `fleet_handover` or the user moves the agent (ADR-109). `fleet_send` with `names`/`team` skips such agents per line, as the prompt gate does (§6.8).
- `fleet_spawn` and `fleet_adopt` make the agent its own. A user's shell CLI spawn (`fleet spawn`) gives it to `main` unless `--admiral NAME`.
- Teams belong to the admiral that made them; a team cannot mix agents of two admirals.
- The user's own shell CLI is never scoped.

**Reports and wakes.** `fleet_report` from an agent goes to its admiral's mailbox and wakes that admiral's pane with the §6.7/ADR-53 per-mailbox machinery; dead-pane, stuck and auto-close reports follow the same route. `fleet_reports` and `fleet_brief` show the caller's own. Each admiral pane border shows its own unread count (`admiral print · ✉ 2 · ctx 34%`); the global status line and the overview badge show the total, split by admiral when more than one has unread ("✉ main 1 · print 2").

**Shared memory.** `decisions.log` and `pinned.log` stay one file each. Every new line names the writer: `2026-11-07T21:40 @print [agent] text`; lines without `@` are read as `main`. `fleet_brief` shows all pinned notes and the last 30 log lines from every admiral, the writer shown. The handoff is per admiral: a reset of `print` must not hand it `main`'s plans.

**Telling them apart.** Each admiral gets a colour from the team palette, chosen at `add` and kept in its `session.json`. The admiral pane border shows the name in that colour; each overview card and row, and each wall pane border, carries a small mark in its admiral's colour when more than one admiral exists. One admiral means no mark, so today's screen does not change.

**Handing an agent over.** Only the user decides:
- CLI `fleet admiral give <agent> <admiral>` (also takes `--team T` for a whole team).
- Overview: a key on the selected card opens a picker of admirals (the key is the designer's call; `O` is free).
- MCP `fleet_handover(name, to)` lets an admiral *give away* its own agent (never take one); the user's yes is not needed for giving.
The agent's unread reports move with it, and the receiving admiral gets one wake line: `[fleet] main handed you api-auth (done: …). Full text: call fleet_reports.`

**Admirals message each other (§6.6 mail, same policy).** An admiral is a mail identity: `main` uses the mailbox it has (`admiral`), another uses `admiral.<name>`; agent names starting `admiral.` are reserved. Admiral MCP gains the agent-mode mail tools, limited to admiral recipients and addressed by admiral name: `fleet_admirals` (the other admirals, profile, ctx, access state with me), `fleet_msg_send(to, text)` (`to` = admiral name or `*` for every admiral I may message), `fleet_inbox`, `fleet_access_request`, `fleet_access_respond`, `fleet_access_revoke`. Access follows `[defaults] agent_mail` exactly as for agents: `open` makes every admiral pair free; `team` and `ask` give admirals no implicit access (they have no team or repo), so one asks and the other, or the user (`fleet access grant main print`), decides. The messages are in `fleet access log` and `fleet_access limit=N` like any agent mail. Messages and reports share the admiral's inbox file but not their read mark: `fleet_reports` reads reports, `fleet_inbox` reads the rest (requests, grants, messages). A message or request to an admiral is announced with the guarded wake line of §6.7 (`[fleet] message from admiral main. Read it with fleet_inbox.`), never the agent nudge, and counts in that admiral's border badge. Admirals do not mail agents (they use `fleet_send`), and agents cannot mail admirals (they report).

**Settled 2026-11-07.** The second admiral stacks under the first in `fleet:main`'s left column; the handoff is per admiral; up to `[admiral] max` (default 3) admirals, built and tested for 2; an admiral may give away its own agent, only the user takes or moves another's; admirals message each other through §6.6 mail.

**Out of scope:** admirals on separate fleets or machines; a separate overview or wall per admiral; per-admiral `max_working`, spend or memory budgets; routing new agents to an admiral by repo; renaming `main`; admirals mailing agents or agents mailing admirals; access control stronger than cooperation.

---

---

## 7. Memory (RAM): parking (ADR-44, built)

An idle agent still holds its CLI process, about 300-450 MB (`docs/perf.md`). Parking gives that memory back without losing the conversation.

- **Park** = stop the process and everything it started (the pane's whole process tree, so a CLI's helper such as a voice server is not left behind; `fleet close` does the same), keep the agent on the board as `parked` with its session id, dir, worktree, goal and report. `fleet park <name>`, `fleet_park(name)` for the admiral, or the memory guard. Only an `idle` agent can be parked: a working, waiting, stuck or starting one, an agent sitting on a prompt, and a pinned pane (`@fleet_pin`) are refused with the reason.
- **Wake** = `fleet wake <name>` (`--force` to wake while memory is still low) or `fleet_park(name, action="wake", force=…)`: the profile's resume command on the same session id, in the same dir, under the same name. It is the adopt path without the session hunt, since fleet stopped that process itself. A profile with no `resume` args can't be parked back to life, so waking it fails with that reason.
- **Memory guard**: when free RAM (`MemAvailable`) falls under `[memory] min_free` (default 4 GB), fleet parks the longest-idle agent, one per `check_every` (default 30 s), never a pinned, working, waiting or prompted one, and tells the admiral in a report line. While memory is low, new spawns queue instead of starting (§7a), with the reason on the tile. Everything is reversible (`fleet wake`) and `guard = false` switches it off. With `park_idle` set (e.g. `"10m"`, default off), it also parks an agent idle that long while memory is fine, logged only (ADR-106).
- **Live settings**: `max_working` and the `[memory]` keys are re-read from `config.toml` on every use, so a running fleet retunes without a restart.
- Target: 40 tiles visible, about 12 processes running, about 5 GB.

## 7a. Heat: a cap on working agents, spawn env, cheaper polling (ADR-23)

Measured in `docs/perf.md`: an agent costs its CLI process (about 300-450 MB) and the CPU of whatever it runs, mostly builds and tests. Idle agents cost almost no CPU. So the heat lever is how many agents *work* at once, and how hard each one's toolchain runs.

- **Working cap and spawn queue.** `max_working` in `[defaults]` of `config.toml` (default 4, `0` = no cap). Agents count as busy while their status is `working`, `stuck` or `starting`, and while `unknown` (just started) for at most 2 minutes, so agents whose screen never matches a pattern can't freeze the queue. A spawn also queues while other agents are queued or starting, so the queue stays first in, first out. `fleet spawn` and `fleet_spawn` with the cap reached write the agent to the board as `queued` (profile, dir and goal kept, no pane) and return at once. The reply says where it stands: "queued x (profile, model m) in DIR: n ahead of it, b agents busy, max_working N; it starts when a slot frees". The overview TUI starts the oldest queued agent when a slot frees: at most one per poll, claimed under the board lock as `starting`, launched in the background so the poll never blocks. The queue also drains outside the TUI: the admiral's MCP server drains it on each poll, and `fleet ls` drains it synchronously before printing, so a fleet run from the CLI alone still starts its queued agents (ADR-44). Only one of them can win a slot: the claim happens under the board lock. `fleet close` drops a queued agent. `fleet_send` and `fleet_squad_send` text to a queued agent is appended to its first prompt under the board lock, so it is delivered when the agent starts ("queued for x: delivered when it starts"); keys, and the CLI's `fleet send`, still fail with "queued, not started yet" (fleet#33). `fleet adopt` is not queued: it resumes a session the user picked by hand. A `fleet_send resume=true` is: over the cap the closed agent is queued with its session and the text held, and the drain resumes it (ADR-95).
- **Spawn env.** `env = { KEY = "value" }` in `[defaults]` or `[profile.<name>]` is added to the agent pane's environment on spawn and adopt, over the profile's own env; profile keys override default keys one by one. The repo sets nothing (ADR-13). The suggested local setting is `GOFLAGS = "-p=4", GOMAXPROCS = "4"` so parallel `go test ./...` runs don't each take every core.
- **Off-wall polling.** The overview TUI captures panes on the wall every poll (1 s) and panes off the wall every 5 s. Status changes of hidden agents show up to 5 s later. `fleet ls` and `fleet_status` still capture every pane. While the watcher is live, the overview makes no tmux calls of its own per poll: it reads the watcher's shared snapshot (§7c, ADR-98).
- Out of scope: pausing or freezing an agent that is already working; parking (§7); per-agent CPU or memory limits (the user's `copilot.slice`, see `docs/perf.md`).

## 7b. Spend per agent (§18 #11, ADR-46, ADR-71)

**Built 2026-11-05.** Code in `internal/spend` (parsing and the cache), `internal/fleet/spend.go` (board-wide rows and total), `cmd/fleet/spend.go` (`fleet spend`), with the figures shown by `fleet info`/`fleet_info` and `fleet_status`.

Agentic work burns credits quietly: ten agents on autopilot spend while nobody looks. Fleet reads what each agent already cost from the session file its own tool writes, and calls no model to do it.

- **Where the numbers come from.** Copilot writes a `session.usage_record` event per model call into `~/.copilot/session-state/<id>/events.jsonl`, carrying input, output, cache-read and cache-write tokens and `copilotUsage.totalNanoAiu` — nano AI units, a billionth of the AI credit the status line calls *AIC*. Claude writes `assistant` lines into `~/.claude/projects/*/<id>.jsonl` with an Anthropic `usage` block and no credit figure, and newer versions also keep a running `cost-state` total per model. A profile maps to a format by its `patterns` name, so `claude-copilot` and any other Copilot variant work with no extra configuration, and a tool that records nothing (agy, vibe, fake) simply has no spend.
- **Counted once.** A Claude reply holding text and a tool call is written as *two* transcript lines with the same `message.id` and the same usage block, so the records are deduplicated by that id — including across two incremental reads. Where a `cost-state` running total exists it wins over the sum up to the point it was written, because it is the tool's own accounting; records appended after it — a resumed session continues the same file — are added to it, and the call count and the model always come from the records, which the running total does not carry. A session that only ever wrote a running total therefore reports its tokens with no call count. The two paths are asserted equal on the same captured session. An `assistant` line whose model is `<synthetic>` is an API error, not a call.
- **What is shown.** One column: AI credits where the tool reports them (`12.8 AIC`), total tokens where it does not (`30k tok`). A fleet of both kinds shows both, `212 AIC + 30k tok`, because credits and tokens are different units and adding them would be a lie. `fleet info` and `fleet_info` add the call count, the token total and how much of it was cached. `fleet spend [--json]` is the table over the whole board with a `TOTAL` row; `fleet_status` carries the per-agent column and ends with one `fleet spend so far:` line.
- **Read cheaply.** A process-wide cache keyed by path holds the running total, the file size and the modification time of the last read. An unchanged file costs one `stat`; a changed one is read only from the offset where the last read stopped, and a half-written last line waits for its newline. A replaced or truncated file starts again. The first pass over a file is capped at 64 MB, past which the oldest records are skipped and the figure is a floor. Nothing on this path runs in the TUI draw loop.
- **Compactions and the recycle hint (ADR-71).** Copilot writes a `session.compaction_complete` event each time it summarises a full context to go on; fleet counts the successful ones per agent and shows them after the credits, `1108 AIC ⟲2` in `fleet_status` and `fleet spend`, "compacted 2×" in `fleet info`. `fleet spend --json` also carries `context_pct`, the fill at the last main-agent call (`inputTokens` of `maxPromptTokens`; subagent calls run in their own context and are skipped). Claude transcripts carry a `system` line with subtype `compact_boundary` and `compactMetadata.trigger` `auto`, `manual` or `idle`; fleet counts the first two and skips `idle` (Claude 2.1.299+ compacts a large conversation idle for about an hour, before its prompt cache expires: that says nothing about context churn, and fleet agents often idle waiting on peers) (ADR-79). The compaction call's own cost is counted too: Copilot puts it in `compactionTokensUsed` (tokens and `copilotUsage.totalNanoAiu`) on the compaction event, never in a `usage_record`, so fleet adds it, failed attempts included; Claude's appears in its `cost-state` total.
