Once an agent reaches `recycle_compactions` (default 2) or `recycle_aic` (default 1000) from `[defaults]`, 0 turning either off, the resident overview tells its owner (the admiral, or its squad leader) once per agent through the same mail and pending-wake path as a no-report note: "<name> has compacted N times / spent X AIC: hand over to a fresh agent — have it write result.md, close it, respawn with result.md plus a brief". The check runs at most every 30 s, off the poll; `recycle_told` on the board, kept across park and wake, makes it once. Fleet does nothing to the agent.
- **What it leaves out.** The summariser (ADR-40) shells out to a fresh Copilot CLI session per call, so its tokens land in `session-state` under an id fleet never learns and cannot attribute to an agent or to the fleet. Every spend output says so rather than quietly undercounting.
- **Out of scope for now:** a per-task or per-fleet credit cap and the pause that would enforce it, spend on the overview card (the header is already crowded, §5d), and a spend history beyond the usage bar's 40-day ledger.
- **The usage bar (ADR-97).** The tmux status line, after the report badge, shows what each profile spent today and how much of the Copilot month is gone: `today claude-work $12 · copilot $3.10 │ Copilot 38% of month`. Today means since local midnight. The badge writer (the watcher while it is live, otherwise the overview, ADR-75) runs the pass at most every 30 s, off the poll, through the same spend cache. It reads every agent on the board and every agent closed today, and adds what each session grew since its last mark to the day ledger `~/.fleet/usage.json`, by profile. A session first seen on the day it was created counts in full; one fleet first meets later only sets a baseline. The global options `@fleet_usage` and `@fleet_usage_level` are set only when they change. Each profile shows an estimated cost in $ at list price: Copilot AI credits at GitHub's fixed $0.01, Claude tokens at Anthropic's per-model price (`[usage] price_<family>`). A model with no price shows tokens instead. For Copilot profiles, the share of the monthly allowance (`[usage] copilot_month_aic`, default 3,900 for an Enterprise seat) is the sum of their AI credits since the 1st. Past 100% the bar shows the overage in $ (`Copilot 112% of month +$47 over`). A profile with a `[usage] day_usd` budget shows its percentage of that. At 80% of an allowance or budget the text turns the waiting colour, and at 100% the error colour, a steady colour that never blinks. Past 90 columns the bar shows the day total only. `fleet spend --today [--json]` is the details view with raw AI credits and tokens. The figures cover fleet's own agents and are not the bill: other Copilot or Claude use on the same seat isn't seen, and Claude subscriptions have no allowance fleet can read.

## 7c. Live status: the watcher and tool hooks (ADR-49)

Status has to be right when nobody is looking. Until the watcher there was no process refreshing it unless the overview TUI was open, so a pane border could say "working" while the agent sat on a trust prompt.

- **One watcher per fleet.** `fleet up` starts `fleet watch` detached; it writes a heartbeat at `~/.fleet/watch.json` (pid, time, and the time of its last *successful* refresh — a watcher that breathes while every refresh fails is not live, and the overview goes back to refreshing for itself) and is the one process that refreshes, drains the spawn queue and claims stuck episodes. `fleet watch --stop` stops it, and only ever signals a watcher that is live, so a pid left by a crash and since recycled is never killed. The claim is an exclusive `flock` on `~/.fleet/watch.lock` held for the watcher's life, so a second watcher refuses to start and a crash or reboot frees it with no stale pid to trip over (ADR-62). It exits by itself when the `fleet` tmux session or `~/.fleet` goes away, and when `bin/fleet` is rebuilt it re-execs into the new binary, same pid, once `fleet watch --probe` answers `ok` (ADR-62, as `fleet mcp` does under ADR-25); the new image takes its own pid's heartbeat for itself, not for another watcher. `fleet up` replaces a watcher still running an older or deleted binary.
- **Events coalesce.** Two event-driven refreshes are at least 500 ms apart. A working agent appends to its session log many times a second, and one capture pass covers every event since the last one, so a chatty agent cannot turn the watcher into a 5 Hz poll.
- **Events first, polling as the floor.** Every 200 ms it stats a short list: `~/.fleet/status/` (one stat covers every hook-driven agent) and each running agent's session event log (Copilot's `events.jsonl`, Claude's session jsonl, which grow on every turn and tool call). A change refreshes at once; with nothing happening it still refreshes once a second, so tools without hooks are no worse off than the old overview poll. Pane activity was rejected as a source: a working agent redraws its spinner constantly, so it is a faster poll wearing an event's clothes.
- **The watcher is the resident poll.** It owns the once-a-poll housekeeping as well as the refresh: admiral wakes (§6.7), the done sweep, unclaimed done-checks and dead-pane reaping. Those jobs belong to whichever process is resident, and with no overview open that is the watcher alone (ADR-51).
- **One refresher at a time.** While the heartbeat is live the overview TUI reads `board.json` instead of capturing panes itself (it still delivers admiral wakes, which are not a refresh), so opening or closing it changes nothing about how fresh status is — and the cost of 8 agents stays one capture pass per second, as measured in `docs/perf.md`.
- **One tmux snapshot per tick (ADR-98).** The watcher's refresh takes its pane list as one `list-panes -a` that also carries each window's wall options, patches in the statuses it has just worked out, and writes it to `~/.fleet/tmux.json` beside `board.json`. While the watcher is live and the snapshot is at most 3 s old and newer than the overview's own last wall change, the overview's poll checks the wall, the target, the pins and the admiral list against the snapshot and calls tmux only when something there needs changing; any key or click still works on live tmux. The admiral's context meter is recomputed at most every 5 s. The watcher deletes the snapshot when it stops; with no live watcher the overview polls tmux as before.
- **Hooks are per agent, never global.** Each Claude and Copilot agent gets its own fleet plugin, `~/.fleet/plugins/<name>/` (ADR-99): `.claude-plugin/plugin.json`, `hooks/hooks.json` mapping `SessionStart`, `UserPromptSubmit`, `PreToolUse`, `PostToolUse`, `Notification`, `PermissionRequest`, `Stop`, `SubagentStart`, `SubagentStop`, `PreCompact` and `SessionEnd` to `fleet hook <event> --agent <name>` (Claude's format, which Copilot reads too), and for Copilot a `.mcp.json` with fleet-peer. It is passed as `--plugin-dir` (Copilot on both drivers, Claude in tmux) or in the Claude adapter's `session/new` `_meta.claudeCode.options.plugins`. Only changed files are rewritten, so a fleet upgrade updates it on the next spawn or wake; close removes it. Claude keeps `--settings`, now for the write fence only (ADR-79). Fleet never writes to `~/.claude/settings.json` or `~/.copilot`. `fleet hook` takes Claude's and Copilot's names (`agentStop`, `userPromptSubmitted`, `postToolUseFailure`, …) and both payload spellings: `Stop`/`agentStop` → idle, `Notification` → waiting (its message, else its kind, becomes `waiting_for`), the rest → working. `PermissionRequest` changes no status: Copilot fires it before `--allow-all` approves the call, so it would read "waiting" for as long as every command runs. Subagent events, and any hook fired inside a subagent (`agent_id`/`agentId` set: Copilot also fires `Stop` and `PermissionRequest` for its subagents), change no status. Under the acp driver the hooks run with `--no-status`, since the host owns the state file. An unknown event is ignored, and a failed hook never fails the agent's turn.
- **No report (ADR-68).** An agent handed work (`prompt_at`) that has sat idle, with an unchanged screen, for 20 s with no done, blocked or question report since is "no report": `…` on its tile and border, "idle (no report)" in `fleet_status`, and one wake to its owner per task. An agent the user is talking to gets the soft form instead: a muted `…`, "idle (no report, the user's: don't send)" and no wake, when its latest turn ended with no report of any kind and the user has not typed in its pane for 30 s. Copilot, agy and vibe also speak the Agent Client Protocol (ACP), but only to the process that started them over stdio, so it can't watch a pane agent. Claude has no ACP, and none of the tools serve A2A. An agent idle on its own open `question` report reads as waiting instead, and a question or blocked report counts as answered once a turn starts after it (ADR-90). The flag is the fallback: where the fleet plugin's hooks run (Claude and Copilot, both drivers), `fleet hook stop` first blocks the stop once, so the agent reports in the same turn (ADR-105).

## 7d. ACP agents: fleet drives the tool over the Agent Client Protocol (ADR-74, the default for Copilot and agy since v1.0.0)

An ACP agent runs the tool's ACP server (`copilot --acp`, `agy acp`) instead of its TUI, and fleet is the ACP client. Status, prompts, answers, cancel and resume go over JSON-RPC, so nothing is read off a screen. Since v1.0.0 this is how `copilot` and `agy` agents run unless told otherwise, and every other profile that runs the copilot or agy binary too, `claude-copilot` first (ADR-74 (i)). Claude stays on tmux unless a profile opts in (ADR-76: the pinned `claude-agent-acp` adapter); vibe (`vibe-acp` can follow once smoke-tested) stays on tmux.

- **The driver.** The tool profile's `driver` key (tools.toml) is its default: `acp` for the built-in `copilot` and `agy`. A profile with no `driver` key whose `command` is `copilot` or `agy` (by base name: `claude-copilot`, a user's `[gpt-copilot]`) defaults to `acp` too, and takes the built-in's `acp` keys when it sets none (ADR-74 (i)); every other profile defaults to `tmux`. `fleet spawn --driver tmux|acp`, `driver` on `fleet_spawn`/`fleet_squad_spawn`/`fleet_adopt`, or `driver = "tmux"`/`"acp"` under `[defaults]` or `[profile.<name>]` in config.toml override it; the flag wins, then config's `[profile.<name>]`, then config's `[defaults]`, then the tool profile, then `tmux`. A profile with neither `acp` args nor an `acp_command` (vibe) refuses `--driver acp`. For a profile with an `acp_command` (claude) `[defaults] driver` is ignored: only the flag, `[profile.<name>]` or the tool profile's own `driver` key select acp (ADR-76). The board records `driver: "acp"` (empty means tmux).
- **Upgrading from v0 (BREAKING in v1.0.0).** A copilot or agy agent spawned after the upgrade shows fleet's transcript in its pane, not the tool's TUI. Agents that already exist keep the driver the board recorded: a running tmux agent stays on tmux, a parked one wakes on tmux and a queued one starts on tmux, because neither wake nor the queue resolves the driver again. To keep the old behaviour for new agents, put `driver = "tmux"` under `[defaults]` (every profile) or `[profile.copilot]`/`[profile.agy]` in config.toml, or pass `--driver tmux`.
- **Profiles.** `acp` holds the args that start the tool's ACP server and `acp_mode_args` its starting modes: copilot and claude-copilot `--acp` (auto: `--allow-all`), agy `acp` (auto: `--dangerously-skip-permissions`), the fake `--acp`. The ACP argv is command, peer args, profile args, `acp`, mode args, context args.
- **Claude (opt-in, ADR-76).** The built-in `claude` profile carries `acp_command` (the pinned adapter, `npx -y @agentclientprotocol/claude-agent-acp@<tools.ClaudeACPVersion>`), which replaces the whole claude argv: profile args, peer args, extra args and context args are claude flags and are dropped. `acp_env` adds `CLAUDE_CODE_EXECUTABLE={command_path}` (the profile's `command` found in `~/.local/bin` or on PATH; the spawn fails without it) and `ANTHROPIC_MODEL` (the model; the adapter takes it from the env). `acp_modes` maps fleet's mode to an ACP session mode the host sets with `session/set_mode` before the first prompt (auto and bypass → `bypassPermissions`, accept-edits → `acceptEdits`, plan → `plan`, else `default`). `acp_mcp = "session"` passes the peer MCP server in `session/new`/`resume` `mcpServers` instead of `--mcp-config`. `acp_meta = "claude"` sends `_meta.claudeCode.options` on `session/new`, `load` and `resume` (host flag `--meta`): the fleet plugin as `plugins: [{type: "local", path}]` and the write fence as `settings` (ADR-99). `acp_unset_env` names env vars the host drops before starting the adapter (`ANTHROPIC_API_KEY`, `ANTHROPIC_AUTH_TOKEN`, `CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN`). A per-account profile (`claude-personal`, ...) sets `acp_from = "claude"` to take every unset `acp*` key from `claude`, so the pin lives in one place, and keeps its own `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR`. The ACP session id is the Claude session id, so spend, resume and `session_path` work as for a tmux agent.
- **Which driver an agent is on (ADR-77).** The board records it at start: `driver` (`acp`; absent is tmux, as for every agent from before v1.0.0), `acp_server` (the ACP server's label, `tools.Profile.ACPLabel`) and `fleet_version`. `fleet_info` and `fleet info` show `driver: acp via copilot --acp` (or `driver: tmux`) and `fleet version: v1.2.0 (started the agent)`; `fleet_status` and `fleet ls` have a `driver` column; the overview tile's second line reads `copilot·acp · working 3m`. Nothing is guessed from the screen; an acp agent started before v1.2.0 has no `acp_server` and shows its profile's current server.
- **The host lives in the agent's tmux window.** The window runs `fleet acp-host <name> --dir D [--profile P] [--session ID] [--prompt-file ~/.fleet/acp/<name>.prompt] [--copilot-events] -- <ACP argv>`. The first prompt, playbook included, goes through the 0600 prompt file; there is no paste, no mode keys and no ready wait. The host starts the ACP server as its child and draws the conversation (messages, tool calls, the pending question) in the pane. The pane is also the user's console (ADR-82): a themed full-pane view (ADR-85) with a header, the conversation wrapped to the pane width, and the input box `› ` with a key hint at the bottom; Enter sends it as a prompt. The box is a small line editor (`internal/acphost/editor.go`): a cursor moved by the arrows, Home/End, Ctrl-A/E/B/F, word jumps with Ctrl- or Alt-arrows and Alt-B/F, a left mouse click (the pane turns on SGR mouse reports; tmux keeps the drag for its selection, ADR-38, and the wheel scrolls the conversation), Delete, Ctrl-D/K/U/W, Alt-D and Alt-Backspace; Alt-Enter, Shift-Enter (when the terminal reports it, as CSI u or modifyOtherKeys) and Ctrl-J add a line, and the box grows to a third of the pane, ten rows at most; Up and Down move between its lines and, from the first or last line, through the lines sent before (Ctrl-P/N too). A bracketed paste lands at the cursor as one edit and stays one prompt; a paste of more than one line or over 200 characters shows collapsed as `[Paste #1 +N lines]`, is expanded on Enter, and Backspace removes it whole; the pane is drawn once per read from the terminal, not per byte, so a paste of any size lands in milliseconds, #42), a digit answers an open question, Esc or Ctrl-C on an empty line cancels the turn, and Ctrl-T takes the agent over in the tool's own screen (`fleet takeover`). What is typed there is recorded with `by: pane` and marks the agent as the user's, as typing into a tmux agent does. Window, wall tile, park, close and dead-pane reaping work as for any agent, and the agent outlives a watcher re-exec.
- **Status comes from the protocol.** The host writes `status/<name>.json` (the ADR-49 state file) on every change: `session/prompt` sent or a foreground `assistant.turn_start` → working; an open `session/request_permission` → waiting with "<title>: <option> / <option>", which wins until every question is answered; a foreground `assistant.idle` or `session.idle` (Copilot opts in at `initialize`), or the prompt's response → idle; a session established with no prompt (spawn without a task, wake) → idle; the child exiting → error. A tool call or message while idle (autopilot or background work) → working. Subagent events (`agentId` set) and `assistant.turn_end` never change status. Responses are handled in order after the updates that preceded them. Fleet offers the agent no file system or terminal: `fs/*` and `terminal/*` get -32601 and the agent uses its own tools.
- **The refresher never captures an ACP pane.** It reads the state file (with no hook expiry) and the transcript `~/.fleet/acp/<name>.jsonl`: the last line, the session id (into the board's `session_id`) and the last error. `peek`, `peek -f` and the dead-pane report show the transcript tail; `fleet snap` refuses an ACP agent.
- **Control goes over `~/.fleet/acp/<name>.sock`** (one JSON line per request: `prompt`, `answer`, `cancel`, `state`, `stop`). `fleet send`, mail nudges and wake lines send a prompt (a prompt during a turn steers it where the tool allows); Escape and the stuck interrupt cancel the turn; `fleet answer y`/`n` picks the first `allow*`/`reject*` option, a digit picks that option, and the menu shown is the open question's options.
- **Park and wake.** Park and close send `stop` (the host closes the session if offered, stops the child and exits), then kill the window as before; `reapDead` skips parked agents. While park waits for the host (up to `ACPStopWait` plus 5 s), a refresh keeps the entry parked and paneless instead of reading the stopping host's last state: it had turned a park into `idle` and then `gone`. Wake starts a new host with `--session <id>`: `session/resume` when the tool offers it (agy 1.5+), else `session/load` (Copilot, which replays the history as updates the host draws).
- **No prompt vanishes in a failed start.** Prompts that reach the host before its session is ready are queued (`fleet send` succeeds). A host that ends before then (the tool exits, or `initialize` or the session call fails) writes the first prompt and the queued ones to `~/.fleet/acp/<name>.prompt` (0600) and adds an error entry listing them (first line each) to the transcript, so the dead-pane report to the owner names what was not sent. When the agent has a session and is parked and woken, wake passes that file to the next host, which sends them first and removes the file once they are with the agent. A first spawn that never got a session has nothing to wake: the owner resends from the report. Close and a new spawn of the name remove the file.
- **Adopt.** `fleet adopt` resolves the driver like spawn. Under `acp` it starts a host with `--session <id>` on the given session, as wake does; the in-use and session-exists checks are the same.
- **Spend and subagent dots.** The host records each turn's `usage` from the `session/prompt` response as a `usage` entry, and tags tool calls that start a subagent (agy's `invoke_subagent`, named by its `Role`) with `sub`. Copilot keeps its `events.jsonl`, whose dir is the ACP session id, so its spend, dots and last message read as for a tmux agent. Claude's dots come from its plugin's subagent hooks (§4 Subagents, ADR-99). agy keeps no log fleet reads, so its spend comes from the transcript's usage entries (agy counts cached tokens apart from input) and its dots from the tagged tool calls: running from `in_progress` until `completed`/`failed`, or until the turn ends. The transcript is in the watcher's list of event logs.
- **Tests** use `fleet fakeagent <name> --acp [--resume] [--no-load] [--state DIR]`, a scripted ACP server whose prompt keywords pick a scenario (`[ask]`, `[slow N]`, `[hang]`, `[crash]`, `[error]`, `[subagent]`, `[invoke]`; a prompt during a turn steers it; every response carries usage), so no test spends tokens. `internal/acp`'s live tests run `initialize`, `session/new` and `session/close` against `copilot --acp` and `agy acp` when installed (no tokens; skipped in CI); a prompt only with `FLEET_ACP_LIVE=1`.

## 8. My additions

These came up while writing this down and weren't in the conversation.

1. **An attention queue, not just a grid.** With 40 tiles, colour alone doesn't tell me what to do next. `Tab` walks the waiting agents in order of how long they've waited, so clearing the queue means pressing Tab, then `a`, `x` or `s`, until nothing is yellow.
2. **Rules for the admiral.** It can read anything and send ordinary prompts. It must not approve, merge, push to a shared branch, `apply`, or kill agents without me confirming in the TUI. Any action that needs approval appears as a yellow card in the admiral pane, and I approve it there with `a`.
3. **Visible admiral actions.** Every `fleet send` or `fleet spawn` the admiral makes briefly flashes the target tile blue and is written to `decisions.log`. I should never wonder why an agent suddenly changed direction.
4. **Pasting, not typing.** Use `tmux load-buffer` + `paste-buffer -p` (bracketed paste) instead of `send-keys` for prompts. Multi-line prompts don't get sent half-way, and tool input boxes handle pasted text better.
5. **A worktree per agent in the same repo.** Two agents editing one checkout will collide. `fleet spawn --worktree` creates `git worktree add ../<repo>-<name>` and points the agent there.
6. **Adopting the sessions I already have.** The current terminals aren't in tmux. `fleet adopt` resumes an existing session by ID inside fleet, so I can move over one at a time instead of starting from scratch.
7. **Desktop notification when an agent goes yellow** (`notify-send`), with a minimum interval so 10 agents finishing together isn't 10 pop-ups. Optional sound.
8. **Group by repo.** Rows are repos, and `1`–`9` jumps to one. Seeing all of one project’s agents together makes it easier to spot two agents doing the same work.
9. **Recording status changes.** `events.jsonl` costs almost nothing now and later answers questions like "how long do agents sit waiting on me".
10. **Restart safety.** The TUI only reads state and tmux. Killing and restarting it changes nothing. Agents keep running because tmux owns them.

---

## 9. How I'll build and iterate quickly

The approach is thin vertical slices. Each slice is 1–2 hours of work, ends with something I can see and use on screen, and gets a git commit. Fake agents come first, so nothing depends on real agents, tokens or RAM until slice 5.

| Slice | What gets built | I can see/do this afterwards |
|---|---|---|
| 0 | `git init`, Go module, fake agent, `fleet-dev` tmux layout with the watcher, `make test` running | Run `make dev` and an empty TUI appears; saving a file runs the tests and restarts it |
| 1 | `fleet spawn --fake` and `fleet ls` on a separate tmux server; status read by pane scraping | 20 fake agents running; `fleet ls` shows correct status for each |
| 2 | Overview grid: coloured tiles, arrow keys, mouse click selects | 20 tiles in the right colours; arrows and clicks move the selection |
| 3 | Live wall: `Enter`/click brings a pane onto the wall; layouts 1/2/4/9; zoom | Clicking 4 tiles gives a 2×2 wall of live fake agents I can type into |
| 4 | Motion and attention: spinner, pulse, Tab queue, peek overlay, send, approve | Clearing 3 waiting fake agents takes only Tab + `a` |
| 5 | Real tools: spawn and resume for copilot and claude, then agy and vibe; capture real "waiting" patterns | A real Claude and Copilot agent on the wall with correct colours |
| 6 | Park and wake, live cap, RAM guard, `adopt` | 40 tiles, at most 12 live processes, RAM under about 6 GB |
| 7 | Admiral pane, its `AGENTS.md`, summarising `peek`, reset key, approval cards | The admiral reports what's waiting; `R` resets it without losing anything |
| 8 | Control mode events instead of polling, hooks, notifications, worktrees, named walls | CPU use is low with 40 agents; desktop alert when one goes yellow |

Slices 0–3 give a usable tiled wall of fake agents and are about a day of work. Slice 5 makes it useful for real work.

**Each change during a live session:**

1. I say what I want ("make waiting tiles pulse faster", "drop the RAM line").
2. The coding agent in the right-hand pane edits one feature file and its test.
3. The watcher runs that package's tests, rebuilds and restarts the TUI on the left within about 1–2 seconds, on the same tile.
4. If I like it and the tests pass, it gets a commit. If not, `git checkout .` puts back the previous version.
5. Removing a feature: turn it off in `config.toml`, and delete the file once I'm sure.

**Keeping the loop fast**

- Dev runs on the fake agents. Real agents are never restarted by the dev loop, because they run on their own tmux server (`tmux -L fleet`) and the TUI only reads from it.
- Every component ships with its test (§15). Tests run on every save, but they **don't block**: if the code builds, the TUI restarts with it, and test failures go to the log pane and `~/.fleet/logs/fleet.log`.
- Logging is the main way issues get found: every tmux error, status change, spawn/kill and caught panic is logged with the component name.
- One rule for every slice: if a slice takes longer than about 2 hours, it is split, not pushed through.

## 10. Dev loop: change it live while it runs

Goal: the TUI stays open in one pane while I talk to a coding agent in another, and each change shows up within a second or two.

```
┌──────────── tmux "fleet-dev" ────────────┐
│  fleet TUI (auto-restarting)  │  copilot │
│                               │  editing │
│                               │  the code│
├───────────────────────────────┴──────────┤
│  test output + build errors + app log    │
└──────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

- **Save → test → rebuild → restart in about 1–2 seconds.** `watchfiles` (already installed) runs `go test` for the changed package, then `go build`, then restarts the TUI. A small Go program builds in well under a second. A restart costs nothing because all state is on disk and in tmux. The cursor position is saved to `~/.fleet/ui.json` so a restart lands on the same tile.
- **Colours and sizes in a theme file.** `~/.fleet/theme.toml` is reread on change without a rebuild, so visual tweaks take effect immediately.
- **Errors don't kill the loop.** A failing test or build shows in the bottom pane, and the last working binary keeps running. The next save tries again.
- **Fake agents.** `fleet spawn --fake <name> <script>` starts a small script that prints output like a real agent and moves through working, waiting, error and idle on a timer. What it prints is scripted data (`internal/fake/script.go`): a task line, the tool results that task would produce, an approval it wants, a failure and a summary, under a footer redrawn in place. The directives `!work`, `!wait`, `!error` and `!done` hold it in one state so a screenshot or a demo can be composed rather than waited for (ADR-52). The grid can be built and tested with 40 tiles without spending tokens or RAM on real agents.
- **Features as small modules.** Each feature (tile animation, Tab queue, notifications, parking) is one file plus its `_test.go` file in `internal/features/`, registered in a list. `~/.fleet/config.toml` turns each one on or off, and the setting is read at startup. Adding a feature = one new file; removing it = one line in the config.
- **Git checkpoint after every working change.** If a live change breaks something, `git checkout .` gets back the last good state in seconds.
- **Screen snapshot tests from day one.** `teatest` stores the rendered grid as a golden text file. A layout change shows up as a readable diff, and `make golden` accepts it.

## 11. What still needs doing before the first line of code

| Item | Why | Default if I don't decide |
|---|---|---|
| `git init` in `ai-fleet` | Checkpoints for the live loop | Do it |
| Go module with Bubble Tea, Lip Gloss, bubblezone (mouse), teatest | One binary, few dependencies | Go 1.26, already installed |
| Fake agent script | Build the grid without real agents | First thing to build |
| One real pane capture per tool, in each state | Waiting/error patterns for status detection | Collect during step 1 |
| Name of the command | `fleet` might clash with something | `fleet` |
| Summariser model | Cost vs keeping text local vs the RAM the agents need | Haiku 5.5 through the installed Copilot CLI (ADR-40) |

## 15. Tests per component (R16)

Each component lives in its own Go package with its own `_test.go` file. `make test` runs all of them in under about 10 seconds. Tests that need tmux use a throwaway server (`tmux -L fleet-test-<random>`) that is removed afterwards, so they never touch real agents. Every package whose tests start a tmux server or a process runs them through `testenv.Main` (ADR-83): a marked process still alive after the tests fails the run and is killed, and marked processes left by a run that died are reaped when the next one starts. On Linux `testenv.Main` also sets `no_new_privs` on the test binary (with cgo linked in, it re-executes itself to do so). Its tmux servers inherit the flag, so the setgid utempter helper cannot write a utmp/wtmp login record per pane. Such records used to number hundreds per run, and accounts-daemon re-reads wtmp after every write. The real `fleet` server keeps its records, one per pane: the flag would stop `sudo` working in agent panes.

| Component | Package | Test |
|---|---|---|
| tmux adapter (new window, capture, send/paste, join/swap pane, kill) | `internal/tmux` | Integration test against a throwaway tmux server: spawn `cat`, paste text, capture it back, move panes |
| Status detector | `internal/status` | Table test over real pane captures stored in `testdata/<tool>/<state>.txt`. One file per tool per state; a tool UI change shows up as a failing case |
| Teams (groups, colours, membership) | `internal/team` | Unit test in a temp directory: create/delete, add moves between teams, colour auto-pick and parse, survives reload, SameTeam |
| What's new (embedded changelog, `feat` commit fallback, seen marker, build version) | `internal/whatsnew` | The embedded `CHANGELOG.md` parses with days newest first; parse of headings, refs and ids; seen file round trip and new marks; fallback against a temp git repo (HOME and GIT_CONFIG_GLOBAL pinned): uncited `feat` commits only, cited/CHANGELOG-touching/older ones dropped; repo detection from an exe path |
| Board and cards store | `internal/board` | Unit test in a temp directory: write, read, concurrent update, broken JSON recovery |
| ADR number claims (§14, ADR-50) | `internal/adr` | Unit test in a temp directory: bold `**ADR-NN**` entries counted and in-line references not, concurrent claims get distinct numbers, a number on main releases itself, `OnMain` reads the committed DESIGN.md of a temp repo |
| Session discovery (list and search Copilot/Claude session stores for adopt) | `internal/sessions` | Unit test over fake session folders and transcripts in a temp dir: titles, filters, word search, suggested profile |
| Tool adapters (spawn and resume command per tool, finding the session ID) | `internal/tools` | Unit test of the exact command lines; finding the session ID against a fake session folder |
| Local spawn preferences (mode, auto-submit, extra args, local profile layer) | `internal/prefs` | Unit test in a temp directory via `FLEET_CONFIG`: missing file gives neutral defaults, `[profile.x]` beats `[defaults]`, broken TOML names the file |
| Parking scheduler (cap, least recently used, RAM guard) | `internal/park` | Unit test with a fake clock and fake RAM readings |
| Admiral memory (§6.5) | `internal/supervisor` | Fill reads the last usage record from the tail of `events.jsonl`; handoff rotation keeps 20; notes format; colour band edges; reset respawns a fake admiral on a `fleet-test-*` socket |
| Summariser (`peek`) | `internal/summary` | Unit test with a fake model, no network: cache hit when the pane hash is unchanged, rate limit reuses the last summary, a broken model falls back or returns nothing, at most 5 lines out, raw text never returned in admiral mode |
| Approval gate (ADR-9) | `internal/gate` | Unit test: risky actions are held for approval, ordinary sends pass |
| Wall manager (layouts, pin, swap out least recently used) | `internal/wall` | Integration test on a throwaway tmux server: bring 5 agents onto a 4-slot wall and check which one went back |
| Overview TUI (tiles, colours, arrows, mouse, Tab queue) | `internal/ui` | `teatest`: send key and mouse events, compare the screen with a golden file |
| Fake agent | `cmd/fakeagent` | Unit test that its script goes through each state in order |
| Whole system | `e2e/` | Start 10 fake agents, run `fleet ls --json`, check every status; then park one, wake it and check it again |

Rules:
- Every component has a test, but a failing test doesn't block a build, a restart or a commit. Failures are logged and fixed when they matter.
- When a bug is found, the fix comes with a test that fails without it.
- Real tools (copilot, claude, agy, vibe) are tested only by an opt-in smoke test (`make smoke`) because it costs tokens. Everything else runs on fakes and recorded captures.

## 16. Keeping maintenance low (R15)

The biggest maintenance risk is not the language. It's the four agent tools changing their screens every week, which breaks status detection. The design limits this:

1. **Small on purpose.** The target is roughly 2,000–3,000 lines of Go. Anything tmux already does (layouts, mouse, scrollback, resizing) is not rebuilt.
2. **Tool-specific details in one place.** Screen patterns per tool live in `~/.fleet/patterns.toml`, not in code. When a tool changes its UI, it takes one line in that file and one new capture in `testdata/`.
3. **Hooks over scraping where the tool supports them** (Claude Code, Copilot). Hooks don't break when a tool changes how it looks.
4. **Fail soft.** An unknown screen shows a grey "unknown" tile. It never crashes the TUI or blocks other agents.
5. **`fleet doctor`** checks tmux version, each tool's version and resume flag, and runs the status detector against a fresh capture of each tool. It says exactly what broke. *Built (#23) as a static pre-flight: tmux 3.3+, a UTF-8 locale, `~/.local/bin` on PATH, config.toml and tools.toml parse, and the admiral's and each user-defined profile's command is found. Tool versions, resume flags and a live status capture are not checked: those need an agent running.*
6. **Few dependencies, pinned.** Only the Charm libraries (Bubble Tea, Lip Gloss, bubblezone) and the standard library.
7. **No server, no database, no daemon.** tmux, files in `~/.fleet/` and one binary. Removing it is `rm`. *Exception (ADR-66): `fleet web` is an opt-in, stateless HTTP server you start by hand.*

## 12. Open questions

- Where agy and vibe store session IDs, so `fleet` can find them after launch.
- The exact pane patterns for "waiting" in each tool (collect real screenshots of each).
- Which Copilot and Claude hook events give "needs permission" and "turn finished" reliably.
- Default live cap (12?). *(The stuck timeout is settled: 10 minutes, configurable per profile — ADR-45.)*

## 13. Not in scope (for now)

- Agents talking directly to each other. All coordination goes through the board and the admiral.
- A web UI. *Lifted by ADR-66: `fleet web` (§5f) is an optional second front end; the TUI stays primary.*
- Running agents on other machines.

---

## 14. Decisions (ADRs)

Short records: what was decided, why, and what was rejected. Change a decision by adding a new ADR that replaces the old one, not by editing it.

**ADR-1: tmux underneath, not a custom terminal manager** (R1, R13)
Decision: every agent runs in a tmux pane on its own server (`tmux -L fleet`).
Why: it survives crashes of the TUI, supports mouse, resizing and layouts already, and agents keep running while the TUI restarts during dev.
Rejected: a custom pty manager (a lot of work for the same result); agent-deck (switches sessions but doesn't supervise, R14).

**ADR-2: Textual (Python) for the overview** (R3, R4, R5, R12). *Replaced by ADR-10.*
Decision: the overview, supervisor pane and status bar are a Textual app.
Why: CSS-like styling that reloads live, mouse and keyboard support built in, timers for animation, quick to change while talking.
Rejected: Go + Bubble Tea (faster but slower to iterate on and no live styling); a Zellij WASM plugin (tied to Zellij, rebuild needed for every change).

**ADR-3: live sessions drawn by tmux, summaries drawn by the fleet TUI** (R7, R8, R9)
Decision: the wall is made of real tmux panes; the overview never embeds live terminals.
Why: a terminal emulator inside the app for 9+ busy panes would be slow and a lot of code. tmux is native code and already does this well.
Rejected: a terminal widget inside the TUI for every tile.

**ADR-4: state on disk; supervisor stateless** (R2, R11)
Decision: `board.json`, cards and `decisions.log` hold all state. The supervisor can be reset at any time.
Why: a long-running supervisor fills up with every agent's details and gets worse at its job.
Rejected: one long supervisor conversation with occasional compaction.

**ADR-5: park idle agents, resume by session ID** (R9, R10)
Decision: cap live processes (default 12); park the least recently used idle agent; resume with each tool's resume flag.
Why: the measured RAM per agent makes 40 live sessions about 15–20 GB on a 30 GB machine. All four tools can resume by ID (checked 2026-11-04).
Rejected: keeping everything live and buying RAM; killing sessions and starting fresh (loses the conversation).

**ADR-6: summaries for the supervisor, never raw panes** (R11)
Decision: `fleet peek` sends pane text through a cheap model and returns about 5 lines; raw output is only for me.
Why: keeps the supervisor's context small and focused.
Rejected: giving the supervisor the last N lines of raw output.

**ADR-7: scrape panes first, events and hooks later** (R9, R13)
Decision: start by reading the bottom of each pane; move to tmux control mode events and tool hooks in slice 8.
Why: scraping works with all four tools on day one. Events and hooks are faster and more reliable but differ per tool.

**ADR-8: fake agents and live reload for development** (R12)
Decision: build slices 0–4 against scripted fake agents, with `watchfiles` running test, build and restart on every save.
Why: changes show up in 1–2 seconds, and the dev loop costs no tokens or RAM.

**ADR-9: the supervisor needs my approval for anything risky**
Decision: the supervisor can read and send ordinary prompts. Approving, merging, pushing to shared branches, applying infrastructure or killing agents needs my `a` in the TUI.
Why: A supervisor acting on 40 agents can do a lot of damage quickly.

**ADR-11: hand off to a fresh supervisor instead of compacting** (R11, R21). *Builds on ADR-4.*
Decision: the supervisor writes notes to `decisions.log` as it goes. At 70% context it writes a handoff and fleet respawns it with a new session that starts from `fleet_brief`. The fill is read from Copilot's own `events.jsonl` usage records. Warnings are added to MCP tool results, so the rule doesn't depend on the model remembering a prompt.
Why: a fresh session with a brief of under 2k tokens is cheaper and more predictable than a compacted long chat. Fleet controls what survives: the board, notes and handoff, not a model-written summary of everything. The old session can still be resumed.
Rejected: Copilot `/compact` (opaque, keeps growing, can't be triggered from outside); a supervisor summary written by another model (costs tokens; the supervisor knows best what is pending); restarting without a handoff (loses open questions to the user).

**ADR-10: Go, one static binary** (R9, R12, R15, R16). *Replaces ADR-2.*
Decision: write the CLI and the TUI in Go (Bubble Tea, Lip Gloss, bubblezone for mouse clicks) as a single `fleet` binary.
Why:
- Nothing to install or break: no Python environment, no virtualenv and no `node_modules`. Copy one file and it runs. Python version or package updates can't break it.
- Go keeps old code compiling with new compiler versions, and the few dependencies are pinned in `go.sum`. Code that works now will still build in two years.
- Fast: low memory and CPU use, and it handles tmux control-mode events well.
- Testing is built in (`go test`), including screen snapshots with `teatest`.
- The coding agents write Go well, and a small Go program builds in well under a second, so the live loop stays fast.
Cost: no live CSS reload as Textual has. The theme file and a 1–2 second rebuild cover most of that.
Rejected: Python + Textual (quicker first draft, but brings a Python environment to maintain); Rust + Ratatui (fastest, but slower to write and compile); a bash script (fine for a prototype, breaks at this size).

**ADR-12: one key registry; plain words; `Alt-?` for help**
Decision: every hotkey lives once in `internal/keys`. The tmux status hint, overview header, `Alt-?` popup, `?` overlay, `fleet keys` and the §5 keys table are generated from it, and tests fail when tmux bindings or DESIGN.md drift from it. Hints say `Alt-`, not `M-`, and name what the key does.
Why: the old hint ("M-s sup · M-S side · M-1/2/4/6/9/m wall") was cryptic, and three hand-written key lists had already drifted apart.
Rejected: `Alt-h` for help (it did not reach tmux in a live test; `Alt-?` did, and matches the overview's `?`); keeping hints hand-written next to each binding.

**ADR-13: a spawned agent starts ready to work; the mode is a local preference** (R12, R15)
Decision: `spawn` delivers the first prompt through the tool's own launch flag where one exists (`copilot -i`), and otherwise waits for a *detected-ready* pane before pasting — never a fixed sleep. Starting mode (autopilot, plan, accept-edits) is `mode_args`/`mode_keys` data on the profile, selected by a per-user file at `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME/fleet/config.toml` that the repo neither ships nor commits. Repo defaults are neutral.
Why: the fixed 1500 ms sleep lost the first prompt outright (the TUI was not in raw mode yet), and hand-switching every new agent to autopilot was the single most repeated manual step. Keeping it in a local file keeps an opinionated, blast-radius-heavy default (`--allow-all`) out of the repo while still being one line to turn on. Keeping it as profile data means Claude Code's `--permission-mode` needs no new code.
Rejected: raising the sleep (still a race, just a slower one); auto-answering the autopilot permission dialog with keystrokes (ADR-9 — and `--allow-all` at launch makes the dialog not appear at all); committing `autopilot = true` as a repo default.
**ADR-14: the wall evicts by status class, and never evicts a pane that needs me** (R4, R7). *Amended by ADR-31: the `▶` target slot replaces the `#{pane_last}` rule. Extended by ADR-41: the same order sheds panes that no longer fit the readability floor, and still protects class 0 and 1.*
Decision: when the wall is full, the incoming pane takes the slot of the wall pane the user was last in (`#{pane_last}`) via `swap-pane`, unless that pane is pinned or isn't a wall pane. Otherwise eviction orders panes pinned → waiting/error/stuck → working → idle/unknown and takes the least recently used pane of the worst class present. If every pane is protected, the new pane stays off the wall and tmux says so.
Why: last-use order threw away exactly the pane I had come to answer. Status is already on the pane (`@fleet_status`), so this costs one tmux field, not a board read.
Why the last-focused pane first: pressing `Enter` on a tile is an explicit choice, and the slot just left is the one the user is already looking at — replacing it in place avoids a re-tile and never surprises them by taking a different pane.
Rejected: a hard LRU with a manual pin only (needs me to pin before it helps); forcing the new pane on anyway (silently loses a waiting agent); class order even for an explicit `Enter` (it would evict some other pane while the slot the user just left sits there).

**ADR-15: the wall grid is computed from the window, presets are caps** (R7, R9)
Decision: `internal/layout` picks rows and columns so every pane keeps at least 64×17 cells of the wall rectangle and the space is filled; shapes score `min(cellW/min, cellH/min)` rather than area; `M-1/2/4/6/9` only cap how many panes may be on the wall. 5 panes become 2+3.
Why: a fixed 2×2 or 3×3 either wastes a wide monitor or crushes panes below a readable width. One rule covers every window size, and the `Wall9MinCols` special case disappears.
Rejected: more presets (more keys, same problem); tmux's own `tiled` layout (can't keep the supervisor and overview sized, ADR-3's wall must stay one computed layout string); refusing to place a pane when the grid falls below the *comfort* minimum (contradicts "cramped beats hidden" in the same rule — a separate, lower floor does it instead, ADR-41).

**ADR-16: one emphasis pane at 60%, chosen by attention then focus** (R4, R9). *Amended by ADR-31: off by default (`fleet wall emphasis`), attention only, focus never picks it.*
Decision: the longest-waiting pane — or the active pane when nothing waits — gets about 60% of the wall; the rest auto-grid in the remainder. Orientation is whichever remainder grids better, tie to stacked. Skipped when it would push panes under the minimum size. Moves are damped (10s dwell, 30s hold, explicit focus wins for 60s) and an unchanged layout string is never re-applied, so agent TUIs don't reflow every time a status flips. Toggled by `fleet wall emphasis`, no key binding.
Why: tiles of equal size spend the monitor evenly on work that needs me and work that doesn't; without the damping, resizing panes on every status change costs more than it gives.
Rejected: zoom only (all or nothing, and hides the others); sizing every pane by status (layout churns on every status change); an `M-e` binding (the hint bar is full; the next key belongs to pinning).

**ADR-17: teams are a coloured dot and a separate file** (R4, R9)
Decision: a team is a named group with a colour, stored in `~/.fleet/teams.json` (not `board.json`). One team per agent. A team shows as a `◆` in its colour after the agent name on tiles, chips, wall borders and `fleet ls`; status keeps every other colour. Same team = implicit access grant for agent comms.
Why: tile borders, text and pane borders already mean status; recolouring them by team would make a waiting agent hard to spot. A separate file means a running older binary rewriting `board.json` cannot drop team data. One team per agent keeps the dot and the access rule unambiguous.
Rejected: recolouring rows/tiles/borders per team (fights the status colours); a `●` dot (it is the waiting glyph); team fields in `board.json` (lost by older writers); multi-team membership (no single colour; more than the request needs).

**ADR-18: subagents come from the session event log, shown as status-coloured `•` on the tile's bottom row** (R4, R9)
Decision: subagents are read incrementally from Copilot's `events.jsonl` (`subagent.started/completed`, `write_agent`, and a subagent's final tool-less turn, which is the only end signal after many re-wakes; a refused `write_agent` restores the previous state; a new turn of a finished subagent wakes it again), not scraped from the pane. Each shows as a `•` on line 4 of the tile, coloured by the subagent's state with the status palette (green running, grey done, red failed); running dots move as a colour wave on the existing animation tick.
Why: the pane only shows the visible screen; subagent lines scroll off and background subagents leave nothing on screen, so pane counts are wrong exactly when several run. The log is complete, append-only and already has a session id per agent. Status colours keep the dots apart from team colours (ADR-17 left them out of the team palette), and a separate row and a smaller glyph keep them apart from the team `◆` and the waiting `●`.
Rejected: pane scraping (only the visible screen; misses background work); team-palette or per-agent-type colours (clash with teams); blinking by hiding dots (the row changes width and flickers); a separate poller (the refresh loop already runs each second).

**ADR-19: agents may message each other, on granted access, through an on-disk mailbox** (R11, R15). *Replaces the §18 "not recommended" line on agents talking directly.*
Decision: messages and grants live in `~/.fleet/mail/` (flock + atomic rename). Agents reach them through an agent-scoped MCP server wired into copilot/claude spawns via profile data (`peer_args`), with a `fleet mail` CLI for every other tool. Access is requested per pair and is two-way once granted. The target agent or the user decides, the requester never does, and either side or the user can revoke.
Why: the user asked for agents to coordinate directly. Files survive restarts and need no supervisor, matching ADR-4. MCP alone would leave out agy, vibe and agents already running. Grants stop agents from flooding or steering each other unasked.
Rejected: A2A/HTTP endpoints per agent (the tools expose none; needs a daemon); MCP server push notifications (the CLIs don't surface them as turns); tmux send-keys alone (lost on restart and lands in approval prompts); open messaging without grants (the user asked for access on request).

**ADR-20: agents report to the supervisor through the mailbox; reports wake an idle supervisor** (R11). *Builds on ADR-19.*
Decision: `fleet_report` writes a `report` message to the reserved recipient `supervisor` and the latest report to the board. For done, blocked and question, it types one line into the supervisor pane only when that pane is idle, once per unread batch. Auto-closing an agent on `done` is an opt-in local preference, and only applies when the agent's work tree is clean and merged.
Why: the supervisor otherwise has to poll screens to learn that work finished, and markers printed on screen get lost or misread. The mailbox already gives persistence, locking and the nudge rule.
Rejected: scraping a `FLEET-DONE:` line (fragile; the tool UI wraps or redraws it); typing into a busy supervisor (it would land in the middle of a turn or of the user's own typing); auto-closing by default (closing loses the pane; the user must opt in).

**ADR-21: recent changes are read live from git, read-only, in a tmux popup** (§5d). *Replaced by ADR-30.*
Decision: `Alt-g` / `g` / a click on the status hint opens `fleet changes --popup`, which reads main, every worktree branch (`main..branch`) and dirty trees straight from git for the agents' repos, and shows a diff with `git show | less -R`. Only read commands run, with `GIT_OPTIONAL_LOCKS=0`. A status-line click outside the hint keeps tmux's default (`switch-client -t =`).
Why: git already is the record of what agents added; reading it on demand costs about 70 ms and needs no state, so nothing can drift. A popup matches `Alt-?` and leaves the wall untouched.
Rejected: a CHANGELOG kept in sync (fleet has none; `--markdown` prints a pasteable section instead of writing files); an index or cache of commits; listing branches without a worktree (noise from old branches).

**ADR-22: the worker process reaches agents as a playbook prepended to the first prompt at spawn** (R11, R15). See §4 "Worker playbook".
Decision: `fleet spawn` composes `playbook + "## Your task" + prompt`. The playbook is an embedded default, layered with `~/.fleet/worker.md` and the repo's `.fleet/worker.md` (append, or replace with a marker), and can be switched off in the local `config.toml`. It is short on purpose (about 30 lines).
Why: it works for every tool profile (copilot, claude, agy, vibe, fake) through the prompt path that already exists, needs no file in the user's repo, and the user can read exactly what the agent got. The supervisor no longer has to repeat rules in each prompt and can't forget them.
Rejected: MCP server instructions (only agents with the peer MCP server get them, and clients weight them weakly); generating an `AGENTS.md` into each worktree (clashes with the repo's own file and would get committed); a global `~/.copilot` or `~/.claude` instruction file (applies to every session on the machine, not just fleet workers, and differs per tool); a shared skill (agents load skills only when they think of it).

**ADR-23: cap working agents with a spawn queue; throttle toolchains through local env** (R15). *Measured in `docs/perf.md`.*
Decision: a fleet-wide `max_working` cap (default 4) queues new spawns instead of starting them; the overview TUI drains the queue FIFO. Toolchain limits (`GOFLAGS`, `GOMAXPROCS`) are a local `env` preference, not a repo default. Off-wall panes are captured every 5 s.
Why: heat follows concurrently working agents and their builds, not open panes; idle agents use under 1 % of a core. A queue keeps the work without running it all at once. Queuing at spawn is simple and loses nothing; stopping a running agent would.
Rejected: a cap on open agents (idle ones are cheap in CPU; §7 parking covers RAM); SIGSTOP or cgroup freeze of busy agents (breaks tool calls and network streams mid-turn); a built-in `GOMAXPROCS` (repo defaults stay neutral, ADR-13).

**ADR-24: supervisor tools read prompts and press keys, gated like answers** (R11). *Builds on ADR-9.*
Decision: fleet parses numbered pickers from the pane and lets the supervisor answer by option number or press allowlisted keys; any text or key sent to an agent on a prompt needs the same explicit confirmation as `fleet_answer`, and an answer can carry the confirmed question (`expect`) so a changed prompt is refused. Send verifies submission against a `pending` pattern (data, per tool). Peek is cleaned of chrome by default; facts about one agent come from `fleet_info` instead of peeks.
Why: Copilot's approvals are 3-option pickers that y/n can't express, pasted prompts were sometimes left unsubmitted, and full-width box rows made peeks cost tokens for nothing.
Rejected: free-form `send-keys` (C-c/C-d could kill the agent); typing the option digit (not verified on every tool's picker; arrows + Enter, as the pickers' own hint says, are); auto-answering safe-looking options (ADR-9).

**ADR-25: `fleet mcp` re-execs itself in place when its binary changes** (R11, R15)
Decision: before each request the MCP server stats its executable. When the file changed and `fleet mcp --probe` on the new file passes, it saves the unread input to a pending file and `syscall.Exec`s the new binary with the same argv. The new process sends `notifications/tools/list_changed`, serves the pending input and then stdin.
Why: the supervisor's MCP process otherwise runs stale code until a handoff, and stale and current code then fight over the board. An in-place exec keeps the pid and the pipes, so the client stays connected and the chat survives. All state is on disk, so a fresh process loses nothing. The probe keeps a half-written or broken build from killing the server.
Rejected: (a) a thin proxy shim in front of a backend that re-execs (works, but two processes and a protocol relay to maintain); (b) restarting the supervisor CLI with `--resume <session id>` (kept as the manual fallback; needed only when the instructions text changes); (c) a handoff to a fresh supervisor (loses the chat; last resort).

**ADR-26: the top-level chat agent is called the admiral; the old name keeps working** (R11, R15)
Decision: the agent earlier ADRs (4, 11, 20, 22, 24, 25) call the supervisor is the **admiral** wherever a user or an agent reads it: CLI, usage text, pane title, keys, prompts, MCP tool text, logs and docs. Kept as aliases: `fleet supervisor ...` (same handler, one deprecation line on stderr), `FLEET_SUPERVISOR_PROFILE`/`FLEET_SUPERVISOR_DIR`/`FLEET_SUP_HANDOFF_PCT` (used when the `FLEET_ADMIRAL_*` one is unset), `~/.fleet/supervisor/` and `~/.fleet/mail/supervisor/` (moved to `admiral/` on first use, the mailbox only under `mail.lock`, and left as symlinks to it, by `internal/legacy`), and `supervisor` as a reserved agent name next to `admiral`. Unchanged on purpose: the Go package `internal/supervisor` and every exported identifier that existed (`mail.Supervisor` now equals `mail.Admiral`), the `fleet_*` MCP tool names, tmux `@role sup`, and the repo dir `supervisor/` (the admiral's cwd, overridable with `FLEET_ADMIRAL_DIR`).
Why: "admiral" fits a fleet and says it commands agents rather than runs processes. A running admiral on the old binary keeps calling `fleet supervisor reset` and writing the old paths, so both names must reach the same command and the same files during the rollout. Copilot's folder trust is keyed by path, and the live admiral's cwd is `supervisor/`.
Rejected: renaming the Go package now (breaks four in-flight branches that import it); moving the repo dir (breaks the live cwd and asks for folder trust again on the next reset); a hard rename without aliases (the live session still uses the old names).

**ADR-27: the admiral's profile is a choice; a swap hands off, a same-profile restart resumes** (R11, R21)
Decision: the admiral profile comes from flag > env > last choice > `[admiral] profile` > copilot, and can be swapped live (`fleet admiral profile`, `Alt-P`, `fleet_admiral_profile`). A swap to another profile writes an automatic handoff and starts the new one with the boot prompt; a restart under the same profile resumes the same session. A start that dies within 5 s restores the old admiral.
Why: R21 makes Copilot the default, not the only admiral; the user moves between accounts and CLIs during the day. Chat history can't cross CLIs, so the handoff (ADR-11) is the carrier; within one CLI `--resume` keeps the chat, which is also the manual fallback ADR-25 left open. Restoring the old admiral on failure means a typo or an expired login never leaves fleet without one.
Rejected: (a) asking the outgoing admiral to write a handoff and waiting for it (slow, and the user may swap because it is stuck); the pane tail plus any handoff it wrote is enough with the board and notes on disk; (b) a static `display-menu` built at `fleet up` (goes stale when profiles change); (c) last choice below the config default (then a live swap would not survive `fleet up`).

**ADR-29: auto-close judges the agent's own work against a spawn baseline; report wakes are queued, not dropped** (R11)
Decision: spawn records the dir's git baseline (HEAD, dirty-path fingerprints, worktrees) on the board. A `done` agent auto-closes when its dir is not a git work tree, or when, compared to that baseline, it left no new or changed dirty paths, no dirty or unmerged worktree of its own and no moved, unmerged `HEAD`; a git error blocks. Agents without a baseline keep the strict clean-and-merged rule. Optional `auto_close_idle` re-checks idle done agents from the overview poll. A wake line for a busy admiral is queued on disk and typed by the overview poll when the admiral goes idle.
Why: read-only reviewers in a plain folder, or in a checkout with someone else's uncommitted edits, reported done and stayed open with "not a git work tree" or "uncommitted changes", and a report that landed while the admiral was mid-turn was never typed. To the user it looked as if agents didn't report back. Comparing against the state at spawn tells the agent's work from earlier work without trusting the agent's word.
Rejected: (a) a read-only flag at spawn (the admiral would have to set it right every time, and a mislabelled writer would be closed with its work); (b) closing whenever HEAD didn't move (misses uncommitted edits); (c) typing into a busy admiral pane (the text lands in the middle of its turn); (d) a retry loop in the reporting MCP process (it dies when the agent auto-closes 2 s later).

**ADR-28: copy-mode copies go to the system clipboard through `copy-command`** (R6)
Decision: `fleet up` sets the server option `copy-command` on fleet's own tmux server to the session's clipboard tool: `pbcopy` on macOS, `wl-copy` on Wayland, else `xclip` or `xsel` on X11. If none is found, the option is left alone. `set-clipboard` stays at tmux's default (`external`), and fleet's own text delivery keeps its named buffer (`load-buffer -b fleet-<pane>`, `paste-buffer -p -d`), which never writes the clipboard.
Why: with `mouse on`, tmux takes the drag selection and only forwards it as OSC 52, which VTE terminals (Ptyxis, GNOME Terminal) ignore. A copy in any fleet pane went into a tmux buffer and never reached the system clipboard, so the user pasted something older. Piping to the clipboard tool works in every terminal, and in terminals that do honour OSC 52 both paths write the same text.
Rejected: (a) `mouse off` (breaks R6 click-to-focus, border resizing and scrolling); (b) `set-clipboard on` (lets any agent in a pane overwrite the clipboard with OSC 52); (c) telling users to Shift-drag (works, but nobody finds it).

**ADR-30: `Alt-g` shows fleet's own changelog, embedded in the binary, not the pane's git history** (§5d). *Replaces ADR-21.*
Decision: the view reads a curated `CHANGELOG.md` (dated, one line per user-facing change, newest first) built in with `go:embed`, plus uncovered `feat` commits from fleet's own repo when the binary runs from it. A last-seen file marks unseen entries as new. Adding the line is part of done in the worker playbook.
Why: the git view showed whatever repo the focused pane was in, so its content changed with the pane and was mostly other projects' commits; what I wanted was "what can fleet do now". Embedding means an installed binary knows its features without a checkout; the commit fallback catches features merged without a line.
Rejected: generating the list from commit subjects only (many fleet commits are not conventional, and subjects describe the code, not what the user can do); reading the changelog from the checkout at runtime (an installed binary may have no checkout); keeping the git review in the same view (two unrelated lists under one key).

**ADR-31: a sticky target slot decides where a card goes** (R6, R7). *Amends ADR-14 and ADR-16; its colours are amended by ADR-37.*
Decision: the last focused wall pane is the target (`@fleet_target`), shown with `▶` on the pane and its card, kept while focus is in the overview or the admiral. A card click replaces the target whether or not the wall is full; `a` adds, `u` undoes, `Esc` clears, `Tab`/`Alt-n` send the next waiting agent there. Focus no longer moves emphasis, and emphasis is off by default.
Why: clicking around moved panes the user had not touched. The user's model is "select a terminal, then pick what goes in it" (Grafana/OBS scene slots, VS Code editor groups). Before, the same gesture appended on a wall with room and replaced on a full one, and `#{pane_last}` was invisible and overwritten by any focus change. Emphasis on focus resized the slot the user had just chosen.
Rejected: pick a card, then click a pane (arms a mode that eats a click meant for the agent, and is the reverse order); only showing today's rules (with room on the wall a card still appends); Shift+click to add (terminals keep it for selection, it never reaches tmux); drag and drop (tmux delivers the release to the starting pane).

**ADR-32: the overview keeps its height and gives full cards only to agents that need you** (R4, R9)
Decision: full cards for waiting, failing and on-wall agents; one-line rows for the rest; idle and gone agents fold into a chip when even rows overflow; order is stable within a tier and frozen while the user interacts.
Why: past about 14 agents the second card row filled and the rest became an unclickable `+k`. Growing the strip resizes every wall pane and repaints every agent TUI; scrolling hides exactly what you want to click.
Rejected: one-line rows for everyone (loses the context meter and subagents on the agents that need you); growing the strip; grouping by team as the main fix (only helps when teams are used).

**ADR-33: dead agent panes close themselves; the exit code comes from tmux** (§6.7, R11)
Decision: the overview poll reads `#{pane_dead_status}` and `#{pane_dead_signal}` for agent panes (they keep `remain-on-exit on`). Exit 0 closes the agent at once with a `done` "exited, closed" report; anything else is reported `blocked` with the code and the last screen lines and closed after `dead_pane_grace` (default 5m, `0` at once, `off` never). The ADR-29 check still runs, but only adds a "left work behind" note: the pane is closed either way, since the dir, branch and the tool's session stay on disk and `fleet adopt` can pick them up. The first sighting is stored on the board (`exit`) so each death is reported once.
Why: users end agents with `/exit`, and the dead pane then sat on the board and the wall as `error / process exited` until someone closed it by hand. An exited pane holds nothing the dir and session don't; a crash deserves a look first, hence the grace.
Rejected: (a) scraping tmux's "Pane is dead (status N" banner (the format exists, and the banner is styled text that can change); (b) a `pane-died` hook running `fleet` (global per server, needs `fleet up` to re-apply, fires while fleet's own panes die too, and the overview already polls every agent pane each tick); (c) dropping `remain-on-exit` (the pane would vanish with its last screen, and the agent would turn `gone` with no exit code); (d) keeping dirty dead panes open (the work is in the dir, not the pane, so it only hid the problem on the board).

**ADR-34: agents in one team, or one repo without a team, message each other without asking** (R11). *Amends ADR-19.*
Decision: `[defaults] agent_mail` sets implicit access: `"team"` (default: same team, or same git repo when neither has a team), `"open"` (all) or `"ask"` (ADR-19's request for every pair). Other pairs keep the request and grant flow. An explicit grant or denial beats the policy, and revoking implicit access stores a denial. Every message stays logged and readable by the admiral (`fleet_access limit=N`) and the user (`fleet access log`).
Why: asking per pair stalled work: coordinators like fleet-tui-coord waited on approvals for agents the user had already grouped. A team is the user's statement of who works together; a shared repo without a team is the same thing unstated. Across teams the request stays, because teams can be different clients or private projects (a client project next to the public fleet repo), and a message can carry their private data across.
Rejected: `"open"` as the default (leaks across clients); keeping `"ask"` as the default (the friction that blocked work); same repo across teams (a team is the explicit boundary and wins); dropping the pair on revoke (the policy would reopen it at once).

**ADR-35: project-management practice maps onto existing fleet parts, not new ceremony** (R11). *Builds on ADR-20, ADR-22, ADR-34.*
Decision: ownership, definitions of ready and done, dependencies, a standard report shape, early escalation, a RAID log, WIP limits, a coordinator for overlapping work, scope control and a one-line retro go into the worker playbook and the admiral instructions as one line each, using spawn prompts, teams, `fleet_report` detail lines (`next:`, `risks:`, `decisions:`, `retro:`), `fleet_note` prefixes (`risk:`, `assumption:`, `issue:`, `decision:`), `fleet touching` and `max_working`. The mapping and reasons live in `docs/process.md`, not in the injected text.
Why: the user treats fleet as part project management. Agents act on written rules literally, so a practice helps only where it changes an agent's next action; and the playbook is paid for on every spawn.
Rejected: new tools or files for a RAID register, status documents or task boards (`fleet_note`, the board and `fleet_brief` already hold this); estimates, stand-ups and per-task RACI matrices (no action for an agent to take); putting the rationale in the playbook (tokens on every spawn).

**ADR-36: report wakes never type over the user; the badge carries the report, the pane line only wakes** (§6.7, R11). *Amends ADR-29.*
Decision: an unread report shows on the tmux status line (`@fleet_reports`, kept by the overview poll) and as a `✉ N reports` badge in the overview header, both fed by the same unread count and both cleared by reading the reports. The wake line is typed into the admiral pane only when the pane is idle, its input box is on screen and empty (a new `input` status pattern), and no key was pressed in the fleet session for 3 s. Queued reports coalesce into one line. The line is pasted without Enter, and Enter is pressed only when the box reads back as exactly that line; otherwise fleet takes back its own text with Backspace, re-typing any characters the user added after it, and gives up on that batch.
Why: ADR-29's queue typed the line when the pane "looked idle", and idle ignored the input box. Twice in one day the user's half-typed sentence got the wake line glued on and was submitted. A screen is the only view fleet has of the box, so the rule is "type only into a box seen empty", and the read-back makes Enter safe even if a key lands between the check and the paste. The badge means a held wake loses nothing, so fleet can afford to be strict.
Rejected: (a) a timing guess alone (no keys for N s), since a user can pause mid-sentence; it stays only as an extra condition; (b) only showing reports out of band (an idle admiral then sits on a `blocked` report until the user looks at the status line); (c) `display-message` popups (vanish after a few seconds and steal the message line); (d) the overview header badge *instead of* the status line (the header is only visible in the overview pane; the badge now sits in both places); (e) clearing the box before typing (deletes the user's text); (f) retrying a batch whose read-back failed (could type and delete every second while the user works).

**ADR-37: Lavender means "the ▶ target slot" and nothing else; the cursor is an underline** (§5e, R4). *Amends ADR-31.*
Decision: `theme.Select` (Lavender) is used for exactly one thing on screen, the `▶` target mark on a wall pane's border title and on its card. The overview cursor loses the Lavender card border and the reverse-video row: it is an underline, drawn in the cell's own status colour, on the card title, the one-line tier row, the overflow chip and the `+N idle` fold. A `gone` or `queued` cell takes `theme.Text` instead, because Surface2 is 2.5:1 and an underline in it would not be seen; the `▶`, `⚑` and team `◆` marks keep their own colours under the underline. The tmux active pane keeps its heavier border and the bright neutral `theme.Text` instead of Lavender, and the pane-number overlay follows. The `next out` mark drops to `theme.Gone` (Surface2), the role for "finished with". Unfocused panes recede one step, `window-style fg` Subtext0 → Overlay2, while the pane you are in stays `theme.Text`. No new colour role.
Why: after ADR-31 three different states wore Lavender (cursor, active pane, target), so the one state that says "the next card lands here" was the one the eye could not find. Lightness, shape and an underline separate the rest without a second accent, which is the §4 rule in docs/style.md. Overlay2 is 5.8:1 on the base, so an emphasis pane (ADR-16, resized but not focused) is still readable: emphasis stays a size signal, dimming stays a focus signal, and the two do not stack into "big and unreadable".
Rejected: (a) a new colour role for the cursor (a cursor is not a new meaning, and the user rejected every new look pending their own); (b) dimming unfocused panes to Overlay1 (4.4:1 — the resting text colour, too dark to read an agent's output in); (c) per-pane styles so an emphasised pane dims less (`select-pane -P` is wall logic, not theme); (d) restyling `mode-style` and the current-window tab, which are tmux's own modal and tab chrome, not fleet states; the `fleet up` admiral-profile picker is a modal popup for the same reason, and keeps its own highlight (its cursor glyph moved ▸ → ❯ so it cannot be read as the `▶` target).

**ADR-38: a left-button drag selects text in every fleet pane, even over a program that asked for the mouse** (§5a, R6). *Completes ADR-28.*
Decision: `fleet up` rebinds `MouseDrag1Pane`, `DoubleClick1Pane` and `TripleClick1Pane` without tmux's `#{mouse_any_flag}` test, so a drag starts `copy-mode -M` and a double or triple click selects the word or line and runs `copy-pipe-and-cancel` through ADR-28's `copy-command`. Two panes keep the old behaviour: a pane already in copy-mode (the drag belongs to the mode) and the overview (`@role ov`), whose cards act on the left-button release that tmux swallows once it takes the drag (ADR-31), and which holds no text worth copying. Button presses, the wheel and the right-click menu are untouched everywhere, so a pane's program keeps the mouse it actually uses.
Why: ADR-28 wired copy-mode to the clipboard but nothing ever entered copy-mode. Every fleet pane runs a program with mouse reporting on (Copilot, Claude and fleet's own overview all set `mouse_any_flag`), so tmux's default bindings forwarded the drag to the program, and `mouse on` meanwhile stops the terminal from making its own selection. Dragging over the admiral produced no selection anywhere, `Ctrl+Shift+C` copied nothing, and `Ctrl+Shift+V` then pasted whatever was in the clipboard before, which read as "paste is broken too". The A/B is a test (`TestMouseDragOverMouseReportingPaneCopies`): a scratch tmux server, a pane program that turns mouse reporting on, a real SGR drag through an attached pty client, and `copy-command` writing to a file. With tmux's defaults the file stays empty; with these bindings it holds the dragged text. Accepted cost: once tmux takes the drag the pane's program sees the button press but no release, so a TUI that tracks a held button can be a frame behind until the next click.
Rejected: (a) a modifier drag such as `C-MouseDrag1Pane` (discoverable only from the docs, and it is tmux's `new-pane -M`, which §5e already unbinds); (b) Shift-drag in the terminal (ADR-28 rejected it, and a native selection ignores pane borders, so it grabs the neighbouring wall pane's columns too); (c) `mouse off` for agent panes (loses click-to-focus and scrollback); (d) taking presses and the wheel as well (that would break the overview's click-a-card and the right-click menu, ADR-31/32); (e) taking the drag in the overview too (a card click that slips one cell would then do nothing, which is the worse bug).

**ADR-39: spawn gives each agent its own git worktree; a refusal falls back to the dir** (§4, §18 #4, R11)
Decision: when the spawn dir is inside a git repo, `fleet spawn` runs `git worktree add` at `<repo>.wt/<name>` on branch `fleet/<name>`, off the repo's main worktree, and starts the agent there; the board entry keeps `worktree: {path, branch, repo}` and `dir` becomes that path. The worker playbook, the auto-close baseline and `fleet touching` all read the worktree, not the spawn dir. It is on by default, off with `worktree = false` in `config.toml`, `--no-worktree`, or `worktree: false` on `fleet_spawn`; a queued agent carries its own choice to the drain. An existing worktree or `fleet/<name>` branch is reused. When git refuses, the spawn warns and falls back to the dir. `adopt` never creates one but records a linked worktree it finds, so close and the idle sweep name the work left there. Closing still removes nothing (ADR-29).
Why: §18 #4 — parallel agents on one checkout fight over one index and one HEAD, which is the collision the playbook was asking every agent to avoid by hand, and a rule only works when it is the default. A worktree also makes auto-close exact: a fresh one is clean, so every dirty path in it is the agent's own, and the branch makes "merged into main?" a real question instead of a guess about a shared HEAD.
Rejected: (a) failing the spawn when git refuses (an admiral that cannot start an agent is worse than one working in the shared checkout, and the warning is in the log and in `fleet info`); (b) removing the worktree on close (ADR-29 already says fleet never destroys work, and a branch is the only trace of a closed agent); (c) putting the worktree inside the repo (`.fleet/wt/<name>`) — it would show up in `git status`, in every tool's file search and in the repo's own ignore rules; (d) a worktree per *task* rather than per agent (fleet has no task object yet, §18 #5); (e) deriving the path from the branch instead of the name, which would break the playbook's `../<repo>.wt/<name>` and the auto-close rule that already matches on the agent's name.

**ADR-40: the cheap summariser is Haiku 5.5 through the installed Copilot CLI, not a local model** (§6.2, R11). *Settles the open question in §12; implements ADR-6.*
Decision: `fleet peek --summary` and `fleet_peek(summary=true)` shell out to `copilot -p … --model claude-haiku-5.5 --silent --no-custom-instructions --available-tools none --disable-builtin-mcps`, in a temp working directory. The model name, the command, its argv (`{prompt}`, `{model}`), the rate limit and the timeout are `[summary]` keys in `config.toml`; the default is Haiku 5.5. The summary is asked for, never polled; it is cached under a hash of screen + last message + status; one agent gets at most one call per `min_interval`; and every failure is open.
Why: the Copilot CLI is already installed and already authenticated for the user's tool profiles, so fleet needs no API key, no key handling and no new dependency. Measured on 2026-11-05 over a 200-line pane: 4–7 s and about 6k input tokens (0.06 AI credits) per call. The lean flags are most of that: with the CLI's own instructions and tool definitions the same call is 36k tokens and 0.25 credits. A local model was the alternative in §12, and it is rejected: this machine already runs up to a dozen agents and a 30B model would take the RAM they need. `{prompt}`/`{model}` argv keeps any other non-interactive CLI (including `claude -p`) one config line away.
Rejected: (a) local Ollama (RAM on a machine that has none to spare; the privacy argument is weak when the agent screens come from the same models anyway); (b) the Anthropic API directly (an API key in fleet's config, a key-rotation problem and an HTTP client, for the same model); (c) summarising on every refresh (a model call per agent per few seconds); (d) summarising inside the TUI's draw path (a slow model would freeze the overview).

**ADR-41: the wall has a readability floor derived from the screen, and shows fewer panes rather than smaller ones** (§5b.4, R4). *Amends ADR-15, extends ADR-14.*
Decision: a pane may never be smaller than `clamp(wall/4, 40×10, 64×17)` — a quarter of the wall rectangle, bounded below by the size where text stops being readable and above by the comfort minimum ADR-15 scores against. ADR-15's shape search prefers floor-clearing shapes and keeps its old behaviour only when none clears, so the shape rendered and the shape the floor tested are the same one; `m` is measured against main+stack instead of the grid. The preset's slot cap is stepped down to the largest pane count that clears it (`M-9` on a laptop is four panes; the preset itself is not rewritten). An add that no preset can fit is refused with a message naming the window, not the preset. Every apply enforces the floor, so the `client-resized` hook sheds panes when the terminal shrinks, in the ADR-14 class order — pinned, waiting and failing panes and the `▶` target pane are never shed, and a card into the `▶` slot never trips the floor because it changes no pane count (ADR-31).
