Why: ADR-15's "a cramped pane beats a hidden agent" is right until the pane is 41×12, where it is wrapped garbage and worse than not being shown. The user asked for a *flexible* floor rather than one number, and the two ends prove why: a fixed 64 columns would stop a 95-column laptop wall ever holding two panes, while 40 columns on a 249-column ultrawide would allow a 3×3 of unnecessarily mean panes. A quarter of the wall is the size a 2×2 already gets, so the rule reads as "never smaller than a quarter of what you have", and the absolute bound keeps it honest on small screens.
Rejected: (a0) keeping the shape search floor-blind and testing the floor separately (the two disagree: a 77×21 wall scores two 38-column panes above two stacked ones, so the floor would pass a shape the grid then broke); (a) one fixed floor number (wrong at one end whichever number is picked — the reason this sat in §18 #15 waiting for "a floor number from live use"); (b) refusing below `MinPaneW`/`MinPaneH` themselves (that is the comfort minimum ADR-15 deliberately lets panes fall under; a second, lower number is what makes both rules true at once); (c) auto-shrinking the overview or hiding the admiral to buy wall space (the user owns those with `M-o` / `M-s`, and a layout that rearranges itself under the pointer is ADR-31's complaint); (d) evicting protected panes to honour the floor (the wall exists to show the pane that needs me; cramped-but-visible wins there); (e) rewriting `@fleet_wall` when a preset steps down (the cap must come back up by itself when the window grows).

**ADR-42: a status fixture is captured by `fleet snap`, and tools that draw above the box opt into a whole-window scan** (§4, R2). *Completes ADR-36.*
Decision: `fleet snap <agent> <state>` writes the agent's live pane into `internal/status/testdata/<tool>/<state>.txt`, which `TestCaptures` already picks up by filename, so adding a tool's pattern costs a capture rather than a hand-typed screen. Claude, agy and vibe get `input` and `placeholder` patterns and the scalar key `scan = 'window'`, which makes `working` and then `error` win anywhere in the read window instead of only on the bottom-most line. Copilot and fake keep the bottom-most rule.
Why: the three tools fleet could not read all share two quirks that are invisible until a real capture is in front of you. They draw the spinner and the error banner *above* the prompt box and keep a mode footer below it that reads identically busy or idle, so the bottom-most rule called a thinking Claude idle; and Claude's empty box is `❯` plus U+00A0, a non-breaking space, which Go's `\s` does not match, so `InputBox` returned false and ADR-36's wake guard — which only types into a box it can see and read back — never typed into Claude, agy or vibe at all. Neither is guessable: both were found by snapping the real screens. Keeping the captures in the repo means the next tool version that moves its footer breaks a test instead of silently mislabelling every agent. `TestInputBoxCaptures` then asserts on those same files that each idle box is found and empty and each `-typed` one reads its text back, which is exactly the precondition the wake guard checks.
Rejected: (a) making the window scan the default for every tool (Copilot's transcript quotes its own "Working" lines, and ADR-26's bottom-most rule is what stops a quoted line from winning); (b) stripping NBSP in the capture (it would make the fixture lie about the screen, and the same character shows up in typed text); (c) hand-writing the fixtures (the quirks above are precisely what a hand-written screen leaves out); (d) hooks instead of scraping for these three (worth doing where they exist, §4, but it does not remove the fallback path every other tool uses); (e) letting `snap` read any tmux socket — it is restricted to `fleet-test-*` so a capture can never pull in the user's own session. Accepted cost: a wide window needs narrow patterns, because every line of the agent's own output is now a candidate. A tool result it is still reading (`⎿ Error: ls: /nope`) or a bullet in its prose would otherwise read as a crash or a spinner, so these sets match the banner forms only, and `TestWindowScanIgnoresTranscript` holds that line. The price is that a tool error fleet has never captured goes unrecognised until someone snaps it — which is the trade the fixtures exist to make cheap.

**ADR-43: a spawn may carry a task card, and fleet runs its done-check itself when the agent reports done** (§6.9, §5b, R6). *Builds on ADR-20 and ADR-29, base for §18 #7–#9.*
Decision: `board.Agent` gains an optional `Card{Goal, Check, Want, Result, Checked}`. `fleet spawn --goal/--check/--check-exit/--result` and the same four arguments on `fleet_spawn` fill it, `fleet info`/`fleet_info`, the `fleet_reports` task-cards section and one line on the overview tile show it, and the card's text is appended to the agent's first prompt so the worker is told its goal, the command that will judge it and where to write `result.md` — or `result-<name>.md` when the agent has no worktree of its own and shares its dir, so agents in one dir do not overwrite each other's (#17; an explicit `result.md` is treated as the default, since the card cannot tell the two apart and it would collide the same way). When that agent reports `done`, fleet runs the check with `sh -c` in the agent's dir under a 2-minute timeout, stores the exit code, the pass/fail and a 2,000-rune output tail in `Card.Checked`, and only then wakes the admiral, with the verdict on the wake line. The run is claimed on the board (`Card.Started`) so one check is one run however many done reports arrive, and the overview sweeps claims that went stale — a check whose runner died with the agent is picked up and finished rather than lost. `AutoCloseDone` closes the agent only when the check passed. No card means no behaviour change at all: the prompt, the tile, the report and the auto-close are exactly what they were.
Why: §18's gap is that fleet manages sessions, not tasks, and the loop rule the user works by is that a loop without a machine-checkable done condition is not a loop. An agent saying "done" is the agent's own opinion; a command and an expected exit code is a fact the admiral can act on without reading a screen or trusting a summary. Running the check in fleet rather than asking the worker to run it is the whole point — the worker is the party with the incentive to call its own work finished. Keeping the four fields flat and on the board (rather than in a side file) means `fleet run plan.yaml`, reviewer agents and recipes can fill the same struct later without a migration, and the one-line tile render reuses the existing status roles so `internal/theme` and `internal/ui/style.go` stay the single place colour is decided.
Rejected: (a) running the check synchronously inside `fleet_report` (the MCP loop would block for up to the whole timeout on every done report, and the worker's own tool call would hang on its test suite; instead `Fleet.Go` is a hook — `nil` runs inline, which keeps the one-shot `fleet report` CLI and the tests deterministic, and `mcp.NewAgent` sets it to `go fn()`); (b) amending the mail message with the verdict (mail is append-only JSONL by ADR-20, so the verdict lives on the board and reaches the admiral through the deferred wake line, `fleet_status`, `fleet_info` and `fleet_reports`); (c) a separate `internal/card` package (the card is board data with one helper file in `internal/fleet`; a package would buy an import cycle and nothing else); (d) a fifth tile line for the card (the grid's `TileH` is fixed at 6, so the card shares the fourth row with the subagent dots and yields to them); (e) making `result.md` mandatory or writing it for the agent (fleet cannot know what the agent did; it points at the path, says whether the file was written since the agent was spawned — an older file is a stale result from an earlier task (#17) — and leaves the writing to the worker); (f) re-running the check on every status refresh (a check is a side-effecting command — it runs once, when the agent claims to be finished; the refresh only *starts* a run nobody holds); (g) §18 #6's "every spawned agent writes result.md" — the file is part of the card, so an uncarded spawn has no result file and later items must read the path from the card rather than assume one. Accepted cost: the check runs as the user, in the agent's dir, with no sandbox, so a card is as trusted as the command the user typed; and a check that outlives the timeout is recorded as a fail rather than retried.

**ADR-44: parking stops the process and keeps the board entry; the RAM guard takes the longest-idle agent first and queues new spawns** (§7, §7a, R10).
Decision: `fleet park <name>` / `fleet_park` kills an *idle* agent's pane and leaves it on the board as `parked` with its session id, dir, worktree, goal and report; `fleet wake` re-launches the profile's `resume` args on that id in the same place. The guard reads `MemAvailable` and, under `[memory] min_free`, parks one longest-idle candidate per `check_every` and posts a report to the admiral from the sender `fleet`; while memory is low `fleet spawn`/`fleet_spawn` queue with the reason on the tile and the queue does not drain. Candidates are idle, have a session id and are not pinned, so work, a pending answer and anything the user pinned are never taken. Park and wake both claim the board entry under the board lock before touching tmux — park flips it to `parked` and re-checks there, then kills the pane (and puts the entry back if the kill fails), and then the pane's process tree, read from `/proc` before the kill: SIGTERM, 2 s grace, SIGKILL, by pid checked against its start time so a reused pid is never hit (`internal/proc`; close and the admiral close do the same, #26); wake flips it to `starting` and starts `claimed`, as the queue drain does — so two processes can never kill an agent that just started working or start two processes on one session. An unreadable screen is treated as a possible prompt and refuses the park, as does a tmux that answers with an error while its server is up (a stopped server simply means there is no pane to kill). A `parked` entry whose pane is still alive is a park that did not take: the next poll reads that pane fresh instead of keeping the label, and a wake refuses it rather than starting a second process on the session. `max_working` and `[memory]` are read from `config.toml` on every use, the way `agent_mail` is (ADR-34), once per poll outside the board lock. A wake while memory is still under the floor is refused with the reading (`--force` overrides), so a woken agent is not parked again on the next check. The queue drains wherever fleet polls — the overview, the admiral's MCP server, and `fleet ls` synchronously — while dead-pane reaping and report sweeps stay with the overview only.
Why: an idle agent is 300-450 MB of nothing happening (`docs/perf.md`), and this machine runs 8+ at once, so the fix has to free memory without losing a conversation; a session id plus the tool's own resume is all it takes to get one back. Longest-idle first is the only order the user can predict, and it is the agent least likely to be mid-thought. One park per check means the memory each park frees is measured before the next is taken. Queueing rather than refusing a spawn keeps the request, so a fleet under pressure catches up by itself when memory returns. Re-reading the config on every use is what makes the guard tunable at 2 a.m. without restarting a dozen agents. Draining outside the TUI is the difference between a CLI-only fleet that stalls and one that finishes.
Rejected: (a) §7's original "ask the agent to update its card, then keep the tmux window with a 💤 banner" (an idle agent given a prompt is no longer idle, and a parked window that still holds a shell is a pane the wall has to place and `reapDead` has to special-case; the board entry already carries everything wake needs); (b) parking by cap — "starting one more parks the LRU idle agent" (the cap already queues spawns, §7a, so parking on top of it would stop an agent the user never asked to stop); (c) parking several agents at once under pressure (overshoots: one park often clears the threshold); (d) `SIGSTOP` instead of exit (keeps the RSS, which is the entire point); (e) a guard thread with its own timer (fleet has no daemon; the poll that already runs is the timer, and a flock-ed stamp file rate-limits it across the overview, MCP and `fleet ls` processes, claimed only once there is something to park, and handed back when that park fails); (f) caching the config by mtime (three processes, one file, and a cache that lies for a poll — `prefs.Load()` is a few hundred microseconds); (g) draining the queue from every `fleet` command (a one-shot command that blocks up to the ready timeout; `fleet ls` opts in because the user is watching it anyway). (h) queueing a wake behind `max_working` (waking is an explicit user action on a session the user picked, like `fleet adopt`, which §7a also leaves unqueued; the memory floor is the one condition that holds it back). Accepted cost: the guard is on by default, against the repo's "defaults stay neutral" habit, because the user asked for the RAM fix and a guard nobody switches on fixes nothing; `guard = false` is one line.

**ADR-45: stuck is grinding, and the only thing fleet does about it is say so once** (§4, §18 #12, R1, R6). *Extends ADR-36.*
Decision: an agent is stuck when the same error banner shows twice in a row, or when it is working with an unchanged pane hash for `stuck_after` (per profile in `config.toml`, default 10m, `"off"` disables that trigger). The rule lives in `internal/status` as a pure function over one refresh's view plus a small memory kept on the board entry; `internal/fleet`'s refresh loop feeds it and records the transition. A banner counts again only after a poll that read no error at all — not merely after the screen moved — and a `working` screen between two sightings keeps the memory rather than clearing it, but only for two minutes of progress. Stuck shows red through the existing error/failing theme role — no new colour role. The admiral is told once per episode over the ADR-36 report/wake path: a `blocked` report and one wake line. The episode is claimed under the board lock, and only the queue-draining process (the overview TUI) claims it. Nothing is paused, parked or killed; `stuck_action = "interrupt"` is an off-by-default opt-in that sends one `Escape`.
Why: the playbook already tells every worker "the same failure twice, stop and escalate", and the user cannot enforce it from outside the pane — §0 R1 is exactly not having to visit every terminal to find that out. The timer alone was already in the code and is the weaker half: it cannot tell a silent build from a wedged one, while a repeated banner is direct evidence of a loop. Counting a held banner once per poll would have made every error stuck within seconds, and clearing the memory on the retry screen would have made "twice" unreachable, because an agent always does *something* between two failures. Using "the screen changed" as the proxy for a second failure is the same bug in disguise: with `scan = 'window'` the banner wins for as long as it is on screen, so a token counter ticking in the footer would have called a single error stuck within a second. All three were found by writing the tests against the ADR-42 fixtures, and `TestStuckHeldBannerWithMovingScreenIsOneFailure` and `TestStuckErrorMemoryExpiresWhileWorking` hold those lines. Reporting once per episode is what makes the rule usable: a stuck agent stays stuck for as long as it grinds, so a per-poll notification would bury the admiral. The claim lives on the board rather than in memory because several processes run `Refresh`.
Rejected: (a) auto-pausing or killing a stuck agent (ADR-29's line — fleet never destroys work — and a long silent build is a real false positive, so the default must be harmless; the interrupt is there for people who disagree, one key, opt-in, per profile); (b) a new `stuck` colour role (docs/style.md already maps stuck to the failing role, and a fifth red earns nothing); (c) counting a banner once per refresh (every error would be stuck within seconds); (d) clearing the error memory on any screen change (the retry is a screen change, so "twice in a row" could never happen); (e) notifying from every process that refreshes (a `fleet ls` in a shell would wake the admiral and spend the episode's one notification); (f) a global `stuck_after` (a fast linter agent and an overnight build agent want different numbers, and every other timeout here is already per profile); (g) making stuck a report *from* the agent (it is stuck precisely because it is not reporting); (h) letting the error memory live until the agent next reaches its prompt (an agent that failed twice, recovered and then worked productively for an hour would have stayed red the whole time, so the memory expires after two minutes of progress instead). Accepted cost: an agent whose retry loop is fast enough that no poll ever catches a non-error screen is never caught by the error trigger — the no-progress trigger is the backstop, and a retry screen with a live countdown (vibe's "Retrying… (1m51s)") defeats both, because a ticking timer is a changing screen. That is the same limit as §4's long silent build, and the honest fix is tool hooks (§18 #3), not a cleverer hash.

**ADR-46: spend comes from the tool's own session files, cached by size and mtime, and is one column** (§7b, §18 #11, R11, R15). *Uses the reader shape of ADR-26's context meter.*
Decision: `internal/spend` sums Copilot `session.usage_record` events and Claude `assistant` transcript lines into calls, input, output, cache-read and cache-write tokens plus AI credits (`totalNanoAiu` / 1e9), chosen by the profile's `patterns` name. Claude records are deduplicated by `message.id`, because one reply is written as one line per content block with the same usage repeated, and where the transcript carries Claude Code's own running `cost-state` total that total wins over the sum of everything written before it, with later records added on top so a resumed session is not under-counted. Credits and tokens are never added together: a total covering both kinds prints `212 AIC + 30k tok`. A process-wide cache keyed by file path keeps the running total with the size and mtime of the last read, so an unchanged file costs a `stat` and a changed one is read only from where the last read stopped; a shrunken file is re-read from the start and a line without its newline is left for next time. It is shown as one column — credits when the tool reports them, tokens when it does not — in `fleet_status` (plus a fleet-wide total line), in `fleet info`/`fleet_info` (with calls, tokens and cached tokens), and as the `fleet spend` table. No model is called, and nothing on this path runs in the TUI draw loop. Summariser calls (ADR-40) are excluded and every output says so.
Why: the figure already exists on disk, written by the tool for its own status line, so reading it is free and always agrees with what the user is billed; asking a model, or an API, would spend credits to measure credits. Cache keys of size and mtime are what make it safe to call from `fleet_status`, which the admiral runs constantly: the usage log only grows, so the incremental read is exact rather than an approximation, and `TestIncremental` and `TestClaudeIncrementalDedup` hold the incremental and whole-file paths equal — including a duplicate pair split across two reads, which is why the dedup key is carried in the cache entry. The dedup and the `cost-state` preference were both found by reading real transcripts: without them a Claude agent's spend is roughly doubled, which is worse than no figure at all. One column rather than two keeps the admiral's table readable while still being honest about the unit, since Claude records no credits at all.
Rejected: (a) a tail read like ADR-26's context meter (that wants the *last* record, this wants every record, so the whole file has to be seen once); (b) converting tokens to money (the price list is not on this machine and would rot); (c) a spend figure on the overview card (the header is already crowded, §5d, and the card is the wrong place for a number nobody watches per second); (d) polling spend into the board so the TUI could draw it (a second writer on the board for a number that is only read on demand); (e) giving the summariser `--session-id` so its calls could be attributed (it would tie ADR-40's deliberately tool-agnostic shell-out to one CLI's flag, for a figure that is a rounding error next to the agents); (f) trusting the first glob match for a Claude session id (the same id can sit under two project directories after a change of directory, so the newest by mtime wins); (g) a per-task cap now (the cap needs a pause mechanism that §7's parking will provide, so it waits for it).
**ADR-48: `fleet merge` does the merge, and the only conflict it decides for you is the two shared append-only docs** (§4, §18, R1, R6). *Implements the `fleet-merge` skill; extends ADR-39, bounded by ADR-29.*
Decision: `fleet merge` rebases the current worktree's branch onto `main`, runs the checks, merges into the `main` checkout — fast-forward when it can, `--no-ff` when it cannot — and runs the checks again there. A conflict whose unmerged files are all `CHANGELOG.md` or `DESIGN.md` is resolved in place by keeping both sides, then `rebase --continue`; the order is per file, because the two append at opposite ends — `CHANGELOG.md` is newest-first at the top of today's section, so the replayed line goes above the base's, while §14 ADRs ascend at the bottom, so the base's entry stays above ours. Any other conflicting file aborts the rebase and exits 1, naming the files and the other worktrees that change them, from the same `Others`/`Overlap` pair `fleet touching` uses. If `main` moves between the pre-rebase read and the merge, the whole rebase-run-merge is retried, three times by default. `--dry-run` prints the order from branch state alone. It never pushes, never removes the worktree or branch, and refuses to start from a dirty worktree or from `main` itself. The merge logic is `internal/worktree`, so the CLI is a flag parser over it.
Why: eight merges into one `main` on 2026-11-05 produced eight conflicts, every one in those two files, and one agent sat idle 15 minutes waiting for a coordinator to answer a question the branch state already answered (`docs/process-review.md`, findings 2 and 3). Both files are append-only by construction — the CHANGELOG at the top of today's section, §14 at the bottom — so "keep both entries, each at its file's end" is not a judgement call — it is the only correct resolution, and leaving it to each agent spends a round trip per merge to reach the same answer. The retry exists because the gap between "checks passed on my rebase" and "merged" is exactly where another agent lands; without it the merge is tested against a `main` that no longer exists. Rerunning the checks *on `main` after* the merge is the point of the command: that combination has never been tested anywhere else. Shelling out to `git` rather than linking a git library keeps the one external tool the package already depends on.
Rejected: (a) resolving any conflict with "keep both sides" (outside those two files it silently invents code — two agents editing one function get a file that compiles by accident at best); (b) one shared order for both files (§14 would come out descending, with ADR-48 above the ADR-46 that merged first — caught by `TestMergeDesignConflictKeepsAscendingOrder`); (c) matching by basename or by `*.md` (a `docs/CHANGELOG.md` or a new design doc is not the shared top-of-day file, so the match is on the exact repo-root paths); (d) auto-pushing after a green merge (ADR-24: one designated publisher pushes after the public check); (e) removing the worktree on success (ADR-29 — fleet never destroys work, and the user may still be in it); (f) unbounded retry while `main` moves (a busy fleet would spin forever; three attempts and an error that says to try again when it is quieter is honest); (g) running the checks only on the branch (the cheap half, and not the risky one); (h) keeping the logic in `cmd/fleet` (the tests need two real worktrees and a conflict, which belongs next to `Others` and `Changes`). Accepted cost: the resolution assumes the merging agent's line is the newer one, which is true when it is replaying its own commit onto a `main` that already has the other's. If an agent rebases a week-old branch, its CHANGELOG line still lands on top of a newer one — a one-line edit, and the alternative is parsing dates out of prose.

Amendment (2026-11-10, fleet#43): keeping both sides kept a line the replayed commit had removed. A branch that renumbered its ADR in a second commit hit a conflict on that commit too, and main got the CHANGELOG line and §14 entry under the old and the new number (ADR-94 beside ADR-98, ADR-96 beside ADR-99). Now the rebase runs with `merge.conflictStyle=diff3`. A line in the conflict's base that one side deleted stays deleted, and a line both sides kept or both added (today's heading) appears once.
**ADR-49: one watcher keeps status live, and a tool's own hooks are the event source** (§4, §7c, §18 #3, R1, R2). *Extends ADR-23, ADR-42.*
Decision: `fleet up` starts a single detached `fleet watch` process per fleet, announced by a heartbeat at `~/.fleet/watch.json` (pid, time and last successful refresh, stale after 5 s and checked against the pid) and claimed with an `O_EXCL` lock so two `fleet up`s cannot both start one. It refreshes on an event within 200 ms, never more often than twice a second, and otherwise once a second, with no off-wall discount, and it is the process that drains the spawn queue and claims stuck episodes. While it is live the overview TUI stops capturing panes and reads `board.json`, so exactly one process scrapes whatever windows are open. The event sources are file mtimes, not a pane watcher: `~/.fleet/status/`, written by `fleet hook <event> --agent <name>`, and each running agent's session event log. Claude's hooks are installed per agent by passing a settings document to `claude --settings` at spawn — `Stop` → idle, `Notification` → waiting with its message as `waiting_for`, `UserPromptSubmit`/`PreToolUse`/`SessionStart` → working — and a hook state decides the status when the screen matched nothing, or when it is newer than the pane's last change.
Why: R1 is not having to visit a terminal to find out what an agent is doing, and until now that only held while the overview was open: `Refresh` ran from the TUI poll and from one-shot commands, so with no window open the pane border, `board.json` and the next `fleet ls` could disagree for as long as nobody looked. That is exactly how a Claude agent sat on the folder-trust menu under a border that still said "working". Making the fix a daemon rather than a faster poll is what keeps it cheap: the capture pass is the cost (docs/perf.md), so one process doing it once a second with 8 agents is the same bill as today's overview, and the event sources buy latency rather than CPU. Hooks are better evidence than any screen — they are the tool saying what it did — but they cannot be the only source, because only Claude has them here and a tool can be killed mid-turn; "newest evidence wins" is the smallest rule that lets both sources be right. Installing them through `--settings` on the agent's own command line, like the peer-MCP config already is, means fleet never edits a file the user owns and an agent started outside fleet is unaffected.
Rejected: (a) polling faster (CPU scales with agents × captures, and the stale border was a missing process, not a slow one); (b) tmux `pane-activity`/`alert-activity` hooks as the event source (a working agent redraws its spinner constantly, so activity fires continuously for exactly the agents that need no refresh, and window alerts are rate-limited and unreliable); (c) fsnotify/inotify (the repo is stdlib + Charm only, and a handful of stats at 5 Hz does not justify a dependency); (d) writing hooks into `~/.claude/settings.json` (destructive, survives fleet, and breaks the user's own Claude); (e) letting both the watcher and the overview refresh (two capture passes, double stuck notifications and double queue drains — ADR-45 wants exactly one claiming process); (f) a watcher per agent (8 processes to do one process's work); (g) making hooks authoritative over the screen (a killed or crashed tool leaves its last hook behind and the agent would stay "working" forever); (h) having the hook itself update the board (hooks run inside the agent's pane and would need the board lock on every tool call; writing one small file and letting the watcher coalesce is cheaper and keeps the board single-writer). Accepted cost: a fleet whose watcher is killed falls back to today's behaviour — per-command refreshes and the overview — rather than failing loudly; and hook states are per name, so `fleet close` deletes the file to stop a new agent inheriting the old one's status.


**ADR-47: fleet trusts an agent's dir in the tool's own config before launch, and never starts one in $HOME** (§4, R2, R13). *Extends ADR-39, uses ADR-42.*
Decision: the last step before the tool starts — after the worktree, so the dir is final — writes that dir into the tool's documented trust store: Claude Code's `projects["<dir>"].hasTrustDialogAccepted` (plus the two `hasClaudeMdExternalIncludes…` keys, the modal right behind it) in the profile's own `.claude.json`, Copilot's `trustedFolders`, vibe's `trusted`/`untrusted` lists. Each is a read-modify-rename under a file lock, preserving every other key. agy has no such store and relies on the fallback: for up to 10s, ending as soon as the input box is up, fleet watches the pane for a trust dialog and picks its "Yes, I trust …" option, before the first prompt is pasted. The watch only runs for a dir fleet could *not* pre-mark and a tool whose own `waiting` patterns mention the question — watching costs a spawn real seconds whenever a tool is slow to draw its box, and that is a price worth paying for agy, not for every spawn. A dir that resolves to `$HOME` or `/` is not used at all: the agent is started in `~/.fleet/scratch/<name>` instead, and the CLI, the MCP reply and the log all say so. `auto_trust = false` per profile or in `[defaults]` turns the whole thing off. The fallback reads the dialog with ADR-49's unnumbered-picker parser, and `fleet_answer y` picks the first option starting with "Yes" rather than option 1, because Claude's dialog lists "No, exit" first.
Why: a spawn that stops on a modal menu is the one failure mode that defeats the whole point of a fleet — the user is not there to answer it, and the agent burns its slot doing nothing. Writing the tool's own config is the documented, reversible way to say "this folder is fine", it survives restarts, and it costs no screen scraping; the fallback exists because agy has no file to write and formats change. $HOME is banned because it is the dir that makes every tool unhappy at once: Claude asks about it, vibe prints a danger banner (which is how this was found — it turned a healthy idle vibe into status `error`), and every agent started there shares one project identity and reads every dotfile in the house as project config.
Rejected: (a) answering the dialog on screen only (racy, tool-specific, and it has to run before every prompt paste forever; the config write is the stable half); (b) a `--trust-folder`/`--yolo` style flag (Copilot's exists but only in prompt mode, Claude's `--dangerously-skip-permissions` disables far more than folder trust); (c) one shared scratch dir for every home-directory spawn (agents would share state and fight over one project identity, the thing the per-agent worktree exists to stop); (d) refusing a $HOME spawn outright (the admiral's `fleet up` fallback and a user typing `.` in the wrong shell both hit it; a working agent in a scratch dir beats an error); (e) answering only the folder-trust key for Claude — the live check killed this one: with the dir trusted, `claude-personal` stopped on the *next* modal instead ("allow external imports" for the CLAUDE.md files the user's own config pulls in), so the neighbouring `hasClaudeMdExternalIncludesApproved`/`…WarningShown` keys are set too. Claude reads all three from the entry of the repo's canonical root (the main checkout, also for a linked worktree), not the cwd, so fleet writes both entries; marking only the cwd let an agent started in `supervisor/` stop on the imports modal (2026-11-10). The line is still "only the modals fleet's own spawn put in the agent's way", not Claude's permission model: nothing here touches tool approvals; (f) watching every pane for the dialog whether or not the config write worked (measured: it adds up to 10 seconds to a spawn of any tool slow to draw its box, which is every spawn paying for agy); (g) a trust-dialog parser of its own (ADR-49 already reads unnumbered pickers for the admiral; this reuses it, and only acts on a question that is about folder trust).
---

## 16a. Lean admiral (R11)

The admiral gets fleet as MCP tools (`fleet mcp`, wired in `supervisor/.mcp.json`) and nothing else. `fleet up` starts Copilot with `--no-custom-instructions --allow-all-mcp-server-instructions --available-tools fleet --allow-tool fleet`, and in interactive mode (`--mode interactive`), never autopilot (ADR-70). Its rules live in `internal/mcp/instructions.md` and arrive as the MCP server instructions.

The admiral delegates by default (issue #3): a request that produces an artifact or needs domain thinking (design, code, research, writing, filing, opinions) is spawned to an agent without asking first, and the admiral answers inline only fleet operations, status, relayed reports and trivial facts. On 2026-11-09 an admiral wrote a full design inline and then offered to spawn; the rule and a good/bad example sit at the top of the instructions so they outrank the tool notes. An agent writes to an external shared tracker (ADO, Jira, another org) only after the user confirms the exact target; personal and fleet issues go to GitHub `sturlabragason/fleet`.

Measured on 2026-11-04 with the same one-line question: about 160k input tokens per turn with the default setup (global instructions, skills, every tool, and the fleet tools deferred behind a search, so the model fell back to bash) versus about 8–9k with the lean setup, and a direct `fleet_status` call in 4–7 seconds. The admiral can't read Go code or run shell commands, because those tools aren't available to it.

## 17. Development process (based on Anthropic's Claude Code best practices)

Source: https://code.claude.com/docs/en/best-practices (fetched 2026-11-04).

| Guide's point | How it applies here |
|---|---|
| Tell the agent what it can't see; keep CLAUDE.md short | `AGENTS.md` holds only commands, hard rules, conventions and gotchas. `CLAUDE.md` just points to it. Anything corrected twice gets a line there. |
| Write the spec first, then build in a fresh session | This file is the spec. Builder agents start fresh and read it, instead of inheriting a long chat. |
| Give the agent a check to run | `make test` per package, the fake-agent end-to-end check, and a screen snapshot of the TUI. Agents verify by running the tool, not by saying it's done. |
| Rules that can't have exceptions belong in deterministic checks, not prompts | Socket isolation (`fleet*` only) and "never push" go into the code (socket name is a constant, no push target) and later into a hook. Preferences stay in `AGENTS.md`. |
| One agent writes, another reviews | A separate reviewer agent reads each slice's diff against this spec. The builder doesn't review its own work. |
| Run independent work in parallel with subagents | Builder (code), designer (layout), researcher (tool sessions and hooks) run at the same time; each gets a narrow brief and reports back briefly. |
| Course-correct early | Small commits every working piece; I watch `git log` and the running TUI and redirect the builder by message, not by restarting it. |
| Put repeated instructions where they load automatically | Fleet's own workers get the worker playbook at spawn (§4, ADR-22); the admiral stops repeating process rules in prompts. |

---

## 18. Review after the prototype (2026-11-04): what would make it a real agentic fleet manager

State at the 2026-11-04 review: about 3,900 lines of Go. Built now (2026-11-05): tmux substrate, tool profiles, scraped status with real patterns for Claude, agy and vibe, overview grid and tiers, live wall (smarter tiling, §5b: status-aware eviction, screen-sized grid, emphasis pane, a readability floor, the ▶ target slot, pinning and undo), lean Copilot admiral over MCP, a worktree per agent on spawn, agent reports with a ✉ badge, `fleet snap`, and cheap-model `peek --summary`. Missing: park/wake, task cards and result files, workflows, spend, the stuck rule, tool hooks.

The gap: fleet today manages **sessions**. Agentic work is about **tasks** (a goal, a place to do it, a definition of done, a result, dependencies). The admiral can poke panes, but it has nothing to plan against and no way to know when work is actually finished.

### Priority 1: needed for live testing now

| # | Change | Why |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | ✅ built (2026-11-04, see §4) **`fleet adopt`**: bring an already-running session (copilot/claude ID + dir) under fleet by resuming it in a fleet pane | I already have many sessions open. Without this, live testing means starting over. |
| 2 | ✅ built (2026-11-05, see §4 and ADR-42) **`fleet snap <name> <state>`**: save the current pane to `testdata/<tool>/<state>.txt` when a tile shows the wrong colour. Claude, agy and vibe patterns came out of it. | Turns every misdetection during live testing into a test case in seconds. Fixes the "patterns are guesses" gap with real data. |
| 3 | **Claude hooks for status**. **Built 2026-11-05:** `Stop`/`Notification`/`UserPromptSubmit`/`PreToolUse`/`SessionStart` run `fleet hook`, which writes `~/.fleet/status/<name>.json`; installed per agent through `claude --settings`, never in the user's own settings. A `fleet watch` daemon notices within 200 ms, so the board, borders, `fleet ls`, MCP and the overview agree with no window open (§7c, ADR-49). Scraping stays the fallback. | Claude variants are most of the fleet; hooks don't break when the UI changes. |
| 4 | ✅ built (2026-11-05, see §4 and ADR-39) **Worktree per agent by default** when the dir is a git repo: `spawn` creates `git worktree add` on branch `fleet/<name>` | Agentic work runs several agents on one repo; shared checkouts collide. |

### Priority 2: tasks, not sessions

| # | Change | Why |
|---|---|---|
| 5 | ✅ built (2026-11-05, see ADR-43) **Task card on spawn**: goal, **done-check** (command + expected exit code), result file. Stored in the board; `fleet spawn --goal/--check/--check-exit/--result` and the same arguments on `fleet_spawn`. A card may also carry `may` (2026-11-09, fleet#32): standing OKs the user gave, such as merge-local, push-main or deploy, free text, on `fleet_spawn` and `fleet_squad_spawn`. A card with only `may` is still a card. The first prompt gets "Permissions: you may <list> without asking. Ask before anything else public or irreversible.", and the spawn reply and `fleet_info` show the list. | Same rule as my loop guide: no machine-checkable done condition, no loop. Lets fleet mark a tile ✅ done only when the check passes, not when the agent says so. |
| 6 | ✅ built (2026-11-05, see ADR-43) **Result file**: a carded agent is told to write `result.md` in its own dir (`result-<name>.md` in a shared dir) (what changed, branch, open questions) when it finishes; `fleet_info`/`fleet_reports` give the admiral the path and say whether it exists | The admiral reads results instead of screens: cheaper, and nothing gets lost when an agent is closed. |
| 7 | **`fleet run plan.yaml`**: a small workflow file: tasks with profile, dir, prompt, done-check and `after:` dependencies. Fleet starts tasks as their dependencies finish, up to the live cap. The admiral can write these plans via an MCP tool (`fleet_plan`). | This is the actual agentic workflow: fan out, wait, fan in. Keeps the plan on disk (ADR-4), so an admiral reset loses nothing. |
| 8 | **Review step as a task type**: after an implement task passes its check, fleet can spawn a reviewer agent (different profile/model) on the same worktree | Writer and reviewer separated, as in the Claude Code guide. |
| 9 | **Recipes**: named spawn presets (`review-pr`, `implement-story`, `fix-ci`) = profile + prompt template + done-check | One keypress or one admiral call instead of re-typing prompts. |

### Priority 3: running 40 at once

| # | Change | Why |
|---|---|---|
| 10 | ✅ built (2026-11-05, see §7 and ADR-44) **Park/wake + live cap + RAM guard**: `fleet park`/`fleet wake` and `fleet_park`, a `[memory]` guard that parks the longest-idle agent and queues spawns while RAM is low, live re-read of `max_working` and `[memory]`, and the queue draining from the MCP server and `fleet ls` too. | Still the RAM fix; needed beyond about 15 real agents. |
| 11 | ✅ built (2026-11-05, see §7b and ADR-46) **Spend per agent**: `fleet spend`, a spend column in `fleet_status` and a line in `fleet info`, read from Copilot `events.jsonl` and Claude transcripts. The per-task cap waits for parking (§7); the tile stays clear (crowded header, §5d). | Agentic workflows can burn credits silently. |
| 12 | **Stuck rule**. **Built 2026-11-05:** same error banner twice in a row, or no screen change for `stuck_after` (default 10m) → tile red and the admiral told once per episode; nothing is paused unless `stuck_action = "interrupt"` (§4, ADR-45). | Mirrors the "same failure twice, stop" rule; stops grinding. |
| 13 | **Waiting agents come to me**: optional auto-placement of the longest-waiting agent on the wall, plus a desktop notification | Less hunting; the wall shows what needs me. |
| 14 | **Admiral reset key + context tile** (§6.3, §6.5). **Built 2026-11-04:** notes, handoff, context meter, `M-R`/`R` reset (ADR-11). | The lean admiral is cheap now, but long days still fill it. |
| 15 | **A hard floor under the wall grid**. **Built 2026-11-05:** a floor derived from the wall rectangle, presets step down, adds are refused with a reason, recomputed on resize (§5b.4, ADR-41). | §5b.2 preferred a cramped pane to a hidden agent; past some point a 41×12 pane is just wrapped garbage. |
| 16 | ✅ built (2026-11-04, see §5e and ADR-31) **Pin a pane to the wall** (`p`, the right-click menu or `@fleet_pin`): a pinned pane is never replaced or evicted | §5b.1 is built around it; it is the one key binding worth spending (§5b.3 deliberately skipped `M-e`). |

### Not recommended

- Auto-answering approval prompts. It saves clicks but removes the gate (ADR-9). At most, an allowlist per recipe, later.
- ~~Agents talking to each other directly.~~ Superseded by ADR-19 (§6.6): allowed on granted access. Task cards and result files stay the main way to coordinate work.

### Suggested order for the next builder slices

1, 2 and 4 are built; 5 + 6, 10, 11 and 12 are in flight. Then 3 → 7 → 9, and the rest as live testing shows the need.

### Future work: remote agent runtime (idea only, not scheduled)

Run the agents in the cloud instead of on the laptop, which overheats with many agents. The goal is to run the existing CLI agents (copilot, claude and the rest) unchanged, each in its own container or pod with the full context it needs: repo checkout or worktree, tool config, MCP servers, credentials and session state. The local fleet TUI and admiral attach to their terminals remotely. A Talos Linux Kubernetes cluster is one possible host. This is a large addition that needs infrastructure prepared outside this repo: the cluster, auth and secrets, networking, and a cost budget.

The runtime is an open choice:
- A plain pod per agent, attached through tmux or a TTY.
- Devcontainers.
- Something similar.
- kagent was considered and is probably not suitable: it runs its own agent framework instead of the existing CLIs.

Open questions:
- How panes and tmux map to pods.
- Session persistence when a pod restarts.
- Secret handling for agent credentials.
- Latency of the live wall over the network.
**ADR-50: an ADR number is claimed from a lock, not read out of DESIGN.md** (§14, §17, R11)
Decision: `fleet adr claim [--for AGENT]` takes the next free number and prints it, under `~/.fleet/adr.lock` with the claims in `~/.fleet/adr-claims.json` (flock + atomic rename, the `internal/board` pattern, in its own `internal/adr` package). Next free is `max(highest **ADR-NN** entry in DESIGN.md on main, highest live claim) + 1`: the number is free only when nobody on disk and nobody on main has it. A claim releases itself the moment its ADR lands on main — no bookkeeping after a merge — or by hand with `fleet adr release N`; `fleet adr ls` shows what is out. §14 entries are bold paragraphs, so the parser anchors `^\*\*ADR-([0-9]+)` and an in-line *Amends ADR-29* never counts as an entry. The admiral gets the same thing as `fleet_adr` (`claim`, `list`, `release`), so the number goes into the spawn prompt — the prompt stays the source of truth for a worker, and `fleet adr claim` is the fallback when it gave none. A fresh `~/.fleet` starts from the eight numbers (43–50) handed out by hand on 2026-11-05, so the new counter cannot re-issue a number an agent is already writing — but only when main is fleet's own DESIGN.md, because in another repo those eight would be claims that repo can never release. The store is shared by every repo, so "on main" filters on read and only `Release` deletes: a claim in one repo can never erase a live claim made in another.
Why: `docs/process-review.md` finding 1. The convention was "the prompt assigns it", and when it did not, four agents on one day each read §14, each found the same highest entry and each took the next one — the renumbering 38 → 40 → 41 was paid in merges, not in design. The race is the gap between reading the file and merging it, which no instruction can close because it is minutes wide; a lock closes it to microseconds. Reading main rather than the working tree is what makes "free" the same answer in every worktree at once, and it is also why release needs no step: a merged ADR is proof the number is spent. The claims live in `~/.fleet`, next to the board and the mail that every fleet process already shares, so two agents in two worktrees of the same repo contend on one file.
Rejected: (a) keeping the convention and writing it more firmly in the playbook (it is already there — the failure was not a missing rule but an unenforceable one); (b) one file per ADR in the repo, claimed by `git add` (it moves the race onto the branch, where two unmerged branches can still hold the same number); (c) claiming from a counter alone, ignoring DESIGN.md (the counter would drift the first time an ADR was written without a claim, and it cannot be rebuilt from the repo); (d) putting the claims in the repo (a claim is in-flight state for one machine's fleet, like the board, and it would conflict in exactly the files this ADR exists to stop conflicting); (e) extending `internal/board` (the board is agents, the lock is per-store, and a second writer there costs every other agent's refresh); (f) auto-claiming inside `fleet_spawn` (not every task writes an ADR, and a number taken for a task that never needs one is a gap in the numbering nobody can explain later). Accepted cost: one store serves every repo, so `fleet adr ls` in repo A also lists repo B's live claims, and a claim made in B never self-releases when read from A — B's number is not in A's DESIGN.md, so only `fleet adr release N` clears it. A number abandoned without that release stays out. Numbering per repo would need the claim to record the repo and every read to resolve it; listing a neighbour's handful of claims is the cheaper half of that trade.
Amendment (2026-11-10, fleet#40): a renumber goes through the same lock. On 2026-11-10 one ADR went 85 → 86 → 87 on main: a merge that found its number taken renumbered by hand to "the next free one in DESIGN.md", which was another agent's live claim, so that agent had to renumber too. The lock was never asked. Now `fleet merge` (and `--dry-run`, with `adr_taken` in `--json`) compares the **ADR-NN** entries the branch adds since its merge base with main against the entries on main and the live claims, and stops before the rebase when one is taken: "ADR-N is taken (already on main | claimed by AGENT): run `fleet adr claim` and renumber your ADR to the number it prints". A claim for this agent (its pane, or the branch name without `fleet/`) or for nobody is not a collision. It does not claim or rewrite DESIGN.md itself: a merge rerun until it passes would leak a claim per run, and the renumber touches the §14 entry, the CHANGELOG bracket and the commit, which are the agent's to edit. The same day showed the other side: an agent claimed ADR-88 at ~21:00 and another merged its own ADR-88 at 21:08, after which `fleet adr ls` showed no claim — a number on main counts as landed whoever wrote it. The check covers both sides: the merge that would land a number another agent holds stops, and the claimant whose number arrived on main under someone else is told at its own `fleet merge` that it is taken. Rejected: auto-claiming inside `fleet merge` (leaks a number per retry and still leaves the edit to the agent).




**ADR-51: a wake is retried until it is typed, and every report gets one** (§6.7, §7c, R11). *Amends ADR-36, ADR-49.*
Decision: four rules the wake path was missing. (a) The nudge mark for the admiral is the newest message id a wake has *announced*, not "nudged since the inbox was last read": `ClaimNudgeUpTo` claims a batch by its newest id, so a report that lands after a wake is announced even while the earlier ones sit unread, and `UnclaimNudgeTo` winds the mark back to what it replaced rather than clearing it. (b) Nothing takes a wake off the queue except delivering it, the admiral reading that report, or giving up on it: a failed paste, an Enter guard that found something else in the box, and a claim that could not be taken all put the entries back, wind the claim back and log it. (c) The retry is bounded — `WakeMaxTries` (3) typed attempts per report, counted in `wake-pending-tries` rather than in the queue line (a build from before ADR-51 reads the fourth field as the whole line, and both builds read this file during a rollout), then an `ERROR` line naming the report and the badge carries it alone — and so is the logging: the "waiting for an idle admiral" warning is at most one a minute per process, and a queue the admiral has already been told about is left untouched, so a 1 s poll neither floods the log nor rewrites the queue file every second. (d) Typing into the pane is serialised across processes by a `flock` on `wake-pending.lock`, taken `LOCK_NB` and held across take → paste → Enter guard, and the pane is read again under it: the reporting agent's own process and the watcher can otherwise reach the box in the same second with two different reports and backspace each other's text. The retry itself needs a resident poll, so a resident `fleet watch` sets `Housekeeping` — admiral wakes, the done sweep, unclaimed done-checks, dead-pane reaping (`--once` does not: a one-shot must not auto-close an agent or run somebody's done-check) — and the overview delivers wakes even on the polls where it reads `board.json` instead of refreshing. *Amended 2026-11-10:* the overview no longer delivers wakes while a watcher is live; the watcher's housekeeping does, so one resident process owns each box's waker (see ADR-75).
Why: on 2026-11-05 four done reports (12:59–13:26) never reached the admiral; it found them at 14:26 only because the user asked. Two independent faults, both of which had to be fixed: ADR-49 moved the poll into the watcher, but housekeeping stayed on the overview — which since ADR-49 skips its own refresh while a watcher is live — so after 13:06 *no process anywhere* retried a wake; and ADR-36's one-wake-per-unread-batch rule meant that once the admiral left one report unread, every later report's queue entry was taken and dropped on the spot. The admiral is idle between turns by design, so "deliver when idle" only works if something keeps trying; and an unread report is the normal state of a busy admiral, so it must never mute the next one.
Rejected: (a) waking per unread *count* rather than per id (the count is the same after a read as before a send, so it cannot tell a new report from an old one); (b) relying on the queue file alone to serialise typing (moving the queue aside stops two processes typing the *same* report, not two of them typing *different* ones into one box, which is why the flock is there as well); (c) having the overview keep housekeeping and the watcher stay a pure refresher (the overview is closed most of the time, which is precisely when the wake is the only signal); (d) dropping a leftover queue when the claim is refused (that is the bug, written down as a rule); (e) a desktop notification instead (the admiral is an agent, not a person: the only thing it reads is its pane and its mailbox).
**ADR-52: the fake agent prints scripted work, and a screenshot is composed from directives** (§5, R12)
Decision: `internal/fake` keeps its state machine (ADR-8) but its output is scripted data in `internal/fake/script.go`: each task carries the tool results a coding agent would print (`read`, `edit`, `go test`), the thing it asks approval for, how it fails and how it finishes, and the admiral's pool prints `fleet_*` calls and a short written report instead. The working footer (`⠹ working · 12s · esc to interrupt`) is redrawn in place with `\r\x1b[2K`, so a pane holds a readable transcript rather than one line per tick. A fake agent also takes four directives on stdin, `!work [task]`, `!wait [task]`, `!error [task]` and `!done [task]`: each clears the pane, prints the work that would have led to that state, holds it, and turns the random timer off. `scripts/screenshot.sh` uses them to compose the hero: a 2×2 wall of one working, one waiting on you, one failed and one done, with its geometry, font and output path overridable (`SHOT_COLS`, `SHOT_ROWS`, `SHOT_FONT`, `SHOT_OUT`, `SHOT_NAME`, `SHOT_GIF`) and the render grid pinned so every gif frame is the same size.
Why: the screenshot is the first thing a reader sees, and the old one sold the wrong product: eight panes repeating "Thinking… step N of tidy README", half of them empty, because the only thing a fake agent printed was a spinner line and the shot was whatever the random timers happened to show after a fixed sleep. Both halves had to change. Scripted output makes a pane look like work, which is what the reader is being asked to believe; directives make the picture reproducible, so regenerating after a palette or layout change gives the same composition instead of a new roll of the dice. The same two changes pay off outside the docs: `make dev` now shows eight different believable agents, and a status-pattern fixture captured with `fleet snap` is closer to the real tools it stands in for.
Rejected: (a) a recorded asciinema cast replayed into the pane (another dependency and another artefact to keep in step with the TUI, and it cannot show the live wall responding to a real `fleet send`); (b) hand-editing the captured ANSI before rendering (it would drift from what the binary prints, and the caption promises a real capture); (c) a `--scenario` flag read from a file at spawn (a fake agent is a development toy, and a file format for it is more surface than the four directives need); (d) freezing the clock so the shot is byte-identical between runs (the elapsed seconds in tile ages and footers are what make it look alive, and a diff of a PNG is not reviewed anyway). Accepted cost: the fake agent now carries about 150 lines of prose data that nothing tests for accuracy, and the step lines are kept at or under 50 columns by hand so they survive a quarter-width wall pane; a much narrower terminal will still wrap them.

**ADR-53: an agent can lead its own squad, and reports climb one level at a time** (§6.11, §6.7, R1, R11). *Extends ADR-20, ADR-36, ADR-51; bounded by ADR-24.*
Decision: a board agent may be marked `leader`, which gives it a mirror team of its own name and eight squad-scoped MCP tools (spawn, profiles, status, info, peek, send, reports, close), each refusing any agent that is not its own. Its agents' reports go to the leader's inbox and wake the leader's pane with the same machinery the admiral's wakes use, generalised from one admiral mailbox to one queue, try counter and lock per mailbox, with the four-field queue line of ADR-51 unchanged. The admiral gets the leader's summary, not the squad's six reports. Caps live in `[squad]`: `max_members` 4 and `max_depth` 1, so a squad agent cannot itself lead; `max_working`, spend and memory treat a squad agent as any other agent. A leader may not answer a permission prompt and has no `fleet_answer`: prompts, merges, pushes and infra escalate squad → leader → admiral → user. Closing a leader detaches its agents rather than killing them, unless `--squad` is passed.
Why: the admiral's scarce resource is attention, and §6.7 spends it per agent. A six-agent feature is six spawns, six task cards, six report wakes and six merge decisions in one context, which is the overload §6 exists to stop. One extra level turns that into one spawn and one report, and the leader is where the detail belongs because it is the agent that wrote the brief. Reusing the wake path rather than forking it was the whole design constraint: the admiral's wake took five tries to get right (ADR-36, ADR-51), and the only honest way to wake a second kind of recipient is to make the recipient a parameter. Depth 1 by default because the second level already buys the summarising, and each further level multiplies the ways a report can be lost while adding a layer of telephone. The safety line is the same one ADR-24 drew for the admiral: an agent may not approve on the user's behalf, and a leader is an agent.
Rejected: (a) a separate `reports.<leader>` mailbox (mailbox names are validated agent names, and a leader's inbox already sorts by kind — one box means `fleet_inbox` stays the one place a report is marked read); (b) a fifth field in the wake queue line for the recipient (ADR-51 pinned the format for rollout, and the path already knows whose box it took the line from); (c) giving leaders the full admiral tool set filtered at call time (the model should not see a tool it may never use, so `tools/list` is what differs); (d) letting a leader answer its own agents' prompts (it is the one escalation that cannot be undone, and a leader is no better placed than the admiral to judge it); (e) unlimited depth (a chain of leaders is a chain of summaries, and the user asked for one level); (f) a real nested fleet per leader — separate board, separate session (it would hide squad agents from `max_working`, the spend guard, the memory guard and the wall, all of which the user needs across the whole fleet); (g) killing a leader's squad with it by default (ADR-29: fleet never destroys work the user did not ask it to). Accepted cost: a leader that never reads its inbox leaves its squad's reports unread with no badge anywhere, since the badge and status line stay admiral-only; the admiral finds out through the leader's own status, or when it peeks.

**ADR-54: an overview frame always fills its pane** (§5, §5b, R4)
Decision: `View` builds the header and the body, then `fitPane` cuts every line to the pane width and pads the frame with blank lines to the pane height, so each frame rewrites every row of the overview pane. Nothing else changes about the grid or the tiles.
Why: on 2026-11-05 four of six agents were closed at once and the overview kept a second row of half-drawn tiles below the two live ones — ctx/pool bars and `✓ done:` reports from agents that no longer existed, with doubled `││` edges and bottom borders cut at odd widths. The cause is in two halves. (a) The overview is rendered by bubbletea's standard renderer, which erases below the frame only when the new frame has *fewer lines* than the last one. A frame whose lines were wider than the pane (here 7 tiles of 42 columns = 294 in a 289-column pane, rendered in the window between tmux resizing the pane and the `WindowSizeMsg` arriving) wraps, so 7 logical lines occupy 13 physical rows; the next frame has 7 logical lines too, the renderer sees no shrink, and the 6 wrapped rows are never erased. (b) Even without a wrap, a frame that is shorter than the pane relies entirely on that one erase, so any miscount leaves the old rows on screen for good. Padding to the pane height makes the frame self-correcting: whatever is on a row, the next frame overwrites it. The width clamp is the belt to those braces: bubbletea already cuts each line to the width it was last told, so it does not help in the race above (both widths are stale together), but it does catch the other way a line can outgrow the pane — a tile whose content measures wider than the grid width (a glyph the width tables and the terminal disagree about, a lipgloss block that grows past the width it was given), and a renderer that has no width yet.
Rejected: (a) sending `tea.ClearScreen` on every resize (it flashes the pane, and it only covers the resize path — a stale `r.width` or a mis-measured glyph would still wrap on a pane that never resized); (b) fixing the line accounting inside bubbletea (a vendored patch to a dependency, against the stdlib-plus-Charm rule); (c) padding each line out to the full width instead of appending blanks (it writes 289 spaces per row every 200 ms and defeats the renderer's unchanged-line skip). Accepted cost: `View` now always returns exactly `m.h` lines, so tests that counted the content rows count the non-blank prefix instead.

**ADR-55: the palette is a named theme, chosen in config, and Mocha stays the default** (§5, §5c, §14 ADR-37, R4)
Decision: `internal/theme` holds a `Palette` struct whose fields are roles, not shades — the grounds `Base`/`Mantle`/`Crust`/`Surface0-2`, the inks `Text`/`Subtext0`/`Overlay2-0`, the statuses, `Select`, `Supervisor` and a six-colour `Team` list — and five named palettes fill it: `catppuccin` (Catppuccin Mocha, the default and unchanged), `stealth`, `neon-drive`, `phosphor` and `graphite`. `theme = "<name>"` under `[defaults]` in config.toml is resolved once in `run()`, before anything renders; an unknown name stops fleet rather than falling back. The package-level role names stay, as variables rather than constants, so the call sites in `cmd/fleet` and `internal/ui` are unchanged; anything that *caches* a colour — a lipgloss style, a tmux border-format string — rebuilds it from a `theme.OnChange` hook instead. Switching costs no agents: `fleet up` re-runs the tmux `set-option` block and respawns only the overview pane, which it recognises as stale by the `@fleet_theme` option it stamps on it. Team colours live in the palette, so Phosphor gets six steps of its green→teal ramp and Graphite six steps of its grey ramp, told apart by lightness and by the team name the overview already prints; `theme.TeamHex(name)` replaces the old raw-hue constants. `internal/theme.Contrast` is the WCAG ratio, and the palette test holds every role to a floor on its own theme's base, keeps `gone` dimmer than `idle` and `idle` dimmer than `text`, keeps `Overlay2` exactly one step between `Overlay1` and `Text` (ADR-37's dim rule), and refuses a team colour or a status that equals `Select`.
Why: the user asked to see four directions in the real TUI, not in a PNG, and the only way to decide between them is to live in each for a day. Shipping the selector with the default unchanged makes that free: nobody who does not set the key sees any difference, and picking a winner later is a one-line change to that default. Roles rather than hues is what makes a swap possible at all — `theme.Yellow` means nothing on a grey theme, while `theme.Waiting` means the same thing everywhere — and it is why the raw Catppuccin names had to go. Variables plus a change hook, rather than threading a palette through every render, was chosen because the alternative is a refactor of every signature in `internal/ui` for a value that is constant for the life of the process.
Rejected: (a) a `fleet theme <name>` command that re-themes a running fleet (the overview, the admiral's own tool and every agent's TUI cache their colours; re-running `fleet up` already does the honest 90%); (b) a silent fallback for an unknown name (a typo would mean "the theme did not change" with no way to tell it from "this theme looks like that"); (c) one accent hue outside the theme for teams on Phosphor and Graphite (it breaks the single promise each of those themes makes — that the only non-green thing is attention, that the only colour is an alarm); (d) a letter inside the `◆` team mark (it needs a change in the tmux border format and three render paths for a problem nobody has yet: six teams on a grey theme). Accepted cost: a team's colour is persisted as a hex, so teams made before a switch keep the old palette's hexes until `fleet team color` is run again, and a colour *name* from another palette (`sapphire` on Graphite) is rejected — the hex form always works; six greys are harder to tell apart at a glance than six hues, so Graphite users with many teams lean on the name; the role variables are process-global mutable state, which is safe here only because `Load` runs once, before the first frame, and the test that proves the hook fires is the thing that keeps it that way.

**ADR-56: `auto` is one mode name across every tool, and unattended is still a local choice** (§4, §14 ADR-13, R12, R15)
Decision: every built-in profile defines a `mode_args` key named `auto` that maps to its own tool's allow-all flag: `--allow-all --autopilot` (Copilot), `--permission-mode bypassPermissions` (Claude Code), `--dangerously-skip-permissions` (agy), `--auto-approve` (vibe), and an empty arg list for the fake agent, which has no permission prompts. agy and vibe get `mode_args` at all for the first time, so they also gain `accept-edits`/`plan` and `smart-approve`/`ask`. The tool-specific names (`autopilot`, `bypass`) stay as aliases. The mode is applied on the resume paths too, `fleet wake` and `fleet adopt`, because each starts a fresh process that has forgotten it. The admiral is deliberately left out: it is the one session that must still ask the user, and `cmd/fleet/up.go` launches it with no mode. Repo defaults stay neutral exactly as ADR-13 set them: fleet ships with no mode, and `mode = "auto"` under `[defaults]` in the uncommitted `config.toml` is what turns the fleet unattended, now in one line for the whole fleet instead of one line per tool.
Why: an agy agent stopped on "Requesting permission for: timew / Run this command?" and sat there. The user's config did say "no prompts", but it said it as `[profile.copilot] mode = "autopilot"`, which is a Copilot word; agy had no `mode_args` at all, so there was nothing to say it to. That is the real defect: the *intent* ("don't ask me") was per-tool vocabulary, so it could only ever be expressed for the tools someone had already written modes for, and every new profile silently opted back into prompting. One shared name makes the intent portable and makes the gap testable: `TestEveryProfileHasAutoMode` fails when a built-in profile is added without one, which is the check that stops this recurring. Keeping it a local preference rather than a repo default is ADR-13 unchanged and deliberate: auto-approve is the setting with the largest blast radius in fleet, and a cloned repo should not acquire it silently.
Rejected: (a) putting `--dangerously-skip-permissions` into agy's `args`, so it is always on (it makes the repo default unattended, which ADR-13 refused, and leaves no way to spawn an agy agent that does ask); (b) falling back to `auto` when the configured mode is unknown for a profile (it turns a typo like `mode = "pln"` into allow-all, exactly the wrong direction to fail); (c) a new `auto_approve = true` preference beside `mode` (two keys for one decision, and they can contradict each other); (d) answering agy's approval menu on screen the way ADR-47 answers trust dialogs (it is a real flag away, and screen-scraping a menu to press "always allow" is both slower and riskier than telling the tool at launch, and the pattern stays the fallback for tools that genuinely have no flag). Accepted cost: a profile defined in the user's own `~/.fleet/tools.toml` still has to define `auto` itself or it quietly keeps prompting, with only a log line to say so; and `auto` for agy is its *skip-permissions* flag, not its `--mode auto`, so agy's own execution-mode named "auto" is not reachable through fleet by that name.

**ADR-57: the worker playbook comes in three effort levels, and only pro code work pays for all of it** (§4, §14 ADR-22, R11, R15)
Decision: `internal/playbook` gains a `Level` — `light`, `standard`, `pro` — with its own embedded text per level (`light.md`, `standard.md`, `worker.md`). A spawn picks it with `fleet spawn --effort`, `effort` on `fleet_spawn` and `fleet_squad_spawn`, kept on the board so a queued agent drains at the level it was spawned with; `fleet playbook --effort` prints one. The level is chosen *after* the worktree decision: with no explicit effort, a spawn that asked for a worktree in a git repo gets `pro` — including the fallback where git refused the worktree, so the agent that ends up in the user's own checkout keeps the full process — and anything else (`worktree: false`, or a dir outside any repo) gets `light`. Every ordinary code spawn is therefore unchanged; a `--no-worktree` spawn in a repo is the one deliberate behaviour change, and `--effort pro` puts it back. `light` is five rules (stay in your dir, decide rather than wait, no secrets, time matters, report); `standard` adds the plan, build-and-test and a conventional commit on the branch, but no ADR, no subagent review and no merge unless the task asks; `pro` is today's text unchanged. The user's `~/.fleet/worker.md` and the repo's `.fleet/worker.md` layer onto every level, and `playbook = false` still switches it all off. The admiral instructions and the `fleet_spawn` description tell the admiral to pick per task.
Why: the playbook is paid for in tokens on every spawn and, worse, in behaviour: an agent told to start a timewarrior timer and given a seven-step pre-merge checklist makes a worktree, a branch and an ADR for it. The rules are good advice *for professional code work in a repo*, which is what they were written from — so the split is by effort, not by tool or by profile, and the thing that already distinguishes a code task from an errand is whether the spawn made a worktree. Three levels is the fewest that separates "errand", "contained change" and "full process"; the names say what the user is asking for rather than how many lines they cost.
Rejected: (a) one playbook with conditional sections ("if this is a code task, also…") — agents read instructions literally and the tokens are paid either way; (b) deriving the level from the task card or from the prompt's wording (a guess the admiral can make better, and unpredictable); (c) a per-profile setting in `config.toml` (the level belongs to the task, not to the tool); (d) making `standard` the default without a worktree (an errand does not need a plan and a commit either, and `light` is one `effort="standard"` away); (e) letting the user's `worker.md` layer apply only to `pro` (the user's own rules are theirs to scope, and a layer that silently disappears at some levels is worse than one that is always there).

**ADR-58: the board keeps keys it does not know, and a leader is told about its squad in its first prompt** (§6.11, §4, §14 ADR-53, ADR-57, R11, R15)
Decision: `board.Agent` gets `UnmarshalJSON`/`MarshalJSON` that keep any `board.json` key this build has no field for and write it back untouched. Separately, a leader's first prompt carries `internal/playbook/leader.md` — which squad tools it has and what each is for, that its agents report to it, that peers in its team are reached with `fleet_agents`/`fleet_msg_send`, that it must never spawn or manage agents through the `fleet` CLI or a shell, and that a missing `fleet_squad_*` is a `state=blocked` report rather than something to improvise around — at every effort level and whether or not the playbook is on. The playbook's repo layer also stops below the user's home directory, so a `~/.fleet/worker.md` used for machine-wide notes is applied once, as the user layer, and not a second time for an agent working in a non-repo dir under `$HOME`. As a safety net, `fleet spawn` run from inside a leader's pane goes through `SquadSpawn`, so a leader that reaches for the CLI — or one on a profile with no MCP, which has nothing else — still gets a squad member rather than a sibling, and `--leader` from a leader's pane is refused with a pointer to `fleet_squad_spawn`.
Why: observed live on 2026-11-06. `diagram-lead` was spawned with `leader: true`, the spawn reply said "It is a leader", and one second later `board.json` had no `leader` key — and no `effort` key either. The resident `fleet watch` had been running since before both fields existed (squad landed 18:58, effort 21:52, the watcher started 14:57 the same day) and rewrites every agent through its own struct on each status transition, so the oldest process on the machine silently erased what the newest had written. The leader's MCP server then saw a non-leader and served no squad tools; the agent searched its tools for "squad", found nothing, shelled out to `fleet spawn --leader=false`, and the agent it created was a sibling reporting to the admiral. Three defects in one incident: a board that loses fields across binary vintages, a capability advertised only through a tool list that can be wrong, and a CLI that happily does the wrong thing from inside a leader. The round-trip fix is the root cause and is general — every field added from now on survives a stale watcher — while the prompt and the CLI route are what make the failure non-silent and non-costly if it ever recurs.
Rejected: (a) versioning `board.json` and refusing to write from an older binary (it would freeze the board the moment an upgrade lands mid-session, which is worse than losing one flag); (b) having `fleet watch` exit when its binary changes, like the MCP hot reload of §6.9 (worth doing one day, but it only narrows the window and nothing restarts the watcher automatically); (c) inferring leadership from the mirror team in `teams.json` when the board flag is missing (two sources of truth for one fact); (d) refusing `fleet spawn` from a leader's pane outright (it throws away the work the leader was trying to start, and the leader has already been told the rule twice). Accepted cost: a process started before this change still drops unknown keys, so the fleet must be restarted once for the fix to take effect; and `board.json` can accumulate keys from a newer fleet that this build will never use.

**ADR-59: every effort level carries the safety core, and closing a leader loses nothing that is not its own** (§4, §6.11, §14 ADR-51, ADR-53, ADR-57, ADR-58)
Decision: (a) Five rules are the playbook's safety core and every level carries them in its own words: read-only tasks edit nothing; never push main, never force-push or rewrite pushed history (`light` pushes nothing; `standard` and `pro` push only their own `fleet/` branch, for CI, ADR-69); commits carry `Assisted-by:` and never an AI `Co-authored-by:`; secrets and private data stay out; work on your own branch — if not on a `fleet/` branch, make one before committing, never commit to main. A test loops over the levels and fails if any lacks one. `standard` no longer says both "stay in your dir" and "never work in the main checkout": with no worktree it makes a branch. (b) Closing a leader kills its pane first and disbands only once that worked; the drain of its mailbox returns exactly the messages it marks read, and every report among them is re-filed to the admiral (marked as re-filed) and wakes it like any report. (c) The squad mirror team carries `squad = <leader>` in `teams.json`; fleet adds a squad only to a team it created for that leader and deletes only that team, so a user's team that shares the leader's name is left alone and the squad goes uncoloured. (d) The report wake keeps its own announced-up-to mark, `mail/<box>/woken`, apart from the peer-mail `nudged` mark, so a peer message nudged into a leader's pane cannot swallow a squad report's wake. (e) Run from inside a leader's pane, `fleet close`, `send`, `park` and `wake` refuse any agent outside its squad, and `send` refuses a member sitting on a prompt; run from a squad member's pane, `fleet spawn --leader` is refused.
Why: a review of ADR-53 and ADR-57 found each by probe. `light` is the default for a `worktree=false` spawn in a repo, which is the user's own checkout, and it had lost the push, trailer and read-only rules. A member's `question` to its leader was marked read when the leader closed and never reached anyone. A leader named like a user's team joined it and deleted it on close. A peer nudge moved the shared mark past a queued report, so the leader was never woken. The squad's scope held over MCP only, while ADR-58 makes the CLI the supported path for a leader without MCP, and a member's `--leader` spawn made a top-level leader around `max_depth`.
Rejected: (a) prepending one shared core file to every level in code (pro already says each rule in its own sections, so it would be said twice, and a test over the texts catches the same loss); (b) a reserved `squad-<name>` team name (a user may still have that team; ownership is the fact that matters, and the wall keeps showing the leader's name); (c) refusing the leader spawn when the name is taken by a team (throws work away for a cosmetic mirror). Accepted cost: a mirror team created before this change has no `squad` field, so a live squad's new members are not coloured into it and it is no longer deleted on close; delete it with `fleet team delete`. An older binary that rewrites `teams.json` (a `fleet watch` left running) drops `squad` the same way, so restart the fleet once after upgrading. The first wake after the upgrade may repeat one already-announced report, because `woken` starts empty.
**ADR-60: a spawn's effort level also sizes its tool's own context** (§4, §14 ADR-57)
Decision: a profile has `context_args`, an inline table of space-separated args keyed by effort level (`light`, `standard`, `pro`) plus `leader`, with the same shape and override rule as `mode_args` (a layer that sets it replaces the whole table). Spawn appends the entry for the agent's effort level, after the mode args; a squad leader takes `leader` when the profile has it, at any effort.
