The board's `effort` now holds the level actually used, the default included, and `fleet wake` applies that entry again because the resumed process is new; wake also keeps the effort and leader role on the board, so a second wake does the same. `fleet adopt` adds none, and neither does a wake of an agent with no recorded level: an adopted session is the user's own. The built-in `copilot` and `claude-copilot` profiles ship a default: every level gets `--excluded-tools run_dynamic_workflow dynamic_workflows_manage sql session_store_sql store_memory vote_memory list_canvas_capabilities open_canvas invoke_canvas_action manage_schedule read_resource semantic_code_search` and `--disable-builtin-mcps`; light and standard also exclude `task read_agent list_agents write_agent`. Pro and leaders keep the subagent tools because the pro playbook asks for a subagent review and a leader delegates. `--disable-builtin-mcps` comes last because `--excluded-tools` takes every argument up to the next flag. `skill` and `web_fetch` stay at every level.
Why: a new Copilot CLI agent started at about 49k of 200k tokens, and about 46k of that arrived before fleet said a word: the CLI's built-in tool schemas are sent on every call and are not deferred like MCP tools (investigation 2026-11-06). Measured on `claude-haiku-4.5` in this repo, prompt `hi`: no context args 33,780 input tokens, `pro` 24,559, `light` 18,989; scaled to Opus (×1.38) about 46.6k, 33.9k and 26.2k. Making it the default for an effort level means no user has to know the flag list. Effort already decides how much process an agent is given, so it is the right key for how many tools it gets.
Rejected: (a) excluding `skill` for light, measured at a further −5.2k (13,824): light is the level for errands, and errands are what skills are for (ADO, browser, timesheet chores); a user who wants it can set their own `context_args`; (b) `--available-tools` allowlists, which silently drop any new built-in the CLI adds, including ones fleet may want; (c) per-user `extra_args`, which is what the investigation proposed first and which every user would have to rediscover; (d) dropping `skillDirectories = ["~/.claude/skills"]` as a skills-listing fix: measured at 0 tokens, because the CLI reads `~/.claude/skills` by itself and dedupes skills by name.

**ADR-61: fleet keeps out of a session the user is talking to** (§6.7, §14 ADR-29, ADR-36, fleet#1)
Decision: (a) An agent can be *interactive*: `fleet spawn --interactive` or `fleet_spawn interactive=true` sets `interactive` on its board entry, which survives the queue and `fleet wake`. Its first prompt gets `internal/playbook/interactive.md`, playbook on or off: report `progress`, not `done`, until the user says the session is over. Every playbook level also says so for a task that says the user talks to the agent directly. (b) Fleet also notices the user by itself. `tmux list-clients -F '#{pane_id} #{client_activity}'` gives each attached client's active pane and its last key press; fleet's own paste and send-keys never move `client_activity`. Each Refresh records a key press newer than the agent's spawn in its pane as `user_at` on the board. An agent is the user's (`WithUser`) while it is interactive or within `UserHold` (10 min) of `user_at`; the overview marks it `✎` after the name, `fleet_status` shows `<status> (interactive)` and `fleet info` an `interactive:` line. (c) Auto-close, at report time and in the idle sweep, is refused before the git check when the agent is interactive ("not auto-closed: interactive"), when a client has its pane open now, or when the user typed there within `UserHold` ("not auto-closed: interactive (the user typed in its pane 2m ago)"). (d) `fleet_send`, `fleet send` and a team send refuse to type into an agent when the user pressed a key in its pane within `UserTyping` (30 s), when it is interactive and a client has its pane open, or, for text, when its input box (the `input` pattern, §4) already holds text. `fleet_send force=true` or `fleet send --force` types anyway; a team send has no override and reports the member as skipped, naming `fleet_send force=true`. A keys-only send checks the box too when it presses Enter, unless the box shows fleet's own pending paste (the `pending` pattern). `fleet_answer` is not guarded: the user has just confirmed that answer. `fleet wake` keeps `interactive` and `user_at`.
Why: fleet#1. An agent spawned for the user to talk to reported `done` as every playbook told it to, and auto-close killed its pane while the user was dictating into it. Then a `fleet_send` pasted into and submitted the user's half-written input. A focused pane with no recent key press may still be in use: hold-to-talk dictation sends nothing until the user lets go, so for an interactive agent an open pane is enough to refuse.
Rejected: (a) making auto-detection set the sticky `interactive` flag: a user who glances at a worker would keep it open for good; the 10-minute hold covers the case. (b) Reusing `confirmed=true` as the override: it already means "the user said yes to answering this prompt", and the two cases are different questions. (c) Queueing a refused send and typing it later: the admiral decides when to retry, and a queued line could still land in the middle of the next sentence. (d) Watching pane output for typed text: fleet's own paste and the agent's output look the same there, and `client_activity` already separates them. Accepted cost: tmux keeps one activity time per client, so a key press is credited to whichever pane is active when a poll sees it; switching panes by key counts as touching the new pane.

**ADR-62: every store keeps keys it does not know, the watcher follows its binary, and standing rules are pinned** (§6.5, §7c, §14 ADR-25, ADR-49, ADR-58, ADR-59)
Decision: (a) `internal/jsonx` holds one helper pair, `Unmarshal`/`Marshal`, that keeps the keys of a JSON object its Go type has no field for and writes them back. Every type fleet stores opts in with an unexported `extra` field and two one-line methods: in `board.json` the top level, `Agent` (ADR-58, now on the helper), `Card`, `Check`, `Report`, `Worktree`, `Exit` and `GitBase`; in `teams.json` the file and each team; each pair in `mail/grants.json`; the file and each claim in `adr-claims.json`; and `admiral/session.json`. (b) `board.Store.Update` does not write when the result is byte-identical to the file it read. (c) The watcher's claim is an exclusive `flock` on `watch.lock`, held for the process's life and never removed; the pid in the file is only for messages. A fresh heartbeat from a live pid also counts as held, so a watcher from before this change is not doubled. Every 5 s the watcher checks its binary: if `bin/fleet` is a different file from the one it runs, it probes it with `fleet watch --probe` and execs it with the same pid and arguments (the flock is close-on-exec, and the new image claims it again); a failed probe or exec keeps the old code until the file changes again. It also stops when its home is gone. The overview (`fleet ui`) follows its binary the same way (amended 2026-11-10, after a stale overview made the badge blink, ADR-75): every 5 s it checks, probes a new build, quits the TUI to give the terminal back and execs the new binary in the same pane with the same arguments; the shared check is `watch.Binary`. `fleet up` stops and replaces a running watcher whose `/proc/<pid>/exe` is not `bin/fleet`. (d) `fleet_note pin=true` and `fleet note --pin` append the note to `decisions.log` and to `pinned.log`; `fleet_brief` shows every pinned line, then the last 30 notes without them. (e) `playbook.Ceiling` is a directory the repo-layer walk stops at, like the home directory; the test binaries that spawn set it to `os.TempDir()` in `TestMain`.
Why: a state review on 2026-11-06 found the ADR-58 incident still open. The resident watcher ran a deleted binary that predated ADR-59 and ADR-61, so it skipped the ADR-61 auto-close rule and dropped `squad` from `teams.json` on every close, which turned a squad's mirror team into a user team fleet would never delete. ADR-58 had fixed one struct of six stores and rejected re-exec because "nothing restarts the watcher". Re-exec is that restart, and it closes the vintage window for every file at once; the generic helper means the next new field in any store survives without anyone remembering to add it. Twenty duplicate watchers had been seen earlier; the pid-file claim was check-then-write and open to pid reuse, which a kernel lock is not. An idle fleet rewrote `board.json` every second, giving every stale process a chance to clobber it every second. The brief showed about ten hours of notes, so a rule the user set on day one was gone by day two. The playbook tests read the real `~/.fleet/worker.md` when `TMPDIR` was under `$HOME` and `HOME` did not match it (a test's `Setenv("HOME")`, or a symlinked home): the walk never met its stop.
Rejected: (a) a schema version that refuses writes from an older binary (ADR-58 (a): it freezes the board mid-upgrade); (b) SQLite (cgo or a non-Charm dependency, loses `cat`/`jq`, and the failures were schema drift, which a row update has too); (c) having the watcher exit, not re-exec, on a new binary (nothing would start the next one until `fleet up`); (d) a rules file the admiral rewrites (a second copy of a note that can drift; `pinned.log` is append-only like `decisions.log`, and the user deletes a line to unpin); (e) compacting `decisions.log` (loses history for a problem that is about what the brief shows); (f) `testing.Testing()` inside `playbook` to pick the ceiling (hidden behaviour in production code). Accepted cost: a process started before this change still drops unknown keys and does not re-exec, so the fleet must be restarted once (`fleet watch --stop && fleet up --no-attach`); stores can accumulate keys a newer fleet wrote; once an unknown key is present that object's keys are written in sorted order. Resuming running agents after a reboot stays out of scope.

**ADR-63: built-in Claude profiles start on the newest Opus, their subagents on the newest Sonnet** (§4, §14 ADR-56)
Decision: the built-in `claude` profile starts `claude --model opus` with `CLAUDE_CODE_SUBAGENT_MODEL=sonnet`; `claude-copilot` starts `copilot --model claude-opus-5.5`. The values are the constants `ClaudeModel`, `ClaudeSubagentModel` and `CopilotClaudeModel` in `internal/tools`, and a test fails if a built-in or example profile passes any other Claude model.
Amended 2026-11-11 (ADR-100 (d)): `CLAUDE_CODE_SUBAGENT_MODEL=sonnet` and `ClaudeSubagentModel` are gone; built-in and example profiles set no subagent model, so Claude subagents inherit Opus, and a test fails if one sets it.
Why: profiles pinned `claude-opus-5` and `claude-sonnet-5` and quietly kept agents on old models after newer ones shipped. The claude CLI's aliases always resolve to the newest model, so they cannot drift; the Copilot CLI needs an exact id, so it lives in one constant with a test.
Rejected: (a) a `model` key per profile in config.toml — a second place for the same value; (b) pinning full claude ids such as `claude-opus-5-5` — the same drift one release later.

**ADR-64: several admirals on one fleet, each owning its agents and their reports** (§6.12, §6.5, §6.6)
Decision: an admiral has a name; `main` keeps every existing path, mailbox (`admiral`) and pane, others live in `admirals/<name>/` with box `admiral.<name>`, each admiral's MCP server is told its name (`--admiral`, never guessed: an unknown identity is read-only), and each board agent carries `admiral` (empty = main). Reports go to the owner's box, write tools refuse another admiral's agents while reads stay open, `fleet admiral add|ls|close|give` manage them, and closing one returns its agents and unread reports to main. Admiral-to-admiral mail follows the access policy.
Why: one admiral steering unrelated streams mixes chats and report queues and burns context twice as fast; keeping main unchanged means a running admiral and older binaries keep working.

**ADR-65: pane captures drop ghost text** (§4, §14 ADR-36, ADR-45)
Decision: `tmux.Client.Capture`, which every status, input-box, wake and peek reader goes through, captures with `-e` and keeps plain text, minus any faint (SGR 2) run that only spaces follow on its line. Faint text with normal text after it on the same line stays.
Why: Claude Code draws a grey next-prompt suggestion in its empty input box after a turn. Plain `capture-pane` loses the attribute, so fleet read the suggestion as typed input. The wake guard (ADR-36) then waited for "an idle, empty input box" for as long as the suggestion stayed, so reports sat in the admiral's mailbox and the admiral never heard about them. On an agent the same text broke the empty-prompt idle pattern, the screen read as unknown, the watcher kept "working", and the stuck rule (ADR-45) reported a finished agent as stuck 10 minutes after its done. Typed input is never faint; suggestions and placeholders are. Copilot's transcript bar and Claude's diff line numbers are faint but have content after them, so trailing-only keeps them. Placeholder hints (`Try "…"`, `Ask anything`) are faint too and now vanish from captures; the `placeholder` patterns stay as a fallback and the older idle fixtures still show the hint.
Rejected: (a) dropping all faint text, which deletes Copilot's transcript bars and Claude's line numbers; (b) a per-tool "suggestion" pattern, which can't tell a suggestion from the same words typed by the user without the attribute; (c) turning off Claude Code's prompt suggestions in the profile, which fixes one tool and leaves the reader wrong.

**ADR-66: an optional web view, `fleet web`** (R3, §5f, §13, §16 rule 7)
Decision: Sturla approved a web UI on 2026-11-08. This amends R3 and §13 (no web UI) and makes `fleet web` the one exception to §16 rule 7 (no server). The TUI stays primary. `fleet web` lives in the fleet binary (`internal/web`), uses `net/http`, `html/template`, `embed` and SSE, and calls the same `internal/fleet` methods as the CLI and MCP. The open decisions in the proposal, each taken as recommended: (1) amend R3, §13 and §16 rule 7 with this ADR; (2) start it by hand, no `fleet up` key yet; (3) draw panes server-side, ANSI to HTML, with no raw terminal; (4) bind loopback by default, a LAN bind only through `--listen`, never the default, no built-in TLS; (5) the first slice writes only send, allowlisted keys and menu answers, not spawn, close or park; (6) it acts as the user and sees every admiral's agents. Two choices go beyond the proposal: the token is stable (env or `~/.fleet/web.json`) and also accepted as a bearer header, and `--allow-host` names extra host names, so a reverse proxy behind a login gateway can be set up once; and send has no force, so the web never types over the user.
Why: steering the fleet from a phone or another machine without moving agents off the laptop (landscape survey, option A). Stdlib plus the existing allowlist keeps the safety model the admiral already works under. Own token auth stays on behind a gateway: the page can type into agents running with bypass permissions, so one misconfigured proxy must not be enough.
Rejected: xterm.js over a WebSocket (two new dependencies, raw keys skip the allowlist, an attached client resizes the wall); a separate binary shelling out to the CLI (fork per poll, version skew, CLI JSON turns into an API); a token that changes on every start (the proxy config would break on each restart).

**ADR-67: minimize all, with one level of restore** (§5e, §14 ADR-37)
Decision: one action takes every agent pane off the wall (`Alt-X`, `fleet wall hide-all|restore|toggle-all`, `fleet_wall` `hide_all|restore`). It breaks the panes out like `Alt-x` (processes untouched, agents stay on the board) and records their ids in wall order in the window option `@fleet_minimized`. Restore joins the recorded panes that are still alive and not on the wall, up to the preset's slots, then clears the record. Hide-all on an empty wall changes nothing and keeps the record, so a second press never loses what to restore. `Alt-X` toggles: restore when the wall is empty and there is a record, hide-all otherwise. Pinned panes go too: pin guards against eviction and replacement, not an explicit request to clear the wall.
Why: clearing a busy wall took one `Alt-x` per pane, and putting the same set back meant finding each agent again. The record lives in tmux like the rest of the wall state (§5e), so the key binding, the CLI and MCP share it with no new file.
Rejected: a new preset "0" (presets size the grid, and `fill` would refill it on the next change); a separate restore key (`Alt-X` as a toggle needs no second key to learn); a restore stack (one level matches `u` undo).

**ADR-68: status says what the agent is doing, and an agent that stops mid-task says so** (§4, §5, §7c, §14 ADR-19, ADR-36, ADR-49)
Model: an agent is `queued`, `starting`, `working`, `waiting` (a prompt or menu on screen), `idle`, `stuck`, `error`, `parked` or `gone`, as before. Sources by profile, newest evidence wins (ADR-49): `copilot`/`claude-copilot` read the screen (copilot patterns) plus `events.jsonl` as an event source; `claude*` read the screen plus per-run hooks through `--settings`; `agy` and `vibe` read the screen only. On top of `idle` the board derives **no report**: the agent was handed work (`prompt_at`, set by a spawn with a task and by every text `fleet_send`, team send and yes/no answer), has been idle for `NoReportAfter` (20 s, longer than a turn boundary), is not the user's (ADR-61), and has no `done`, `blocked` or `question` report since `prompt_at`. A `progress` report doesn't end a task. The stored status stays `idle`, so eviction, tiers and sweeps are unchanged.
Decision: (a) The overview shows a no-report agent with `…` in the waiting colour after its name and "idle · no report" on its card, and never folds it with the idle ones. The wall border shows the same `…` (`@fleet_noreport`), and `fleet_status` shows `idle (no report)`. (b) The resident refresher tells the owner once per task, as a fleet-written `blocked` mail like a stuck note, with the wake line "[fleet] <name> stopped mid-task without a report". A new `prompt_at` re-arms it, and the agent's own report on the board is untouched. (c) While the admiral is busy no wake line can be typed (ADR-36), so the "(N unread reports)" suffix on every admiral result now names the newest three by sender and headline. (d) The copilot patterns read Copilot CLI 1.0.107's busy footer (`Autopilot · Allow All · ◉ Working · … ctrl+c interrupt`) as working. Before this, the idle pattern `autopilot · ` matched it and nothing in working did, so on 2026-11-09 all 16 `copilot` and most of the 17 `claude-copilot` agents went working→idle about 2 s after spawn and stayed idle through their whole task.
Why: the user saw the board call working agents idle and mid-task agents done. The event log for 2026-11-09 shows copilot agents with exactly one working→idle transition each. email-scrub and diagram-find went idle on "Next I'm mapping…" with no report and continued only after a nudge. Reports from rema-android, gh-email-audit and release waited for an idle admiral box while the admiral saw only a count.
Protocols, checked against the installed CLIs on 2026-11-09: the structured protocol these tools speak is the **Agent Client Protocol (ACP)**, JSON-RPC 2.0 over the agent's stdin and stdout, in which the client starts the agent, sends `session/prompt` and gets `session/update` notifications. Copilot CLI 1.0.107 serves it with `copilot --acp`, and `copilot help acp` documents opt-in events `assistant.turn_start`, `assistant.idle` (with `data.aborted`) and `session.idle`, sent as `github.com/copilot/sessionEvent`. agy 1.5.3 serves it with `agy acp`, and vibe 3.0.3 ships a `vibe-acp` binary. Claude Code 2.1.319 has no ACP (Zed's adapter wraps the Agent SDK, not the CLI, and isn't installed). None of the four serve A2A. IBM's Agent Communication Protocol is also shortened to ACP and was folded into A2A in 2025. It isn't the one these CLIs speak. Copilot's `events.jsonl` has `assistant.turn_start`/`turn_end` but no `assistant.idle`, `session.idle` or permission events, and Copilot's hooks need a user or repo `settings.json` with no per-run flag, which ADR-49 (d) rules out.
Rejected: (a) ACP as the status source for today's agents. It is a client protocol: whoever starts the agent owns its stdio and draws the conversation, so an ACP agent has no TUI on the wall, can't take the user's typing and has no menu for `fleet_answer`, and a second ACP process would be a different session. A headless ACP agent kind, with fleet drawing the transcript, is the follow-up that would give exact idle. (b) A2A for status. Its task states map neatly onto fleet's, but no CLI serves it and it needs an HTTP endpoint per agent (ADR-19). (c) A new stored status `stalled`: every consumer of `idle` (wall eviction, the idle fold, the done sweep, parking) would have to learn it, while a derived flag changes only what is drawn and told. (d) Waking the admiral on `progress` reports: they are frequent by design (interactive agents), and the suffix now names them. (e) Treating `assistant.turn_end` in `events.jsonl` as idle: under autopilot or a background shell more work follows it, as `copilot help acp` says.
Accepted cost: an agent that finishes and simply forgets to report is flagged too, which is the point. An agent given work by typing into its pane through a route other than `fleet_send` has no `prompt_at`, unless the user typed there, and then it is the user's anyway.
Amendment (2026-11-09, fleet#8): the user's agents were exempt, so they never showed a stop. an interactive `claude-copilot` agent the user had typed in went idle at 14:48:14 on "Row 38 is done and verified. Logging everything now." with no report since 13:48 and the board said plain idle: its `prompt_at` was empty (spawned before ADR-68) and the user's `Alt-x` at 14:48:32 counted as typing. Now (e) the refresher records `turn_at`, set on the switch to working that starts a turn: the first one, after starting, or after an idle of at least `NoReportAfter` (a shorter idle, a menu or a stuck spell stays inside the turn). (f) An agent that is the user's (ADR-61) and idle for `NoReportAfter`, whose user has not pressed a key in its pane for `UserTyping` (30 s), and with no report of any state since `turn_at`, is "no report" in a soft form: `…` in the muted colour on its tile, row and border (`@fleet_noreport` = `user`, the hard form is `1`), still unfolded, and "idle (no report, the user's: don't send)" in `fleet_status`. Any report counts here, because an interactive agent reports progress, not done. (g) The soft form wakes nobody: the user is in that pane, and a send would land in their input. Once `UserHold` passes without a key press the agent is no longer the user's and the hard rule applies, with its one wake.
Rejected for the amendment: reading "now I'll…" or "Logging…" off the last message (a phrase list per tool and language that drifts like the status patterns, #7); `UserAt` as the turn start (any key in the pane moves it, including fleet's own `Alt` keys, so a glance would arm the flag).
Amendment (2026-11-09, fleet#12): an `agy` agent went working→idle 11 s after a send while it was still editing files, and 20 s later the owner got `blocked: no report` a minute before the agent's real `done`. The agy patterns read its footer as idle while a tool ran, and the screen was not captured in that state, so the patterns are unchanged. Now (h) both forms of no report also need the pane screen unchanged (`last_change`, the screen hash the refresher already keeps) for `NoReportAfter`, not only `idle` for that long: a changing screen is work whatever the patterns read. (i) Once the owner was told in an idle spell, the hard flag stays until the agent works or reports, so a footer that ticks (agy's quota countdown, about once a minute) can't blink the `…`. The soft form has no such marker and blinks off for `NoReportAfter` after each such tick.
Amendment (2026-11-10, ADR-105): where the fleet plugin's hooks run, the Stop/agentStop hook blocks the stop once per prompt for an agent that would be flagged here, so most no-report agents now report before going idle. The 20 s flag and its wake stay as the fallback for agy, vibe, an agent without the plugin and a block the agent ignores.
Rejected for (h): a longer `NoReportAfter` (delays every true stop to fix one tool's patterns); fitting agy's tool-run screen from its binary's strings (`Running command`, `Editing files`) without a capture, which the fixtures rule forbids. Accepted cost of (h): an idle pane whose screen never settles for `NoReportAfter` (a clock or animation redrawn faster than that) is never flagged, for any tool.

**ADR-69: heavy checks run in CI on the agent's pushed branch** (§4, §14 ADR-29, ADR-48)
Decision: (a) `.github/workflows/ci.yml` runs on pushes to `main`, `fleet/**` and `v*` tags and on pull requests to main: `make build`, `go vet ./...`, `go build ./...`, `go test ./...`, and `go test -race ./...` as a parallel job, with Go module and build caching (`setup-go`) and one run per branch at a time (`concurrency`, cancel in progress). (b) The pro and standard playbooks run only the changed packages' tests locally. Before merging, a pro agent squashes, pushes its own branch with a normal push (`git push -u origin fleet/<name>`), waits for the run on its head commit (`gh run watch`, or `gh run list --branch fleet/<name> --json headSha,conclusion`) and merges only on green; on red it fixes and pushes again. It opens a PR only if a push starts no run. `fleet merge` then gets a cheap `--check`. (c) Agents may push their own `fleet/` branch to origin. Main is still pushed only by the designated publisher, fast-forward only, and nobody force-pushes. Fixups made after a push go on top as new commits, so squashing happens before the first push. `fleet merge`'s rebase of the pushed branch is allowed; the agent doesn't push the branch again after it. (d) Done-checks on task cards should be cheap: a build plus the touched packages' tests, or a check that CI passed on the branch head. The admiral's instructions and the `fleet_spawn` `check` help say so.
Why: every agent ran the full build, vet and test suite twice, as its pre-merge check and as fleet's done-check, and `fleet merge` ran it twice more. With 4–6 agents at once the laptop was saturated. CI already existed but only gated main, after the merge. A run takes about a minute (two with `-race`), costs the laptop nothing, and tests the exact commit that is merged when main has not moved.
Trade-offs: merges now depend on the network and on GitHub. Offline, or in a repo whose CI doesn't run on `fleet/*` branches, the agent runs the full suite locally as before and says so in its report. Pushed branches are public in a public repo, so the pre-push hook's public check (`scripts/check-public.sh`) runs on them too. When main moved since the push, `fleet merge` rebases, and the rebased combination is covered only by the cheap local check until main's own CI run.
Rejected: PRs for every agent (extra state to clean up, and a push already runs CI); `-race` locally (slowest of all); force-pushing a branch after a squash (pushed history is never rewritten, even on a fleet branch).

**ADR-70: fleet acts on the 14-day usage insights: interactive admirals, eager fleet tools, one ending** (§6.9, §14 ADR-25, ADR-34, ADR-43, ADR-53; fleet#28, #29, #31–#35)
Context: a read of 455 Copilot sessions (2026-10-26 to 2026-11-09, 190k AI credits, 48% of it fleet work) found turns paid for nothing. 174 of 195 agents spent a `tool_search_tool` turn to find `fleet_report` (fleet-peer tools are deferred in Copilot, and there is no per-server switch to load them eagerly), most of them at the end, with a full context. 85 agents ended twice, `fleet_report` then `task_complete`. 77 autopilot "not complete yet" nudges were sent, 20 to admirals, and admirals called `task_complete` 59 times. 23 `kill $P` calls and 21 `create` calls into missing dirs failed.
Decision: (a) A Copilot admiral starts with its profile's `interactive` mode (`--mode interactive`), also on resume, whatever mode the user's config gives agents: an admiral idles while its agents work, and in autopilot each idle turn ends in a nudge. Its instructions say to end a waiting turn with no tool call and never call `task_complete`. (b) The worker playbook tells an agent to load the fleet tools in its first response with one `tool_search_tool` call (pattern `^fleet-peer-fleet_`), when the context is small, and in autopilot to end a done, blocked or question report with `fleet_report` and `task_complete` in the same response: `task_complete` only ends the turn, so a waiting agent stops being nudged (57 nudges went to agents) and a reply resumes it. (c) The playbook names the shell habits that failed (`mkdir -p` first, a numeric PID for `kill`, no `sleep` to wait for a peer); the fleet-builder brief repeats the `mkdir -p` line.
Why: the extra search and the second ending each cost one full-context turn, about 5 AI credits per agent at the median and far more for long-lived ones. Skipping `task_complete` instead would trade the second ending for up to five autopilot nudges (`--max-autopilot-continues` defaults to 5).
Rejected: dropping `task_complete` from agents (`--excluded-tools`): autopilot needs it to stop; treating `task_complete` as an implicit done: fleet can't see it, only the pane; turning `toolSearch` off for agents: it is a user-wide setting, and the user's other MCP servers would load in full again.
Decision, spawn and mail (fleet#31, #32, #33, #35): (d) `[defaults] profile` in config.toml, read live, makes `profile` optional on `fleet_spawn` and `fleet_squad_spawn`; an explicit profile wins, and neither is an error naming `fleet_profiles` and the key. Every spawn reply names the profile and the model (the last `--model` in the profile's args, then config `extra_args`; else "tool default model"), and a queued reply gives how many are ahead, the busy count and `max_working`. 185 of 191 admiral spawns had taken a default the user then corrected by hand, and 24 queued silently. (e) Text sent to a queued agent is held: appended to its Goal under the board lock and delivered when it starts. Keys are still refused. (f) The leader owns its squad, so `fleet_squad_close` with `confirmed=true` closes on the first call; the admiral's `fleet_close` keeps its ask-the-user rule. (g) Within one repo, a message to or from a squad leader is free (mail reason `lead`); team members stay private, and a refusal names the lead the sender may reach, so a hand-off goes lead to lead instead of through the user. (h) A task card may carry `may`: standing OKs (merge-local, push-main, deploy, free text), stated once in the first prompt and shown in `fleet_info` and the spawn reply. Fleet does not enforce them; they replace repeated asks (8 of 25 `question` reports asked for an OK the user had already given), not the safety rules.
Rejected for (d)–(h): a separate status call after each spawn (a round trip per spawn); a pending-message list for queued agents (a second delivery path to keep in sync); free mail between any two same-repo agents (team members can belong to different clients, ADR-34).

**ADR-71: count compactions, and tell the owner to recycle an agent that keeps compacting** (§7b, §14 ADR-46, ADR-68, fleet#27)
Context: 14 days of Copilot sessions: 47 of 195 agents compacted their context at least once. Their median spend was 1,108 AI credits against 105 for agents that never compacted, about ten times as much, and they were 70% of all agent spend, because every turn after the first compaction re-reads a nearly full context. The cause was one agent reused round after round ("NEW TASK (round N)"), or a whole spec → build → review cycle in one context.
Decision: (a) `internal/spend` counts successful `session.compaction_complete` events per Copilot session as `Spend.Compactions` and shows them as `⟲N` after the spend wherever spend is shown; the latest context fill is `context_pct` in `fleet spend --json`. Claude stays at 0: its transcripts carry no compaction marker here. (b) `[defaults] recycle_compactions` (2) and `recycle_aic` (1000), 0 = off, re-read on each check. (c) The resident overview checks every 30 s, off the poll, and an agent that crosses either threshold gets one hint to its owner, claimed on the board (`recycle_told`) and sent as a fleet-written `question` mail plus wake line, the ADR-68 path. Once per agent, not per task: an agent that already has a full context stays expensive whatever its next task is. (d) The admiral and leader instructions say what to do with it: ask the agent for `result.md`, close it, spawn a fresh one with the result and a brief, and prefer a fresh agent per round to "NEW TASK round N".
Rejected: fleet recycling the agent itself (closing loses the conversation and may cut a turn short; the owner knows whether the work is at a handover point); a context-% threshold (a fresh agent can sit at 60% after reading a big file, and compaction is the event that marks the expensive regime); counting the compaction's own credits into spend (Copilot's session totals include them in some sessions and not in others, so adding them would double count).
Accepted cost: one hint per agent, so an owner who ignores it is not reminded; an agent below the AIC threshold that never compacts is never flagged, which is the cheap case.

**ADR-72: `fleet_wait` blocks inside fleet until a report or message arrives** (§4, §6.7, §6.11, fleet#30)
Problem: over 14 days of Copilot sessions (2026-10-26 to 2026-11-09), agents ran `sleep` of 30 s or more 128 times, about 22 h of wall time; squad leads slept 590–900 s at a time to poll their members, `read_bash` ran with delay 600 eleven times in a row, and admirals called `fleet_reports` three times in a row in 6 places. Every poll is a full-context model turn, and a long sleep hears of a report minutes late.
Decision: one tool, `fleet_wait(agents?, timeout_s?)`, on the admiral, leader and agent MCP servers, and `fleet wait [--agents a,b] [--timeout D] [--as NAME] [--admiral NAME]` for a shell. It watches the caller's own mailbox (the admiral's box; a leader's or agent's own), remembers which message ids it held when the call began, stats `inbox.jsonl` every second and re-reads it only when size or mtime changed, and returns as soon as a message with a new id (from one of `agents`, if given; a name not on the board is refused) is there: one headline per message and the tool that reads the full text. On timeout it says "timeout after Ns, nothing new"; the CLI exits 2. It marks nothing read: reading stays with `fleet_reports`, `fleet_squad_reports` and `fleet_inbox`, so a wait never hides a report from the unread count, the badge or the wake queue. The instructions tell admirals and leaders to wait with it, never with `sleep` or repeated `fleet_reports`.
Cap: default 300 s, max 600 s. Measured on Copilot CLI 1.0.108 with a probe MCP server: a 100 s call returned, a 610 s call was cancelled at exactly 180 s (`notifications/cancelled`, "request timeout"), and a 250 s call that sent `notifications/progress` every 30 s returned. So while it waits `fleet_wait` sends a progress notification every 30 s whenever the request carried a `progressToken` (Copilot sends one on every call), and a client that sent none gets at most 170 s.
Rejected: (a) returning at once on reports already unread (an admiral that waits without reading would spin; the unread ones are in every result's suffix anyway); (b) `until=idle` from the issue (status changes are not in the mailbox and a report is what the waiter acts on; an idle agent without a report is already flagged and announced, ADR-68); (c) fsnotify (not stdlib, and a one-second stat of one file costs nothing); (d) reading stdin concurrently so a wait can be cancelled (the stdio loop is sequential and hot reload hands its buffered input to the next process, §6.9; a wait is bounded instead).
Accepted cost: while `fleet_wait` blocks, that MCP server answers nothing else, so another tool call made in parallel queues behind it (the description says to call it on its own); a client that gives up on the call leaves the server busy until the wait ends.

**ADR-73: releases are cut by a GitHub Actions workflow from main** (§5d, §14 ADR-24, ADR-69)
Decision: (a) `.github/workflows/release.yml`, run by hand (`gh workflow run release.yml -f dry_run=false`), tags main's head and publishes it. It refuses any ref but `main`, a commit without a green `ci.yml` run, an existing tag, or a version that is not `vX.Y.Z`. (b) The version is the `version` input, or else `scripts/next-version.sh`: svu's rules over the conventional commits since the last `v*` tag — `feat` → minor, anything else → patch, breaking (`type!:`, `BREAKING CHANGE`) → major, but minor while on v0. (c) goreleaser (`.goreleaser.yaml`) builds linux and darwin, amd64 and arm64, `CGO_ENABLED=0`, `-trimpath`, `-X main.version=vX.Y.Z`, as bare binaries `fleet-<os>-<arch>` plus `SHA256SUMS`, the names the hand-cut releases used. (d) The notes are `scripts/release-notes.sh`: the `CHANGELOG.md` lines added since the last tag, in the changelog's words, and a compare link, passed to goreleaser as `--release-notes`. `changelog.disable` must stay unset: it skips the pipe that reads that file, which left the v0.4.0–v0.5.0 bodies empty. The workflow fails a run whose resolved config disables it, and after a real release it fills an empty body from the notes. (e) `dry_run` defaults to true: it tags only in the runner, builds a goreleaser snapshot, checks the sums, runs `fleet version` on the linux/amd64 binary and uploads the files as an artifact. A push to a `fleet/**` branch that touches the release files dry-runs too. (f) `fleet version` prints the linked tag, and `make build` links `git describe`.
Why: every release was a page of hand steps in the fleet-release skill (tag, cross-compile, sums, notes from the changelog), linux-only, and the binary could not say which release it was (fleet#21–#25). The commits are already conventional and CHANGELOG.md already holds the user's words, so the release needs no new ceremony from agents or the user: one command after main is pushed.
Trade-offs: the tag is pushed by `GITHUB_TOKEN`, so it starts no `ci.yml` run of its own; the commit's run already gated it. The bump is a short script rather than svu itself, whose current release needs a newer Go than the repo's. Darwin binaries are built and vetted (`GOOS=darwin go vet`) but not run on macOS.
Amended 2026-11-10: the notes are grouped (Changed defaults / action needed, New, Changed, Fixed, one Internal line) by the type of the commit that added each changelog line, or by a `**Changed default:**` or `Contributors:` prefix on the line; ADR numbers move to a footer linked to §14; edits of old lines are left out; and a non-built-in `[profile.X]` or a private deny match fails the release. Why: the v1.x notes mixed changes in one bullet, gave changed defaults no warning, and named personal profiles.
Amended 2026-11-10 (one publisher): the workflow also refuses a real release of a commit that already has a `v*` tag, and the publisher takes `scripts/release-lock.sh acquire <agent>` before pushing main: a lock file in the shared git dir (`fleet-release.lock`, created with an atomic `ln`) holding the agent, pid, time and host, stale after two hours. Why: three agents released v1.9.0–v1.11.0 within an hour, one pushing main while another was mid-release. The workflow's `concurrency` group serialises runs but not the pushes before them.
Rejected: GitVersion (.NET tool, branch-model config for a trunk-only repo); release-please (a release PR merged on GitHub moves main outside the publisher's fast-forward push, and it wants to write CHANGELOG.md, which is hand-written and dated); tag-push-triggered goreleaser alone (the user still picks and pushes a tag by hand, the step that could go wrong); svu as a pinned binary (one more download for ~20 lines of rules).

**ADR-75: the status-line badge has one writer** (§6.7, §14 ADR-36, ADR-49, ADR-51, ADR-62)
Decision: while a watcher is live, only the watcher sets `@fleet_reports` (and `@fleet_reports_by`, `@fleet_multi`). The overview leaves the admiral and leader wakes to the watcher on those polls too (amended 2026-11-10; it used to retry them, and two wakers on one box logged the same stall twice and could race). With no watcher the overview owns the poll and sets the badge as before. The badge stays steady: one style, `bold` on the waiting colour, no blink attribute.
Why: on 2026-11-10 the badge blinked on and off every second. Nothing in its style blinks: two processes set it in turn. The watcher re-execs into a rebuilt binary (ADR-62); the overview does not, and had run a binary from before per-sender reads (`read.ids`, `fleet_reports agent=`). It counted reports the admiral had read one sender at a time as unread, the watcher did not, and each poll overwrote the other. Any two writers on different builds can disagree like that, so the fix is one writer, and it is the one that follows rebuilds.
Trade-offs: an overview started before this change keeps writing the badge until it is restarted (`respawn-pane` of the overview pane). An overview on a stale build still types wakes with its old code; that was already so.
Rejected: comparing before writing (both already do; they disagree on the value, not the timing); making the overview re-exec on a rebuild (a TUI holds the terminal and its state, a larger change, still left two writers).
Note (2026-11-10, ADR-81): the badge blinked again, from a third writer this note did not foresee: leaked test watchers whose `TMUX_TMPDIR` was deleted, so tmux fell back to the real server. The badge is now a plain `●` in the waiting colour, no `bold` and no background block, and the tmux client refuses a gone `TMUX_TMPDIR`.
**ADR-74: ACP agents, hosted by fleet in the agent's window; opt-in in v0, the default for Copilot and agy in v1.0.0** (§7d, §14 ADR-49, ADR-68, ADR-73; scale plan §7 phase 1, §8.1) — accepted 2026-11-10, (f) and (g) included
Context: ADR-68 rejected ACP as the status source for pane agents, because whoever starts the tool owns its stdio, and named a headless ACP agent kind as the follow-up. Two blockers are gone (checked 2026-11-10 against the installed CLIs, `initialize` probes): Copilot 1.0.105+ sends `session/request_permission` again (github/copilot-cli#4537) and offers opt-in `assistant.turn_start`/`assistant.idle`/`session.idle` events; its `initialize` answers `loadSession: true` and `sessionCapabilities {close, list}`. agy 1.5.4 serves `agy acp` with `sessionCapabilities {resume, load, close, list}`, so a wake needs no history replay.
Decision: (a) A driver is per agent: `tmux` (today's path, unchanged) or `acp`. (b) An ACP agent's process tree is `tmux window → fleet acp-host <name> → <tool's ACP command>`. The host is the ACP client; it writes the ADR-49 state file, appends the transcript to `~/.fleet/acp/<name>.jsonl`, serves `~/.fleet/acp/<name>.sock` for send, answer and cancel, and draws the transcript in its pane, taking typed prompts and numbered answers. (c) For an `acp` agent the refresher takes status from the state file alone and never captures the pane. (d) Park/wake use `session/resume` where offered, else `session/load`; the session id is the board's `session_id`. (e) `internal/acp` is stdlib only (JSON-RPC 2.0 over newline-delimited stdio); the fake agent gains an ACP mode and is the only agent tests run.
(f) Versioning. fleet is at v0.4.0. Under semver 2.0 any 0.x release may break, and ADR-73's bump script maps a breaking commit to a minor while on v0, so "major" here means v1.0.0, chosen by passing `version=v1.0.0` to the release workflow. Shipping `--driver acp` as opt-in, with unchanged defaults, config, status and output, is additive: it ships as a minor (v0.5.0). What would break users or scripts is the default flip for `copilot` and `agy`: the pane shows fleet's transcript, not the tool's TUI; `fleet snap`, the copilot/agy screen patterns and `ModeKeys` no longer apply to those agents; screen-typed paths (pasting into the pane) go through the socket instead; `fleet_status` waiting text comes from ACP options rather than the screen; an existing parked tmux agent wakes on tmux. So: build behind the flag, merge with the default off, and flip the default for `copilot` and `agy` only in one deliberate v1.0.0 with a migration note (`driver = "tmux"` under `[defaults]` or `[profile.<name>]` restores today's behaviour). Nothing breaking ships in a v0.x minor.
(g) Recommendation on §8.1, for the user: (1) ACP primary for the tools whose ACP sends permission requests (Copilot ≥ 1.0.105, agy ≥ 1.5), tmux kept as the per-profile fallback and for Claude. Under "status only" there is nothing to build for today's pane agents (ADR-68 (a) still holds), so (1) is the only way the exact status lands at all. (2) An ACP agent keeps an attachable session: the fleet-drawn transcript in its own tmux window, where the user can read, type prompts and answer. Handing the session to the tool's native TUI (`--resume <id>` in the pane, after closing the host) is a later option, not part of v1.0.0.
(h) Accepted by the user as written in (f) and (g), and shipped in v1.0.0 so: the default is data, a `driver` key on the tool profile (`acp` on the built-in `copilot` and `agy`), below config's `[profile.<name>]` and `[defaults]` and the spawn flag, so `driver = "tmux"` in config restores v0 for every profile or one. The board's empty driver means tmux, and wake and the queue read the board rather than resolving again, so every agent that exists at the upgrade (running, parked or queued) keeps tmux until it is closed. agy's spend and subagent dots come from the host's transcript (per-turn `usage` from the prompt response, `invoke_subagent` tool calls), since agy keeps no log fleet reads; a host that dies before its session is ready keeps its unsent prompts in the prompt file for the next one.
(i) Amended 2026-11-10: the default follows the tool, not the profile name. (h) put `driver = "acp"` on the built-in `copilot` and `agy` profiles only, so `claude-copilot`, which runs the same copilot binary and is the profile nearly every admiral-spawned agent uses, stayed on tmux; the intent in (f) and (g) is "Copilot and agy", the tools whose ACP sends permission requests. So a profile with no `driver` key of its own defaults to `acp` when its `command` (base name) is `copilot` or `agy` and it can run under acp; such a profile that sets no `acp` keys takes the built-in's, as `acp_from` would (`Profile.DefaultDriver`, `internal/tools`). An explicit `driver` (tools.toml, config's `[profile.<name>]` or `[defaults]`, the spawn flag) still wins, adapter profiles (ADR-76) stay opt-in, and agents on the board keep the driver they started with. Rejected: `driver = "acp"` under `[profile.claude-copilot]` in the user's config only (fixes one machine, and the next copilot-based profile repeats the miss); adding the key to the built-in `claude-copilot` only (same, for user-defined profiles). Migration: `driver = "tmux"` under `[profile.claude-copilot]` keeps it on tmux. Copilot's ACP has no autopilot, so the `autopilot` mode maps to `--allow-all` under acp, as `auto` does.
Rejected: (a) the host as a goroutine of the watcher (the plan's first wording): the watcher re-execs on a new binary (§6.9) and would take every ACP agent down with it, and the wall would have nothing to show; a resident `fleetd` is phase 2. (b) Flipping the default in a v0 minor: the user wants the break to be visible in the version. (c) Claude through Zed's ACP adapter: it wraps the Agent SDK, not the CLI, and isn't installed.

**ADR-76: Claude over ACP, opt-in, through the pinned claude-agent-acp adapter** (§7d, §14 ADR-74) — accepted 2026-11-10
Context: ADR-74 (rejected (c)) kept Claude on tmux because the only ACP for it was an adapter over the Agent SDK. `@agentclientprotocol/claude-agent-acp` runs the installed claude CLI when `CLAUDE_CODE_EXECUTABLE` names it, serves `session/resume`, `session/set_mode` and per-session `mcpServers`, and its session id is the Claude session id, so the session file under `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` keeps spend and resume working. Anthropic's terms allow it with conditions (https://code.claude.com/docs/en/legal-and-compliance; checked 2026-11-10).
Decision: (a) A profile key `acp_command` replaces the tool's argv under the acp driver; the built-in `claude` gets the adapter, plus `acp_env`, `acp_modes`, `acp_mcp = "session"` and `acp_unset_env` (§7d). `acp_from` lets a per-account profile take them from `claude`. (b) Opt-in only: no Claude profile has a `driver` key and `[defaults] driver` does not apply to a profile with an `acp_command`; the user picks `--driver acp` or `[profile.<name>] driver = "acp"`. (c) Conditions, enforced or documented, never relaxed without a new ADR: `CLAUDE_CODE_EXECUTABLE` is the installed, unmodified claude binary; one `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` per account and the user logs in by hand (`claude` once in that config dir); fleet never reads, stores or forwards OAuth tokens and drops `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY`, `ANTHROPIC_AUTH_TOKEN` and `CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN` from the adapter's env, so a subscription profile never runs on an API key; no switching accounts to get around usage limits (fleet never picks the account; the profile does). Agent SDK use may count against plan limits faster than the TUI. On a Team or Enterprise account (a profile like `claude-work`), the organisation's policy applies on top. (d) The adapter version is pinned in `tools.ClaudeACPVersion` (0.103.0) because it releases almost daily with breaking changes. To bump: read the adapter's CHANGELOG since the pin for breaking entries, change the constant, run `go test ./internal/tools/ ./internal/fleet/` and `FLEET_CLAUDE_ACP_LIVE=1 go test ./internal/fleet/ -run TestClaudeACPLive` (Haiku on claude-personal, a few cents), then ship it as a fix.
Why: Claude is the remaining large tool on screen scraping. Over ACP its status, questions and answers come from the protocol like Copilot's and agy's, and nothing about spend, park or wake changes for the user.
Trade-offs: the adapter needs node 22+ and npx and is downloaded on first use; its first start is slower. Claude's own flags (extra args, `--add-dir` context) do not reach it. Cancel reports no usage for the turn; spend still comes from the session file.
Rejected: making Claude acp by default (the terms conditions, and it is new); passing tokens or API keys through fleet (terms); following the adapter's latest release (breaks without notice).

**ADR-77: each agent's driver, ACP server and fleet version are on the board and shown** (§6.9, §7d, §14 ADR-74, ADR-76) — accepted 2026-11-10
Context: since v1.0.0 an agent runs on the acp or the tmux driver, and since ADR-76 an acp agent may run the tool or a pinned adapter, but nothing showed which. The admiral could not answer "is this agent on ACP?" without guessing from the pane.
Decision: `start` (spawn, wake, adopt) records `acp_server` (`tools.Profile.ACPLabel`: the adapter package such as `claude-agent-acp@0.103.0`, else the tool and its acp args, `copilot --acp`) and `fleet_version` (the release tag `main` links in, else the build's revision) beside the existing `driver`. `fleet_info` and a new `fleet info` CLI print `driver:` and `fleet version:`; `fleet_status` and `fleet ls` get a `driver` column (`acp`/`tmux`); the tile's meta line gets `·acp` after the profile, and nothing for tmux, the long-standing case. An agent with no `driver` is tmux; an acp agent with no `acp_server` (started by v1.0.0 to v1.1.x) shows its profile's current server.
Why: the board is fleet's own state, so the answer is a fact, not a screen guess. The server label is recorded rather than derived because an adapter pin moves with a fleet upgrade while a running agent keeps the one it started with; the fleet version answers which build's behaviour an agent has.
Rejected: a tile glyph (one more symbol to learn; `acp` reads as itself); a separate status line or tile row (the tile height is fixed, ADR-43); deriving the server from the profile only (wrong after a pin bump).

**ADR-78: a one-line installer, `install.sh`, from the GitHub release** (§5d, §14 ADR-73) — accepted 2026-11-10
Decision: (a) `install.sh` at the repo root, POSIX sh and shellcheck-clean, is fetched from `raw.githubusercontent.com/sturlabragason/fleet/main/install.sh` and piped to `sh`. (b) It maps `uname` to the goreleaser targets (linux, darwin × amd64, arm64) and stops with a `go install` hint on any other. (c) The release is `FLEET_VERSION` (`vX.Y.Z`) or the latest: with curl, the tag at the end of the `/releases/latest` redirect (no API call, no rate limit); with wget, `tag_name` from the releases API, because wget and wget2 print redirects differently. (d) It downloads `fleet-<os>-<arch>` and `SHA256SUMS` into a temp dir, compares the SHA-256 (sha256sum, shasum or openssl) and refuses on a mismatch or a missing line, leaving any installed fleet as it was. (e) It installs to `FLEET_INSTALL_DIR`, default `~/.local/bin`, by copying beside the target and renaming over it, so a rerun upgrades in one step, even under a running fleet. No sudo, no rc-file edits: a directory not on `PATH` gets a printed `export PATH=…` line. (f) `--dry-run` prints the version, URL and target and changes nothing; the end prints `fleet up` and `fleet --help` as next steps. (g) The body runs from a `main` called on the last line, so a truncated download runs nothing. (h) goreleaser attaches `install.sh` to each release (`release.extra_files`); `ci.yml`'s `install` job runs shellcheck and installs the latest release with curl into a temp `HOME` and with busybox wget in alpine.
Why: the README's binary route was five hand steps (pick the asset, download it and the sums, check, chmod, move), and `go install` needs a Go toolchain. One line that checks the sums is the shortest safe path for someone trying fleet.
Trade-offs: the sums come from the same release as the binary, so they catch a broken or swapped download, not a compromised release; releases carry no signature or attestation yet, so there is nothing more to verify. The script on main can be newer than the release it installs; it only depends on the asset names, which ADR-73 fixed.
Rejected: a custom domain for the URL (one more thing to keep alive); editing the user's shell rc (surprising, and wrong for half the shells); `sudo` into `/usr/local/bin` (a user tool needs no root); adding cosign signing now (separate decision; the script verifies what releases already carry).
**ADR-79: what fleet takes from the November Claude Code newsletter** (§4, §7b, §14 ADR-29, ADR-39, ADR-46, ADR-57, ADR-71) — accepted 2026-11-10
Context: Anthropic's November Claude Code newsletter suggested a set of workflow and settings changes. Each was checked against the Claude Code changelog and docs (installed 2.1.322) before use. No model, profile or default changes: Opus stays.
Decision, adopted:
(1) Standing go-ahead. Every playbook level (pro, standard, light) tells the agent to make any call that can be undone itself, state it, continue, and list the calls under `decisions:` in its report ("Just build and use your best judgment … check in later with the calls you made"). Anything public, irreversible or external that the task does not allow (push, deploy, release, external trackers, secrets) still asks first.
(2) Throwaway extensibility build. The pro checklist's docs/ADR step offers, for a new abstraction, a throwaway build of a later feature on it in `git worktree add --detach ../<name>.spike`, noted in the ADR and removed with `git worktree remove --force` (a detached throwaway, so `--force` loses nothing of value).
(3) Review the way Claude Code's team does it (code.claude.com/docs/en/code-review and the published articles): fresh-context reviewers, scaled with the diff (two or three narrow ones in parallel for large or risky changes), findings limited to bugs that break behaviour (not style or what CI checks), each citing file:line and why it is real, verified before fixing. Kept from ours: every finding is fixed or answered; the merge gate stays CI green plus `fleet merge`.
(4) `permissions.blockWritesOutsideWorkingDirectories` (2.1.314) for Claude agents in a fleet worktree, with `additionalDirectories: [<repo>.wt]` so the spike worktree in (2) is writable (§4 worktree). Checked live: a write inside is allowed, outside refused, and Claude does not route around the refusal through Bash; an `additionalDirectories` entry that does not exist yet does not extend the fence, which is why the fence is the existing `.wt` dir and not the spike path. Not covered by Claude: shell, MCP, hooks, so the report, the result file in the worktree and a `cp` to `~/Downloads` are unaffected. On by default, `fence_writes = false` to opt out. Not applied to adopt (the session was not started by fleet), to agents without a worktree (their dir is the user's checkout, already the working dir) or to Claude over the ACP adapter (ADR-76: no hook_args, so no fleet settings JSON).
(5) Worktree disk use, like Claude's `/storage`: `fleet worktrees` lists each leftover worktree with size, merged, dirty and holding agent, and `--prune` removes only merged, clean, unheld ones without `--force`. An untracked `result.md` alone shows as `result.md` and still blocks the prune: it is the agent's handoff.
(6) Spend and compactions. Claude's `compact_boundary` marker does exist, so Claude agents now count compactions and get the ADR-71 recycle hint; `idle` compactions are skipped. Copilot's compaction call is billed in `compactionTokensUsed` on the compaction event and in no `usage_record` (none of 816 compactions in this machine's sessions shared a request id with a usage record), so fleet now adds it. This supersedes ADR-71(a)'s "Claude stays at 0" and its rejected "counting the compaction's own credits": the double count it feared was against Copilot's session-shutdown total, which fleet never reads.
(7) `ultrathink`: the admiral instructions and the squad-leader line say to unstick an agent with one message starting `ultrathink` (that turn only, higher effort) rather than a respawn at a higher effort.
Skipped: cloud environments, the desktop app and IDE extensions (fleet runs CLIs in tmux); `/auto-mode-setup` (fleet sets each profile's permission mode on the command line, so it changes nothing here); the `/storage` command itself (fleet's worktrees are its own, hence (5)); `"idleCompaction": false` (idle compaction makes the next turn cheaper; fleet just doesn't count it); switching to any newer model (explicitly out).
Rejected: removing a merged worktree on close (ADR-29, ADR-39(b) still hold: close never destroys work; pruning is an explicit command); `--force` in `--prune` (would delete uncommitted work); fencing through the user's `~/.claude/settings.json` (fleet never edits it, ADR-49).

**ADR-80: a wake survives a read-only tmux client, and user profiles inherit peer and hook args** (§6.6, §6.7) — accepted 2026-11-10. *Amends ADR-36, ADR-51.*
Decision: (a) `tmux.SendKeys` falls back to `load-buffer` + `paste-buffer -r` of the keys' bytes (literal `-l` text, Enter as CR, BSpace as DEL, Tab, Escape) when tmux answers "client is read-only"; other keys keep the error. (b) When the Enter guard's Enter fails, fleet backspaces the line it typed before queueing the retry. (c) A profile whose command's base name is a built-in profile (`claude`, `copilot`) and which leaves `peer_args` or `hook_args` unset takes the built-in's; `[]` keeps them off.
Why: the second admiral `personal` (claude-personal, tmux driver) got a `done` report in its inbox, but its wake was typed and never submitted: tmux 3.8's `send-keys` refuses when the client it resolves for the command, the most recently active one attached to the pane's session, is read-only (an `attach -r` viewer), while `paste-buffer` has no such check. The guard then returned without removing its line, so every later poll found a non-empty box and waited until the user pressed Enter by hand. Nothing in the Claude or ACP path was at fault: the agent's `fleet_report` succeeded and was routed to `admiral.personal`. (c) closes the gap ADR-79 found: user Claude profiles copied from the example before `hook_args` existed had no status hooks and, without `peer_args`, would have no report tool; inheriting by command keeps profile data the place to opt out without asking every user to edit `tools.toml`.

**ADR-81: nothing in fleet's UI blinks or animates** (§5 Tile states, §6.7) — accepted 2026-11-10. *Amends ADR-75.*
Decision: (a) every status has one static glyph in a steady colour, like Copilot CLI's `○ Working`: `○` working, `●` waiting, `✖` error, `⧗` stuck, `○` idle (dim). No spinner, no waiting colour pulse, no subagent dot wave; the overview's tick only expires header messages, so an unchanged board gives a byte-identical frame and bubbletea writes nothing. (b) The unread-report badge is `● N reports` (or `● main 1 · print 2`) in the waiting colour with no background, in the status line, on the admiral border and in the overview header; `✉` is gone. (c) `tmux.Client` returns `ErrTmpdirGone` instead of running tmux when `TMUX_TMPDIR` is set but missing.
Why: after v1.5.1 the user still saw blinking at the admiral's bottom lines and asked for no blinking reports at all. Measured on 2026-11-10: `@fleet_reports` was unset most of the time and set for a moment every 0.5–2 s. A /proc scan caught `tmux -L fleet set-option -gu @fleet_reports` from four `repo-front` test `fleet watch` processes (fake admiral, temp `FLEET_HOME`). Their `TMUX_TMPDIR` had been removed, and tmux falls back to `/tmp/tmux-UID` when that dir does not resolve, so `-L fleet` reached the user's server. Each poll they cleared the badge and the real watcher set it back. Killing them made the badge steady (24/24 samples). The spinner and the pulse were the remaining motion; without them the overview pane hash is unchanged over seconds while agents work.
Trade-offs: a working tile no longer shows liveness by motion; its last line and age still change when the agent does. Idle and working share `○` and differ by colour; graphite tells them apart by grey level only. A leaked watcher with a gone `TMUX_TMPDIR` now fails its session check and exits instead of running on.
Rejected: a slower spinner (v1.5.1, still motion); a blink option (the user asked for no blinking as the default, not a setting); keeping the yellow badge block (a block that appears and vanishes reads as a blink even when it changes only on a new report).

**ADR-82: the user types into ACP agents in their pane, and takes one over in the tool's screen** (§7d) — accepted 2026-11-10. *Amends ADR-74.*
Decision: (a) When its stdin is a terminal, `fleet acp-host` puts it in raw mode and runs a one-line editor: `› ` plus the typed text stays on the pane's last line and is redrawn under every output write, clipped from the left to the pane width. Enter sends the line as a prompt; a digit with a question open answers it; Esc alone (no byte within 50 ms), or Ctrl-C on an empty line, cancels the turn; Ctrl-C with text clears the line; Ctrl-U and Ctrl-W edit; bracketed paste keeps newlines in one prompt. Ctrl-C never stops the host. (b) Prompts, answers and cancels from the pane are transcript entries with `by: "pane"`; refresh sets the agent's `user_at` from the newest one, so `SendBlocker` and the interactive hold treat it as the user's exactly like a tmux pane the user typed in. (c) `fleet takeover <name>` parks an idle ACP agent, sets its driver to tmux and wakes the same session with the profile's `resume` args in the tool's own TUI, back in the same wall slot; `--acp` goes the other way. A working or waiting agent is refused (Park's rule), a failed wake restores the old driver and wakes it there, and the agent is marked the user's. Ctrl-T in the pane runs it, detached under `setsid`, because the park kills the pane's process tree; its output goes to `~/.fleet/acp/<name>.takeover.log` and is shown in the pane if the host is still there.
Why: the user asked how to type into or interact with ACP sessions. The host already read cooked lines, but Esc arrived as `^[` in the next prompt, Ctrl-C killed the agent, output overwrote a half-typed line, and the only way to the tool's own screen was park plus a hand-edited adopt. Recording `by` in the transcript makes the user's key presses visible to refresh without a new channel; tmux's `client_activity` already covered the pane in general.
Trade-offs: a pane on a non-terminal stdin keeps the line reader (no Esc). Takeover assumes the tool's TUI resumes a session id created over ACP (`copilot --resume <id>` reads the same session store); it was not checked against a paid session. Takeover is CLI and pane only; there is no MCP tool, since it is the user's move.
Rejected: forwarding keys to the ACP server (ACP has no keystroke channel); a second "attach" mode that opens the TUI beside the host on the same session (two writers on one session); keeping cooked mode and binding Esc via tmux (tmux keys would apply to every pane in the window, and the host could not tell a cancel from a typed `^[`).

**ADR-83: tests and scripts cannot leave tmux servers or watchers running** (§15) — accepted 2026-11-10. *Follows ADR-81.*
Decision: (a) `internal/testenv.Main` wraps `m.Run` in every package whose tests start tmux servers or processes. It sets `FLEET_TEST_OWNER=<pid>:<start time>` in the test binary's environment, which every tmux server, pane and `fleet watch` it starts inherits. After the tests it waits up to 3 s for marked processes to exit. Any that remain fail the run (exit 1, each listed on stderr) and are killed: SIGTERM, then SIGKILL, and a `fleet-test-*` socket file is removed. Before the tests it reaps marked processes whose owner pid is gone or has a different start time. (b) `scripts/screenshot.sh` stops its watcher (`fleet watch --stop`, then waits for the pid) before killing its tmux servers.
Why: after ADR-81 four `fleet-test-*` servers from other worktrees' runs were still up 21 hours later, with their panes. Their tests had registered `t.Cleanup(KillServer)`, but a `go test -timeout` panic or a killed run skips every cleanup. The leaked watchers ADR-81 found came from `fleet up` in a temp fleet: `fleet up` starts the watcher detached, outside tmux, so killing the servers did not stop it; it only exits at its next lifecycle check, after the script has gone, and runs on if the temp dir outlives the script. Fixing each test's cleanup cannot cover a run that dies; a mark the processes inherit can, and needs no change at the hundred places that make a test socket.
Trade-offs: the check is Linux-only (/proc); elsewhere `Main` only runs the tests. A test that starts a process with an explicit `cmd.Env` that drops the mark is not tracked. A run killed mid-way leaves its processes until the next test run of any marked package.
Rejected: encoding the test pid in every socket name (about a hundred call sites, and it misses watchers and other children); a reaper keyed on socket name only (it cannot tell a live parallel run's server from a leaked one).

**ADR-84: stacked admirals keep the heights the user gives them** (§6.12) — accepted 2026-11-10.
Decision: `wall.apply` passes admiral heights to `internal/layout` (`Input.SupRows`, fitted by `Config.SupRowsFor`) instead of always splitting the column evenly. The heights live in the window option `@fleet_sup_rows` (`%1=76 %5=2`), kept while the admirals are hidden. `@fleet_sup_seen` holds the window height and pane set of the last apply; an apply that finds the same height and panes but other admiral heights adopts them, since only the user (a border drag, `resize-pane`) changes them without a join, break or window resize. A join, break or resize changes the key, so the stored heights win: they are reapplied, or refitted to a new column height, where a pane under `SupMinRows` keeps its rows and the others scale. A new admiral gets an even share. `fleet admiral min <name>` toggles one admiral between 2 rows and an even share.
Why: every relayout (a tile click, a show, a preset, wall or admiral hide/show) rendered the column from scratch, so a second admiral the user had shrunk snapped back to 50%.
Trade-offs: a drag that lands in the same refresh as a join or break is lost (the pane set changed, so the stored heights win); dragging again keeps it. No key binding yet; the toggle is the CLI.
Rejected: reading the live heights on every apply (a join-pane next to an admiral, or the joins of an admiral show, distort them, and the distortion would stick); a stored ratio only (a minimized pane would grow with the window).
**ADR-85: the ACP pane is a themed screen redrawn on change, not a scrolling log** (§7d) — accepted 2026-11-10. *Amends ADR-82(a).*
Decision: (a) In a terminal, `fleet acp-host` switches to the alternate screen and keeps the conversation as blocks (prompt, reply, thought, tool call, question, note). It redraws the whole pane from them on every change, inside synchronised output (mode 2026), and on SIGWINCH, so text wraps to the pane width and reflows on resize. (b) Layout: a header (` name · profile`, then the status glyph and word, on Mantle), the body anchored at the top, a rule, the `› ` input line and a key hint that drops items to fit. User prompts are a Surface0 band with `›` in the Supervisor colour, and are shown once. Replies render basic markdown: headings, bold, italic, inline code, fenced code, quotes, bullet, numbered and checkbox lists, and links as `text (url)`. Thoughts are `∴`, faint and italic, folded to two lines. A tool call is one line whose glyph changes in place by its ToolCallID (○ pending, ✓ done, ✗ failed). A question shows its numbered options, then collapses to `title → answer`. PgUp/PgDn scroll the pane's own buffer of the last 1000 blocks. (c) Colours come from fleet's theme roles (`theme.Load`, the wall's palette), so a theme override restyles the pane too. `NO_COLOR` or a terminal without colour keeps bold, faint and italic and drops colour. Not a terminal: the old plain lines. (d) No SGR blink, nothing animated, a steady bar cursor (DECSCUSR 6, reset on exit). (e) `--profile P` (passed by spawn and wake) names the profile in the header.
Why: the scrolling log of ADR-82 printed every tool call twice (pending, then completed), mixed raw thoughts into replies, showed markdown syntax, cut long lines at the pane edge, echoed a typed prompt twice and could leave escape bytes in the output. In-place updates and reflow need a model of the screen, not appended text.
Trade-offs: tmux copy-mode can no longer scroll back through the conversation (the alternate screen has no history); PgUp/PgDn and the transcript replace it. A reply still appears when its message ends, not while it streams. Markdown is a subset: no tables and no `__bold__`, so snake_case stays intact.
Rejected: Bubble Tea for the pane (the host owns raw stdin and the ACP child, and a second event loop adds nothing over a redraw on change); drawing in the normal screen with cursor moves (every redraw would land in tmux history, and a shrink pushes lines into it).

**ADR-86: the admiral can reach every fleet command, and every admiral profile gets the same rules** (§6.10, §6.12) — accepted 2026-11-10. *Amends ADR-26's claude flags.*
Decision: (a) New admiral tools that run the CLI command itself (`<exe> <cmd>`, 2-minute timeout), so tool and command can't drift: `fleet_takeover` (scope-checked; `acp=true` hands back), `fleet_touching` (exit 1 = overlap, returned as an answer), `fleet_worktrees` and `fleet_branches` (`prune` needs `confirmed=true`; worktree prune never forces past uncommitted work, git refuses it), `fleet_admiral_add`, `fleet_changes` (also `fleet version`). (b) `mcp.CLIParity` maps every command in the usage text, and every `fleet admiral` subcommand, to its tool or a reason it has none; a cmd/fleet test fails on a command without an entry or an entry naming a missing tool, and an mcp test fails on a tool without a row in the prompt's Tools table. (c) The prompt's Tools section is one table, a line per tool. (d) A claude admiral (main, or one from `fleet admiral add` such as `personal`) reads the rules from `--append-system-prompt-file`; its `fleet mcp --rules-in-prompt` sends a one-line pointer as server instructions; `--strict-mcp-config`, `ENABLE_TOOL_SEARCH=false` and `ENABLE_CLAUDEAI_MCP_SERVERS=false` give it only fleet, loaded up front. Copilot admirals keep getting the rules as MCP instructions (`--allow-all-mcp-server-instructions`), which they show whole.
Excluded, user's: `up`, `ui`, `keys`, `web` (terminals and views the user opens), `snap` (fixtures for fleet's tests), `admiral min` and `admiral act` (pane layout and keys), `peek --live`. Agent's: `merge` (runs in the agent's worktree, checklist 8; the admiral asks the agent), `report`, `mail`, `playbook`. Fleet's own: `mcp`, `watch`, `hook`, `acp-host`.
Why: the prompt's tool list had drifted (nine tools unlisted), four admiral-useful commands had no tool, and the claude admiral `personal` got the rules cut at about 4 KB ("… [truncated]", Claude Code 2.1.322 caps MCP server instructions) with every fleet tool deferred behind ToolSearch among 134, and the user's claude.ai connectors loaded beside fleet.
Trade-offs: the prompt grows by about 0.8 KB for 15 more tools. CLI-backed tools fork a process per call. A claude admiral's rules are fixed at launch: after a fleet update it needs `fleet admiral restart` to read new rules (a copilot admiral reads them only at MCP initialize as well).
Rejected: reimplementing takeover, worktree and branch walks in the MCP package (two copies to keep in step); trimming the rules under 4 KB (they would lose the confirmation and spawning rules); a per-profile prompt (one set of rules is the point).

**ADR-87: merged agent branches are deleted from origin** (§4) — accepted 2026-11-10. *Follows ADR-69, ADR-79.*
Decision: an agent deletes its pushed `fleet/<name>` after `fleet merge` (pro playbook, checklist 8). `Fleet.Close` is the safety net: when the closed agent's branch has a remote-tracking ref, it fetches that branch and deletes it on origin if `git cherry` against main (local, then `origin/main`) shows no unmerged patch, with `--force-with-lease` on the fetched sha; a failure is logged, never blocks the close. `fleet branches --prune` sweeps the rest (merged, no live or parked agent on the board, matched by `worktree.branch` or `fleet/<name>`), run by the publisher at release time. GitHub's `delete_branch_on_merge` is on for PR merges.
Why: ADR-69 has every agent push its branch for CI, and nothing deleted it: 32 branches sat on origin after two days, 13 of them never merged. `fleet merge` rebases before merging, so a merged branch's pushed copy is rarely an ancestor of main; an ancestry check alone would keep almost all of them.
Trade-offs: a close of a pushed agent costs a fetch (bounded at 20 s), in the background for auto-close. Patch equality misses a branch whose commits were squashed or edited while merging; it stays until someone deletes it by hand. The sweep runs at release time, not in the watcher, so a fleet that never releases relies on the close-time net.
Rejected: a watcher interval (it would need to know every repo agents once worked in, and makes network calls from the poll loop); deleting unmerged branches of closed agents (that is the only copy of unmerged work once the worktree is pruned).

**ADR-88: wall moves go to tmux as one command list** (§5e) — accepted 2026-11-10. *Amends ADR-67.*
Decision: `Restore`, `HideAll` and `SetPreset` (Alt-Shift-X, Alt-x, presets) plan their joins and breaks, render the final layout for the state they leave, work out the `swap-pane`s from the predicted pane order, and send the moves, the swaps and the `select-layout` to tmux as one `;`-separated command list under the apply lock. A `select-layout tiled` after each join keeps room for the next; the panes never see it. If the list fails part way, or another process holds the lock, the moves that ran are laid out by a plain `Apply`, as before.
Why: one tmux call per pane resized every pane in the window once per move. Each app redrew at a throwaway size (down to 4 rows), and tmux holds a second resize of a pane back 250 ms, so a five-pane restore left the overview and agent panes blank or torn for about 3 s while the heavy TUIs redrew twice (#41). Measured on a scratch server with a client: a restore went from 34 tmux calls (5 changing the layout) and two SIGWINCHs per pane, full repaint at ~310 ms, to 17 calls (1 changing the layout) and one SIGWINCH per pane, at its final size, with a full repaint by ~85 ms. The apps already redraw at once on SIGWINCH (the overview through Bubble Tea, acp-host per ADR-85), so no app-side change was needed.
Trade-offs: a join that fails stops the rest of the list, where the old loop skipped that pane and joined the next. The fallback `Apply` lays out whatever got joined, and `Restore` counts what is on the wall. The other wall verbs (show, hide one pane, swap) still move one pane per call: they resize only that pane and its neighbour.
Rejected: holding tmux's redraw (no such control); debouncing SIGWINCH in the apps (it would add latency, and the throwaway size would still reach them).

**ADR-90: an open question reads as waiting, and an answered one says so** (§5, §6.7) — accepted 2026-11-10. *Follows ADR-68 and its fleet#8 amendment.*
Decision: (a) An agent that is idle and whose newest report is `question`, with no turn started since (`turn_at` not after the report) and nothing sent to it since (`prompt_at`, a send queued mid-turn), is drawn as `waiting`: colour, glyph, tier, the wall border's `@fleet_status` (so the wall keeps and emphasises it), the header count and Tab. `fleet_status` and `fleet_squad_status` say `waiting (question)`. The stored status stays `idle`, so `fleet_send` is not refused as if a menu were on screen and the sweeps are unchanged. (b) A `question` or `blocked` report counts as answered once a turn starts after it: the `report` column and the tile show `(answered)`, and the admiral and squad leaders leave it out of what waits on the user. (c) The `report` column always shows the report's age (`done, 5m ago: …`). (d) `turn_at` also moves on an idle→working switch of any length when the idle began after a question or blocked report and fleet (`prompt_at`) or the user (`user_at`) typed to the agent since: that switch is the answer, and a quick answer would otherwise fall inside the 20 s turn boundary. (e) The worker playbook tells an agent whose turn ends on a question to the user to report `question`, not `progress`.
Why: the history review of 2026-11-09 found 6+ stale `question`/`progress` reports in `fleet_status` and in the admiral's "Needs you" list after the agent had moved on, one 8 h old (#14). An agent the user talks to asked "Which option…?" with a `progress` report and the board said plain idle (#10).
Rejected: matching "?" in the agent's last message (a pattern per tool that drifts like the status patterns, #7); a new stored status (every consumer of `idle` would have to learn it, ADR-68 (c)); counting a `fleet_send` alone as the answer without a turn (a send can land in a box the agent never submits).
