**ADR-91: workers don't load the user's global MCP servers** (§6.6, §7d) — accepted 2026-11-10. *Amends ADR-60 and ADR-74; the admiral has done this since §16a.*
Decision: (a) Two profile keys, as data: `user_mcp_config`, the file where the tool keeps the user's global MCP servers (`~/.copilot/mcp-config.json`, `~/.claude.json`), and `user_mcp_off`, the args that switch them off. An arg list holding `{server}` is repeated per server (Copilot: `--disable-mcp-server {server}`, Claude: `--disallowedTools=mcp__{server}`); one without it is added once. They go straight after the peer and plugin args on spawn, wake and adopt, under the tmux and the acp driver, and only when the file names a server besides `fleet`/`fleet-peer`. A user profile whose command is a built-in tool inherits both, like `peer_args` (ADR-80); `user_mcp_off = []` opts a profile out. (b) `keep_mcp = ["name", ...]` under `[defaults]` or `[profile.<name>]` keeps those servers. (c) The admiral's list (`fleet up`) reads the same file through the same code.
Why: since the ACP default (ADR-74), Copilot loads every MCP tool eagerly instead of deferring it behind tool search, so each worker call carried the 53 tools of a user-global server no worker uses. Measured on this machine with one `copilot --acp` Haiku prompt each, fleet-peer wired as a worker gets it: 77 → 24 tools, `tool_tokens` 38,452 → 11,031, first-call input 52,659 → 24,268 tokens (-54%). The substrate survey put the same overhead at about 20% of worker spend.
Trade-offs: Claude still starts a denied server's process; only its tools leave the model's context. The Claude side is not measured live (its login had expired), and Claude defers large MCP tool sets behind its own tool search, so its saving is smaller than Copilot's. Claude over the ACP adapter (`acp_command`) gets no tool args, so it is not covered, and servers in `~/.claude.json`'s per-project sections are not read. A repo's `.mcp.json` and plugin servers stay for both tools. A task card cannot opt a server in yet.
Rejected: Claude's `--strict-mcp-config`, which the review showed also drops a repo's `.mcp.json` and plugin servers, so a kept server would have to be copied into the peer config; turning Copilot's deferred tool search back on under `--acp` (no per-launch switch found); listing fleet-peer's tools in `--available-tools` (it would also hide the built-in tools ADR-60 sizes per effort level).

**ADR-92: mail that needs no action does not wake an idle agent** (§6.6) — accepted 2026-11-10. *Amends ADR-19's nudge; admiral wakes (ADR-36, ADR-51) unchanged.*
Decision: (a) `fleet_msg_send` takes `needs_action` (CLI `fleet mail send --act|--fyi`). The default is true for a message to one agent (asks, questions, handoffs) and false for a `*` broadcast. (b) Mail with `needs_action=false` is stored with `fyi: true` and is not typed into any pane: idle, done and parked agents read it on their next turn, when every fleet tool result ends "(N unread)". Working agents get it exactly as before. Access requests still nudge. (c) Nothing gets lost: `fleet_info` shows "mail: N unread (M FYI, queued for its next turn)", `fleet_status` adds "(M FYI queued)" to agents that aren't working, and `fleet_inbox` tags such mail `msg fyi`. A later message that needs action wakes the agent with the whole unread count. (d) Mail to admiral boxes still wakes them (admiral wake coalescing is PF3's).
Why: a wake of an idle agent whose prompt cache has gone cold re-writes its whole context: 20–26 AIC on Opus against about 4 when warm. The restore-lag broadcast cost one idle agent 26 AIC for a message that needed nothing; a broadcast to 13 agents can cost about 250 AIC (performance survey PF2, 2026-11-10).
Rejected: guessing intent from the text ("FYI", "no action", "?"), which drifts and misfires; queueing every message, which would leave a peer's direct question unanswered until the admiral or user happens to prompt the agent; a wake on the next idle→working edge, which a done agent never makes.

**ADR-93: admiral wakes keep the prompt cache warm** (§6.5, §6.7, §6.10) — accepted 2026-11-10. *Builds on ADR-86.*
Decision: (a) A report wake waits until the reports it would announce have settled: the newest is 30 s old, or the oldest 90 s (`WakeSettle`, `WakeMaxHold`), timed by the reports' mailbox times so every delivering process agrees; the watcher's next poll types one coalesced line. Admiral boxes only; a squad leader's wake is typed at once as before. (b) Every admiral, main included, starts in its own dir under fleet home, outside any git checkout; `FLEET_ADMIRAL_DIR` still wins. `session.json` gains `dir`; a claude resume runs in the saved dir, or `supervisor/` for a session saved before this ADR. (c) A claude admiral gets `ENABLE_PROMPT_CACHING_1H=1` (Claude Code 2.1.322: "5 minutes unless ENABLE_PROMPT_CACHING_1H=1"). (d) The handoff warning, and the red border, also fire at 80k tokens of context whatever the percent (`FLEET_ADMIRAL_HANDOFF_TOKENS`, 0 = off).
Why: in 10 main-admiral Copilot sessions (2026-11-07..10, 1362 model calls, 6564 AIC) 39% of spend was cold cache rewrites: 33% after a gap over the 5-minute TTL, 5.8% from a changed prompt, which includes git state Copilot puts in workspace_context from the admiral's cwd, `supervisor/` inside the fleet checkout every merge changes. Median context was 63k, p90 125k; on a 1M window the 70% threshold never fired. Replaying the logs: wakes within 30 s of the previous wake took 117 AIC (1.8%); a one-hour TTL at a 2× write price would have covered 53 of the 61 TTL misses (−14.5%, Claude only, Copilot's TTL is fixed); a handoff at 80k would cut spend about 38% with about 2.5 recycles a day (optimistic: it assumes a 25k fresh context and 12 AIC per recycle).
Trade-offs: a report reaches the admiral up to 30 s later (90 s under a steady trickle); the badge still shows it at once. The one-hour cache costs 2× instead of 1.25× per written token, paid back by any wake 5-60 minutes after the last. Recycles lose chat detail the handoff does not carry. Running admirals pick up (b) and (c) only after `fleet admiral restart`; a running claude admiral resumes in `supervisor/`, so its cache gain waits for its next fresh session.
Rejected: a settle window per agent mailbox (agents' nudges are pf2's ADR-92); a fixed delay on every wake (an idle admiral would wait on a single report for no gain beyond the window); a lower percent threshold (it means different sizes on 200k and 1M windows).

**ADR-95: a send can resume a closed agent with its card** (§6.8) — accepted 2026-11-10. *Follows ADR-43, ADR-64.*
Decision: `Fleet.Close` keeps the closed agent's last board entry, minus its pane, with its team and close time, under `closed` in board.json, when it had a session id. Records count for 24 h (an older one is ignored and pruned at the next close), at most 50; a spawn, queue or adopt of the same name drops one. Nothing that lists agents reads them. A send to a recorded agent is refused with when it closed and `resume=true` (`fleet send --resume`). The resume is an adopt of the recorded session under the same name, owner, profile, driver and dir that restores card, effort, write fence, squad lead (if the leader is still on the board) and team, then pastes the text once the tool is ready (an acp host sends it from its prompt file), with no mode keys. A resumed leader does not get its squad back: close detached its members. Only the owning admiral may resume. A removed worktree or dir is an error, never re-created. Over `max_working` it queues like a spawn and holds the text (fleet#33); the record stays until the drain starts it, which restores the old goal.
Why: #19. After a `done` the agent auto-closed, and the admiral's follow-up got "not running"; it had to find the session and adopt it, losing the card, or respawn.
Rejected: re-adopting from the session store alone (it has no card, team or owner); keeping closed agents on the board as a status (every list would have to filter it).

**ADR-97: a usage bar in $ today and % of the Copilot month** (§7b) — accepted 2026-11-10. *Builds on ADR-46, ADR-75.*
Decision: the status line shows per-profile spend since local midnight from a day ledger (`~/.fleet/usage.json`: each session's last-read total, and per-day per-profile growth, kept 40 days). It is written by the badge writer at most every 30 s through the spend cache, with no model or network call. The metric has two parts. (1) **$ today at list price for every profile.** It is the one unit that adds up across Copilot and Claude and answers "what is this costing". Copilot credits convert exactly ($0.01 per AI credit). Claude tokens are priced by model family. (2) **% of the monthly Copilot allowance used**, because that is the only limit fleet can know without calling anything. The default of 3,900 AI credits per seat per month is the Copilot Enterprise allowance in GitHub's docs ("Usage-based billing for organizations and enterprises", docs.github.com/en/copilot/concepts/billing-and-usage/organizations-and-enterprises/billing, read 2026-11-10; Business 1,900, Pro 1,500, Pro+ 7,000). Past 100% the overage shows as $, which the org pays unless an admin set a budget. Claude has no readable allowance, so it shows a % only against an optional `day_usd`. Raw AIC and tokens are only in `fleet spend --today`. Plan sizes and prices are config (`[usage]`) with these defaults.
Why: "AIC" meant nothing to the user; he wanted to know whether he is about to hit a limit and what a day costs. "Today" since midnight has to survive agent close, so it needs a ledger, not the live board sum.
Trade-offs: only fleet agents count, so the Copilot % is fleet's share of a seat whose allowance is pooled across the enterprise. A session fleet first meets on a later day (an adopt of an old session) counts only from then. A session priced by its last model is approximate when the model changed mid-session. Claude's 1-hour cache writes cost 2×, not the 5-minute price used. The month edge is local, not 00:00 UTC.
Rejected: GitHub's undocumented `copilot_internal` quota call or scraping each tool's footer (network or fragile, against "no calls to fill the bar"). Raw units per tool (they can't be added up and mean nothing to the user). Timestamp-aware session parsing (it changes the parser and the cache for little gain over the ledger). A second status row (the segment fits; it would take a row from the wall).

**ADR-98: one shared tmux snapshot per tick** (§7a, §7c) — accepted 2026-11-10. *Amends ADR-23 (who polls tmux) and ADR-49 (what the overview reads while the watcher is live).*
Decision: (a) While `fleet watch` runs, it is the only process that lists tmux every tick. Its refresh replaces its `list-panes -a` with one that also returns the wall's window options (`@fleet_wall`, `@fleet_target`, `@fleet_next_out`, `@fleet_layout`, the emphasis and admiral-row options, window size and layout), tab-separated with an end marker. It patches in the board status, no-report and team marks it has just computed, and writes the result atomically to `~/.fleet/tmux.json`. (b) The overview's poll uses that snapshot when the heartbeat is live, the snapshot is at most 3 s old, and it was taken after the overview's own last wall change. The poll then reads from it: the wall check (`ApplyCached`), `OnWall`, the pins, the target, the next-out mark and the admirals. Only a wall that needs a write (a layout differing from `@fleet_layout`, a pane to hide, an emphasis or option change) goes to tmux, and that write first rereads tmux live, so a stale snapshot never drives a mutation. Keys, clicks and every other wall verb still use live tmux. (c) The admiral's context fill (`SupCtx`, a pane capture) is computed at most every 5 s on that path. (d) Without a live watcher, or with a missing or stale snapshot, the overview polls tmux exactly as before. The watcher deletes the snapshot when it exits.
Why: the ui-substrate study measured `fleet ui` and `fleet watch` at about 19 tmux execs a second each, about 9.5 % CPU each, almost all of it in fork+exec of the tmux client. The overview's per-second wall check, target and admiral lookups duplicated what the watcher had just listed. Measured on a private tmux server (own `TMUX_TMPDIR` and `FLEET_HOME`), with a fake admiral and 3 fake agents on the wall, CPU ticks over 60 s: the overview went from 73 own + 201 in child tmux processes (4.6 % of a core, about 18 execs/s at about 1.8 ms each) to 45 own + 0 children (0.75 %). The tmux server went from 45 to 21 ticks (0.75 % to 0.35 %). The watcher was unchanged at about 36 + 145 (3 %), because its listing was already one call.
Trade-offs: the overview's view of the wall lags the watcher by up to one tick (≤1 s, ≤3 s at worst before it falls back). Owner chips are synced against the `main` window, the session's current window in practice, since a snapshot does not record which window is current. A pending admiral wake still captures from the overview.
Rejected: a persistent `tmux -C` control-mode client (the next step, kept separate); the overview reading `board.json` only (it has no wall or window state); a snapshot per process (doubles the work this removes).
**ADR-99: one fleet plugin per agent carries its hooks and fleet-peer** (§4, §6.6, §7c, §7d) — accepted 2026-11-10. *Extends ADR-19 and ADR-49; amends ADR-18.*
Context: hooks reached Claude only through `--settings` (tmux), so Claude over ACP and every Copilot agent had none, and Copilot got fleet-peer through `--additional-mcp-config`. The S0 spike (2026-11-10) showed one Claude-format plugin dir works in both tools: `copilot --plugin-dir` (TUI and `--acp`) runs its `hooks.json` and loads its `.mcp.json`, and claude-agent-acp 0.103.0 takes `_meta.claudeCode.options {plugins, settings}` on session new, load and resume.
Decision: (a) fleet writes `~/.fleet/plugins/<name>/` on every spawn, wake and adopt of a profile with `plugin_args`: `plugin.json` (versioned), `hooks/hooks.json` with the 11 events of §7c running `fleet hook <event> --agent <name>`, plus `--no-status` under the acp driver, and `.mcp.json` with fleet-peer when `plugin_mcp = true`. Only changed files are rewritten, each by atomic rename; close removes the dir. (b) Copilot and claude-copilot pass `--plugin-dir` on both drivers and drop `--additional-mcp-config`; a plugin that can't be written falls back to the old args. (c) Claude in tmux gets `--plugin-dir`, and `--settings` keeps the write fence alone; Claude over ACP gets both in `_meta` (`acp_meta = "claude"`), so the fence now covers it too. Claude keeps fleet-peer in `--mcp-config` or the session's `mcpServers`. (d) `fleet hook` reads Copilot's event names and camelCase payloads, ignores events from inside a subagent for status, and `SubagentStart`/`SubagentStop` keep a list in `status/subs/<name>.json` that drives the dots for a profile without `subagent_log` (P6).
Why: one artifact replaces two per-tool mechanisms and gives both tools and both drivers the same hooks, which is the ground for Stop-blocking reports (P2) and a write-fence hook (P7). The plugin is per agent because the agent name is baked into its commands: spawn env has no `FLEET_AGENT`. The subagent list sits outside the status file because the ACP host rewrites that one whole.
Trade-offs: under the acp driver the hooks feed only the dots; status still comes from the protocol. Copilot keeps `events.jsonl` for dots: the log also sees a finished subagent woken again, which no hook reports; the hook list is written but not read for it. A live Copilot smoke (Haiku, `-p`, one explore subagent) fired every event but `Notification` and `PreCompact`, sent the plugin Claude's snake_case payload, and fired `Stop` and `PermissionRequest` inside the subagent too (hence the `agent_id` rule and no status from `PermissionRequest`); Copilot's `Notification` under tmux is not checked yet. A hook adds one `fleet` exec per tool call.
Rejected: fleet-peer in Claude's plugin (Claude names plugin tools `mcp__plugin_fleet_fleet-peer__*`, which breaks the admiral's `--allowedTools` and every doc naming the tools); one shared plugin dir with `FLEET_AGENT` in the spawn env (the env is not set today and a resumed session outside fleet would report as the wrong agent); writing into `~/.claude` or `~/.copilot` settings (ADR-18: never global).

**ADR-106: park idle agents after `[memory] park_idle`** (§7) — accepted 2026-11-10. *Extends ADR-44.*
Context: an idle worker holds its tool process (about 240–380 MB) until the memory guard needs the room; with many agents the laptop sits short of RAM all day. Copilot's prompt cache lasts 300 s, so a wake after that is a cold rewrite whether or not the agent was parked (measured by substrate-cohesion).
Decision: `[memory] park_idle = "<duration>"` (default 0 = off). When memory is not low, the guard parks the longest-idle candidate (same rules as ADR-44: idle, has a session id, not pinned) once it has been idle that long, one per `check_every`, sharing the guard's stamp. It logs the park and does not report to the admiral.
Why: parking past the cache TTL costs only the 2–3 s of a wake, and frees the whole process. Reusing the guard keeps one rate limit and one candidate rule.
Trade-offs: a send to a parked agent is refused and its mail queues until `fleet wake`, so an agent idle while waiting on a peer's reply can miss the nudge; that is why it is opt-in. `guard = false` also turns idle parking off.
Rejected: a report per idle park (it wakes the admiral for nothing); a per-agent timer (one ordering rule is enough).

**ADR-108: two monitors through a grouped tmux session** (§5a) — accepted 2026-11-10.
Context: on a two-screen desk the admiral, overview and wall share one window on one screen. The wall code (`internal/wall`, `internal/layout`) lays out exactly one window, `fleet:main`, with the admiral as an optional column; `Alt+s` already lays it out without one.
Decision: (a) `fleet up --monitors 2` breaks the admiral panes out of `fleet:main` into a window `admiral` (main on top, others stacked) and sets the global option `@fleet_monitors 2`; the wall re-lays `main` as overview + wall. The terminal it runs in attaches to `admiral`, and a plain `fleet up` does the same while the option is set. (b) `fleet wall attach` attaches through a second session `fleet-wall`, grouped with `fleet` (`new-session -t`), on `main`; it has `destroy-unattached on`, so closing its terminal removes only that session. A grouped session shares every window, so both clients drive the same panes, each keeps its own current window, and with tmux's default `window-size latest` each window takes the size of the screen showing it. (c) `--monitors 2` opens the wall's terminal itself: `$FLEET_TERMINAL` (words, then the command), else `$TERMINAL`, else the first known emulator on PATH; otherwise it says to run `fleet wall attach`. (d) `fleet up --monitors 1` clears the option, kills `fleet-wall` and joins the admirals back (`ToggleSup`). (e) `list-panes -a` lists a shared window once per session, so `tmux.ListPanes("")` and the watcher's snapshot keep the first row per pane id; sessions are listed by name, and `fleet-wall` sorts after `fleet`, so rows keep the `fleet` session the rest of the code filters on.
Checked: wall keys are `-n` bindings that target `fleet:main` by name, so presets, `Alt+X` and show/hide work from either screen, and nothing at runtime selects a window, so neither client is moved. The wake (ADR-36, ADR-51) reads `list-clients` across sessions, so a key on either screen holds it back, and finds the admiral session-wide, as for `Alt+s`. A second client is a normal one; the read-only fallback of ADR-80 is unchanged and only matters for `attach -r` viewers. The snapshot of ADR-98 stays one exec per tick; without (e) it would have carried every pane twice. Verified end to end on a private tmux server with a fake admiral and three fake agents, two clients of different sizes: presets from both screens, hide/show, closing either client, reattaching, and back to one window.
Trade-offs: `Alt+s` in two-monitor mode brings the admiral back into `main`, and the first screen then shows another window until `fleet up`; `--monitors 1` is the way back. Only two screens; the board on the first screen is the admiral's own, not a second overview.
Rejected: a second overview in the admiral window (two `fleet ui` processes, double the poll cost for a board already on screen 2); splitting the wall into its own window apart from the overview (the layout code assumes one window holding both); a second client on the same session (both clients would show the same window).

**ADR-107: `fleet exec` runs heavy commands on another machine** (§4) — accepted 2026-11-10.
Context: agents' `go build`/`go test` runs are the CPU spikes on the laptop (≈16% of a core each, measured by ui-substrate), while the agent loop itself is light. Moving whole agents off the laptop needs remote logins, a peer-MCP tunnel and remote placement (result.md of fleet/remote-agents).
Decision: `fleet exec [--host H] -- <cmd>` rsyncs the worktree (no `.git`, git-ignored files filtered) to `[exec] host` under `[exec] root` (default `fleet-exec`, one copy per worktree path) and runs the command there over `ssh -T` in the same subdirectory; output streams back and the exit code is passed on. No host: it runs locally. Plain `rsync` and `ssh` from PATH; the user's ssh config supplies keys and aliases.
Why: it moves the CPU, not the agent, so mail, reports, wakes and tool logins stay as they are, and it works with any tool profile.
Trade-offs: the remote host needs the toolchain and rsync ≥ 3.2.3 (`--mkpath`); no `.git` there, so commands that read git state or VCS stamps behave as in an export; each run copies the diff (fast after the first). The copy is the agent's working tree, so secrets in untracked, unignored files go too.
Rejected: full remote agents over ssh-ACP for this step (larger, needs remote auth and tunnelled MCP; still the next step); copying `.git` (large, and the build doesn't need it).
**ADR-105: the Stop hook sends an agent back once to report** (§6.7, §7c) — accepted 2026-11-10. *Amends ADR-68; builds on ADR-99.*
Context: ADR-68 flags an agent that goes idle mid-task without a report, but only 20 s later, and the fix is a wake from its owner: one more turn, read cold. The S0 spike showed that a Stop (Claude) or agentStop (Copilot) hook answering `{"decision":"block","reason":…}` makes the tool take another turn inside the same prompt, on Claude ACP, Copilot ACP and `copilot -p`, and that Claude's second Stop carries `stop_hook_active: true`. ADR-99 put every Claude and Copilot agent's hooks in the fleet plugin.
Decision: (a) `fleet hook stop`/`agentStop` prints `{"decision":"block","reason":"Call fleet_report (done, blocked or question) before stopping."}` (one shape for both tools) and records no idle when all hold: the agent is on the board and holds a task (`prompt_at` or a task card); it is not the user's (ADR-61, `Interactive` or a key press within `UserHold`, ADR-68's test); its latest report is not a done, blocked or question since `prompt_at`; the payload has no `agent_id` (a subagent's stop); `stop_hook_active`/`stopHookActive` is not set; and fleet has not blocked it since `prompt_at`. (b) Fleet's marker is `~/.fleet/status/stop/<name>`, the time of the last block, written before the hook answers; Copilot does not say a stop follows a block, so the marker is what stops a second block, and it limits blocks to one per prompt, not per turn. A new `prompt_at` re-arms it; close drops it. (c) Any error (board missing the agent, marker unreadable or unwritable) allows the stop. (d) It runs under `--no-status` too: the ACP host owns the status file, not the decision. (e) ADR-68's flag and wake are unchanged and remain the fallback.
Why: the agent reports while its context is warm and in the same prompt, and the owner gets a report instead of a "stopped mid-task" wake.
Trade-offs: an agent that ends a turn on purpose to wait for a peer or subagent is sent back once; it reports `blocked` or `progress` (a progress report does not satisfy the check, but the second stop is let through). A `fleet_send` mid-turn sets a new `prompt_at` and so allows one more block. agy is not covered: its Stop can block, but it has no per-launch hooks (S0 Q4).
Rejected: a per-turn marker reset on `UserPromptSubmit` (Copilot fires `userPromptSubmitted` again for the block's own continuation, so it would re-arm and loop); a counter of blocks per turn (Copilot already caps runaways, and one is all this needs); blocking on a `progress` report's absence for the user's agents (they report progress, not done, and a block would land in the user's conversation).
**ADR-102: a changed-instructions hint and `fleet_instructions`** (§6.9) — accepted 2026-11-10. *Extends ADR-25; fleet#6.*
Context: hot reload swaps tool code, but clients read the MCP instructions only at `initialize`, so a long-running admiral or agent keeps the old rules after a release without knowing it.
Decision: (a) a read-only tool `fleet_instructions`, admiral and agent mode, returns the text the server would serve now for the caller's role and a short hash of it; (b) the server records the hash it served at `initialize`, carries it across the re-exec in `FLEET_MCP_SERVED` (like `FLEET_MCP_PENDING`, cleared at start so children don't inherit it), and on the first tool result whose current hash differs appends `[fleet] instructions changed since this session started; call fleet_instructions`. The hint, or a `fleet_instructions` call, makes the current hash the served one, so each change is reported once, also across later reloads.
Why: the session keeps its context and the client gets the new rules for one tool call, instead of a restart.
Trade-offs: no diff is shown, so the client rereads the whole text. A process started by a binary without this change has no served hash and gives no hint until its next `initialize`. An agent that becomes or stops being a squad leader also gets the hint, because its text gains or loses the leader part.
Rejected: a diff summary in the hint (long and easy to misread); restarting the CLI after every release (loses the session's context; it stays the manual option of §6.9).
**ADR-100: every fleet agent runs Opus; Sonnet only for scoped, checkable subagents** (§4, §6.9) — accepted 2026-11-10. *Overrides the PF4 proposal of the cost survey; builds on ADR-57 and ADR-63.*
Context: the cost survey (PF4) proposed picking the model from the effort level: a Haiku-class model for `light`, Sonnet for `standard`, the profile's model for `pro` and leaders. Before it landed, Sturla decided the other way.
Decision: (a) every fleet agent runs Opus, at every effort (`light`, `standard`, `pro`) and as a squad leader; no fleet agent runs Haiku or Sonnet. (b) A profile gets `model_args` (the tool's model flag, `["--model", "{model}"]`), `default_model` (the Opus model to pin: `claude-opus-5.5` for `copilot` and `claude-copilot`, `opus` for `claude`) and, for an ACP adapter, `acp_model_env` (`ANTHROPIC_MODEL` on `claude`). User profiles on the `copilot` or `claude` command inherit the first two, as they do `peer_args`. (c) On spawn, queue drain, wake, adopt and resume fleet resolves one model: the spawn's `model` (`fleet spawn --model`, `model` on `fleet_spawn` and `fleet_squad_spawn`), else the one the profile's `args` or `extra_args` name, else `default_model`. A model that is not Opus (its id does not contain `opus`) is refused with an error before the agent reaches the board, and so is a named model for a tool with no model flag. The resolved model replaces any `--model` in `args` and `extra_args`, is set in the adapter's model variable under ACP, is kept on the board (`model`), named in the spawn reply and reused on wake. Tools with no model flag (`agy`, `vibe`, `fake`) keep their own default; fleet cannot pin them. (d) Subagents an agent starts itself: Opus by default, never Haiku; Sonnet only for a clearly scoped subtask with a machine-checkable done condition (a mechanical edit, a search, running tests, a summary of known files). Design, review, debugging and judgment stay on Opus. This rule is in `internal/playbook/worker.md` and `internal/mcp/instructions.md`, and a test asserts it is in the injected playbook.
Why: Sturla's decision. A cheaper model on a fleet agent saves tokens on paper and costs them back in rework, wrong turns and review: a fleet agent works without a human watching each step, so its judgment is the product. Sonnet is allowed only where a check, not the model's judgment, says whether the work is right. Making it a refusal in code rather than advice means a misconfigured profile or a cost-minded admiral cannot quietly drop a tier.
Rejected: (a) PF4's model per effort (Haiku for light, Sonnet for standard): the decision above. (b) A config switch to allow other models: the rule is meant to hold on every machine. (c) Checking the model only at spawn: a wake, adopt or resume starts a new process and would escape it.
(e) The admiral runs Opus too: every admiral launch (`fleet up`, restart, profile swap) refuses an admiral profile whose `args` name a model that is not Opus, and pins `default_model` when they name none. The admiral takes no `extra_args`, so only `args` count.
Resolved 2026-11-11: the built-in `claude` profile no longer sets `CLAUDE_CODE_SUBAGENT_MODEL=sonnet` (ADR-63 amended), so Claude subagents inherit Opus and pick Sonnet per subagent under (d).

**ADR-101: the admiral follows its session after /clear, and an exited admiral pane is reported** (§6.5, §6.12, §7c) — accepted 2026-11-10. *Fixes #15; builds on ADR-93.*
Context: `session.json` was written only at start, reset and restart. `/clear` makes the tool start a new session, so `fleet admiral restart` resumed the old conversation. An admiral pane that exited (`/exit`) was reported to nobody.
Decision: (a) the resident poll checks each live admiral's session store at most every 15 s (and restart checks it under the admiral lock first): the newest session with a user prompt, started in the admiral's `dir`, written after the record's `started` and not its `previous`, becomes `session_id` (the old one goes to `previous`). A cheap dir check (Claude's project folder name, Copilot's `workspace.yaml` cwd) runs before any transcript is parsed. (b) The same poll reports a dead `@role=sup` pane once: a non-main admiral's to main's inbox as a `blocked` report with a wake line, main's to the log and an 8 s status-line message. The pane option `@fleet_exit_told` dedupes it; every respawn of the pane clears it; while a restart or swap holds the admiral lock the check waits, so a failed start that is restored is not reported.
Why: admirals run in their own dir (ADR-93), so the newest session there is theirs; the `started` bound keeps a fresh reset from falling back to its predecessor. The admiral has no hooks, so the store is the only signal.
Trade-offs: with `FLEET_ADMIRAL_DIR` set, Claude admirals share one dir and the dir no longer names the owner.
Rejected: a SessionStart hook for the admiral (admirals load no fleet plugin); a new polling loop (the watcher tick already lists the panes).
**ADR-103: release assets carry build attestations, and `install.sh` verifies them** (§5d) — accepted 2026-11-10. *Amends ADR-73 and ADR-78.*
Context: `install.sh` checked the binary against `SHA256SUMS` from the same release, which shows the download is intact but not who built it: anyone able to replace one asset could replace both (fleet#39).
Decision: (a) On a real release, `release.yml` runs `actions/attest-build-provenance` (v4.2.2, pinned by commit) over the four binaries and `SHA256SUMS` in `out/`, with `id-token: write` and `attestations: write`. Dry runs, including pushes to `fleet/**`, skip it. (b) After the SHA-256 check passes, `install.sh` runs `gh attestation verify <binary> -R sturlabragason/fleet --signer-workflow sturlabragason/fleet/.github/workflows/release.yml` when `gh` is on `PATH` and has a token (`gh auth token`), and refuses to install on a failure. (c) Without `gh`, with `gh` logged out, or with `FLEET_SKIP_ATTEST=1`, it installs as before and prints `provenance not checked` with the reason. (d) `scripts/install_test.sh` (in `ci.yml`) runs the installer against a fake release with a fake `curl` and `gh`: verified, failed (exits non-zero, nothing installed), logged out, skipped and no `gh`.
Why: attestations bind each asset's digest to the workflow run that built it, signed with the run's OIDC identity, so there is no key to keep. `gh` is the verifier most users already have. `--signer-workflow` also rejects an attestation made by any other workflow in the repo.
Trade-offs: the check is only as strong as its use: without `gh` it is the SHA-256 check alone. `gh attestation verify` needs a logged-in `gh` and network access to the GitHub API. A real attestation cannot be produced before a real release, so the release step and the installer's check against it are untested until the first release after this ADR; the dry run only shows the step is skipped. Releases made before it have no attestation and fail the check: `FLEET_SKIP_ATTEST=1` installs them.
Rejected: cosign keyless signing (the same guarantee, plus a tool most users don't have); failing when `gh` is missing (would break every install without `gh`).
**ADR-109: main closes admirals by tool; a handover is requested, never taken** (§6.12) — accepted 2026-11-10. *Fixes #18; amends ADR-64 and ADR-86.*
Context: an admiral could not close another admiral without the user, and a write on another admiral's agent ended in "ask the user to hand it over" with no way to ask the owner.
Decision: (a) `fleet_admiral_close name=X` runs `fleet admiral close X` (same refusals, same code), main only. (b) `fleet_handover_request name=<agent>` puts a `handover` notice in the owning admiral's inbox, without an access grant, like an access request; the owner approves with `fleet_handover`, or the user moves it. Nothing is auto-approved. (c) `fleet admiral ls` shows each admiral's pane. Restart stays `fleet_admiral_profile` with the same profile.
Rejected: auto-approve after a timeout; killing and re-adopting another admiral's agent.
